[trialAccount t18420509-1656]
1656. WILLIAM SNOW was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
EDWARD MARKLEW . I keep the Ship in Talbot-court, Gracechurch-street. I became acquainted with the prisoner in September last—he was
introduced to me by my son Edward, who is nineteen years of age—the prisoner said he was the proprietor of the Yarrow Steam-packet hotel, at Port Philip, that he had come from there to London to receive from 700 l. to 800 l., and when he had settled his business and received his money, he intended to take some young persons back with him to Port Philip, and to get situations for them, and as my son had been speaking to him about it, he asked roe whether I was agreeable for him to go—I said as he wished to go, and he had met with so respectable a person as I understood him to be, I was agreeable to trust him under his care—he said he would take him, get a situation for him, and be a father and a friend to him—we had three meetings—I do not know that he told me all this at the first meeting—he afterwards told me that he had agreed with the owner of a ship to take seven passengers, and the next time I saw him he said the ship was the Pathfinder —he said he had paid a deposit on each passenger, and he was to pay 125 l. for the seven—I agreed to give 27 l. for my son, 25 l. for the passage, and 2 l. for extras—it was to be paid partly in goods—the prisoner said the goods would be of more service to him at Port Philip than the money, for he could make more than double on the goods there, than he could on the money—I made him several presents' to induce him to behave well to my son—he said a great deal about the country, about its being a very prosperous place, and that my son could get a situation with-the out any trouble the moment he got there—I settled with him on Saturday, 9th of October, and the vessel left the Docks on the Monday week following—we went to take the luggage to the docks, and found the vessel was gone to Gravesend—the prisoner, my son, and myself, went by the steamer to it—he did not mention what money he had paid—I thought every thing was all right with him and the ship's owners—I remained on board the ship for some time—I said nothing to the captain—I merely saw all my son's luggage placed on board, and then came on shore—I saw several boxes on board, belonging to the prisoner—he said nothing to me about them—the Pathfinder sailed next afternoon—three weeks afterwards I went down to Plymouth, and there found my son, young Williams, and his wife, who was the prisoner's sister—I had known Williams some time—he introduced the prisoner to my ton—all their money was gone—I brought Williams's wife home, and kept her at my house for a long time, till I got her into the Union work-house, and she died a few days after—I heard no more of the prisoner until six weeks ago, when I received this letter from him, enclosed in one directed to young Williams—I know them to be his handwriting, and he acknowledged before the Lord Mayor that they were so—( read)—"Mr. J. Williams, 2, Essex-place, Haggerstone. Wednesday morning. Sir,—I sincearely trust that the step I have now taken will be viewed by you in the light I wish; I am now in London, and with the intention of abiding the whole of my previous conduct to be investigated by the parties most concerned, but mostly by you, whom I have injured, and who must have suffered so much through the cause of my leaving. Oh, that I could recall the past, and restore to you that peace of mind and comfort you enjoyed before the unfortunate circumstance occurred which caused my ruin and wretchedness to you. Believe me, John, sincerely, as there is an Almighty God in heaven to hear what I now say, that not in the least had I any intention of deceiving you or any one; bow it has all occurred, and the principal cause of my downfall, I should wish to prove
to you if you would allow me to see and speak to you. Sincerely do I entreat your pardon, and that of your mother for what I have done, but believe me I am not so culpable as you imagine. I enclose a note to Mr. Marklew, if you will be kind enough, if I may ask such a thing, to deliver; and likewise permit me to see him, if he will consent, at your house; perhaps things may be better arranged by what I propose; still if not, I will then on to-morrow morning go to the Mansion-house, and then trust to Him above. I should much wish to see you and Mr. Marklew this evening, Wednesday, at seven o'clock. Will you consent? if you will, it will add an act of great kindness to the unfortunate W. SNOW."
Letter to Mr. Marklew. —"Wednesday morning. Sir,—You will no doubt feel much surprised at having a note from me, but I write to you with the hope that however culpable I may appear, yet on hearing some circumstances from me you will find I am not guilty. I have arrived in London with the sole purpose of letting justice prove the truth; and though I would fain the matter might be settled if possible in a different way, yet should you not be enabled to listen to what I can say in palliation of what has occurred, I am determined to abide by the laws of the country. Allow me to say, Mr. Marklew, that you are indeed very much mistaken in supposing I am the villain stated in the public papers. I can prove, I trust, to your satisfaction, if you would hear me coolly and dispassionately, that no intention ever existed in my mind of injuring any one party. 1 could also prove, if matters were now settled peaceably, how much better it would be for all parties concerned. This I can do if you would consent to meet me alone at seven o'clock this evening at Mr. Williams's. I have many persons to prove the truth of what I might say to you, and all I ask of you is to hear me first; all may yet be able to go well without loss to any one; and such is my earnest wish that it may. Should you refuse to see me, I can only add, the thing must take its course, and no benefit to any one will then ensue; acceding to my request of conversing together for a short time may prove of mutual advantage. I must again state that you are very much deceived in judging me as you do. I enclose this in a similar note to Mr. Williams, begging him to be kind enough to present it to you, and all I entreat of you is, to beg you will hear me.—
W. SNOW." MR. MARKLEW continued. I found the prisoner at William's mother's, and apprehended him—he made me several offers not to prosecute—he said he had not had much more than a fourth part of the articles, and I should have them, and he would make up all the money if I would not prosecute—I have found part of the property.
Prisoner. Q. At what time did I say I was possessed of 700 l. or 800 l.? A. The first time you came to me—I mentioned it to young Williams, and he said you had brought plenty of property with you, and I thought you were a man of property—he did not tell me you had been out only eighteen months—I gave you seven sovereigns—I did not give you all the money I charge you with having received on the same day—I gave you part, and I sent part by my son—I received a receipt or agreement from you, but thinking you would never show your face in London again I have mislaid it—I did not inquire at the agents whether you had secured the passage—I put too much faith in you—I left every thing to you, and you did every thing as my agent.
Prisoner. Q. How came you to place such confidence in me, only seeing me once? A. Why, knowing your brother-in-law—my son had been
wanting to go abroad for some time, and I was certainly glad of an opportunity of his going with some one who I thought would take care of him.
Prisoner. Q. It was not at my entreaty, then? A. You came to me and asked whether I was agreeable to let my son go—I did not earnestly solicit you to take him—I have been a prosecutor several times before—I was indicted about eighteen months or two years ago for suffering cardplaying in my house, not for keeping a disorderly house, or allowing bad company to come there—I had a very respectable card party up-stairs—it was a charge made by a policeman.
COURT. Q. What did you let him have besides the 7 l.? A. Nineteen yards of broad cloth, two watches, and a time-piece—those were goods paid for part of my son's passage—I gave part of them into the prisoner's hands and part I sent to his lodgings by my son—I let him have them entirely for him to pay my son's passage to Port Phillip, upon hit promise of taking him there and getting him a situation—he stated several times that he would be a father and a friend to him—he said he had paid a deposit to the owners to secure the passage for seven persons, and my son was included in the seven—his representing that he had come over to receive 700 l. or 800 l. operated on my mind in letting him have the rest—he gave me a card stating that he was the sole proprietor of the Yarrow Steam Packet Hotel—I asked him how he and his wife could leave the business—he said he had left some one to. take care of it while he was away.
Prisoner. Q. Have you any witness that heard me say to you that I had come to receive 700 l. or 800 l.? A. There was no one present, but I could bring half a dozen persons to whom you stated the same thing.
COURT. Q. Was it partly owing to that, that you trusted him? A. It was—I thought he was going to take a good deal of property with him to do work.
JURY. Q. Did you send out your son on the faith of that representation? A. I did—he was a stranger to me but I knew his relations—his wife's relations are in very humble circumstances—some of his relations are in very good circumstances—his brother-in-law was in a situation in Throgmorton-street—his mother is a widow, and gets her living by a mangle.
EDWARD MARKLEW, JUN . I am the prosecutor's son. I was introduced to the prisoner by Williams. I was present at conversations between the prisoner and my father—I heard him say, in my father's presence, that he was the proprietor of the hotel, and he had come here to receive money—I do not remember what sum he mentioned—I heard him say he had paid a deposit for the passage of seven persons, of whom I was one—I was not present when my father paid him the money—I went on board the Pathfinder at Gravesend on the Tuesday afternoon with the prisoner and his wife, and Williams and his wife—Williams's wife is the prisoner's sister, and the prisoner's wife is Williams's sister—we sailed about three o'clock on Wednesday morning—we started from the Downs, I think, about the 20th or 22nd of October, and meeting with an unfair wind when we got to Dungeness we were obliged to return to the Downs—the prisoner and his wife left the ship on Sunday afternoon, and said he should be on board in four days—he asked the captain whether he would allow him four days—he promised to meet us at Plymouth in four days—two boxes and one large box were left on board belonging to him—the prisoner did
not meet us at Plymouth—we went on shore there to the Royal Hotel as he had directed us, and stopped there two days, but he not coming, we went on board again on the Friday, when we were informed that only 25 l. or 27 l. was paid as a deposit for our passage, and if he was not forthcoming with the rest, 70 l. odd, we could not be allowed to remain on board—they would not allow us to remain—I received a letter from the proprisoner of the Royal Hotel and went back there—Williams and his wife went to the Brunswick Inn—my father came down on the Wednesday evening—I was put to great inconvenience, and was in a state of distress, disappointment, and anxiety—when we found the prisoner was not forthcoming the captain took on himself, in our presence, to break open hit boxes to see if there was anything in them worth the passage-money—the two first boxes contained a little wearing apparel, but nothing like the quantity which ought to have been in them—the third contained old colonial papers, log-books, and some wood, bone, and iron, not worth a penny to us, and more especially if we had taken them to Australia—they were not worth anything either in Port Phillip or anywhere else—when the prisoner left us he said, "Take care of that box, for in it is all my hopes."
Prisoner. Q. Did I not help to fit up berths on board? A. Of course you did, and I helped you—the things found in your box were useful to no one—I have the bone now in my possession—the iron was an old Italian iron and heaters—there were only five persons on board instead of seven.
Prisoner. The bone was a piece of a whale's jaw, and the other things were useful to any one, especially to a female passenger—how long was it after my seeing you that I spoke to your father about your going? Witness. Four or five days—I have been intimate with Williams above twelve months, and he introduced you to me.
GEORGE LEWIS . I am owner of the Pathfinder. The prisoner made an engagement with me for the voyage of seven persons to Port Philip, at 125 l —he paid me 27 l. deposit on the 14th or 15th of October—the vessel left London about the 18th—73 l. was to be paid at Plymouth—I have never received it, and know nothing of any such payment being made—in the ordinary course I should have received it from the agent there.
Prisoner. Q. In making that agreement with you, I believe I made a great many quibbles about the amount? A. A great many, as to the amount being larger than you liked to agree for—you agreed to take seven persons in a space large enough for six—I agreed to make a difference on that account—I knew nothing of any party but you—I engaged with you for that number of persons, and unless the money was forthcoming, the captain's orders were to turn the persons out of the ship—I do not intend to refund the 27 l. of course—I did not know you before—25 l. was to be paid at Port Philip—you tried to make the most economical bargain you could.
SIR HENRY ATKINSON, BART . I came from Bahia, on the coast of Brazil, in a vessel which came from Sydney. The prisoner was on board that vessel when it arrived at Bahia—he told me he had come from Sydney, and it is most likely he did—he was a steerage passenger—his passagemoney was paid before he came on board—he had no money in his possession, but he told me he had bills to the amount of 700 l. or 800 l.—he borrowed 5 l. of me at Portsmouth, which I lent him on the faith of those bills, and he gave me an "I.O.U.," which I have now in my pocket—I have repeatedly asked him for the money, and he has always put me off
from time to time by letter—at last I waited on him one Saturday, and he promised me faithfully on his word and honour, that he would pay me on the Wednesday—I called on the Wednesday, and was told he was gone back again to P ort Philip—I never saw him again till he was at the Mansion-house—I never saw any thing of these bills—I took his word for it—I knew his father a little, a little, a and on his telling me he had this amount, I lent him the 5 l., and if he had asked me for 20 l. at the time he could have had it—his hather was a lieutenant in the navy, and had the semaphore at Putney for some time—he is dead.
Prisoner. Q. When did I state to you that I had this money? A. On the Passage home, and likewise on the morning before we went into the cutter to go ashore at Portsmouth—I did not solve it you to go on shore gone—I never asked any of your relations whether what you stated was true—I trusted to your "word and honour," as you said in your letters—the doctor of the ship heard you state that you had these bills—I have not been much with Mr. Marklew lastely for the purpose of consulting together—I called on him to know if he could tell me where you were, that I might try to fathom you out and arrest you—I have not frequently called on him since you left the Mansion-house.
JOHN WILLIAMS . I am the prisoner's brother-in-law, I embarked with him on board the Pathfinder —I was not present at the conversation between him and Mr. Marklew—I heard him say he would take his son out with him, and that he kept the Yarrow Steam packet Hotel, at Port Philip—I did not hear him say any thing about receiving 700 l. or 800 l.—I was present at the Mansion-house when he was before the lord Mayor—I will not know whether what he said then was taken down in writing—I will not be sure whether Mr. Marklew stated there in the prisoner's presence, that he (he prisoner) had said he kept the Yarrow Steam packet Hotel at port philip—I married the prisoner's sister—I think she was going on for seventeen years of ago—I never knew of his having to receive 700 l. or 800 l. in England—I have known him about fouryears, before he went to Australia, and when he came back—he married my sister.
Prisoner. It is about two years since I first went out to Australia Witness. Yes, in 1840.
MR. MARKLEW re-examined. I was present at the prisoner's examination at the Mansion-house—I do not think what he said was taken down—I stated there, in his hearing, that he had told me he came over here to receive 700 l. or 800 l., and that he kept the Yarrow Steam Packet Hotel at port philip—the prisoner replied, "it is false," and called me a false perjured villain for stating that he had ever said any thing about the 700 l. or 800 l., for he never had stated such a thing, and that I should suffer for it on my death bed—he said he never was the proprietor of the Yarrow Steam packet Hotel, and he had never stated to me that he was.
Prisoner. I stated that I was never the proprietor, but that I kept the hotel. Witness. He said he never was the proprietor, but he was always there to manage the business—I am sure he did tell me that he was the proprietor in the first instance—it was on Saturday, the 9th of october, that I gave him the goods and money and it was on that day that he told me he had paid the deposit.
— GOSLING. I am a pawnbroker. I produce a pice of cloth which was pawned by the prisoner on the 11th of october, for 1 l. 10 s.
Prisoner. Q. How do you know it was me? A. I can swear it was you—I have not the least doubt of it.
FREDERICK JONES . I am shopman to Mr. Board, a pawnbroker. I produce nine yards of cloth, which was pledged on the 15th of October for 1 l. 10 s. by a man, I cannot say who, in the name of William Snow—I wrote the ticket for it—I saw the person that pawned it, but I do not recollect who it was, it is so long ago—I have no belief on the subject.
GEORGE BECK . I live in the Blackfriars-road. I produce a time-piece, pawned on the 14th of October, in the name of William Snow, for 15 s.—I cannot say who by—the person who received it in pledge has left—it is a repeater.
MR. MARKLEW re-examined. This is a portion of the property I left with the prisoner—I left the time-piece with him on the 9th of October.
Prisoner. Q. Will you swear you gave it me on the 9th of October? A. I think I let you have one or two of the watches before the 9th, as you had none in your pocket, but this time-piece you had on the 9th, and the cloth.
COURT. Q. Are you quite sure the representations about the deposit were made by the prisoner before you gave him the goods? A. Yes, I am certain of it—I can speak to this cloth—one piece I know by the narrow width—it is exactly the same description of cloth, and the same length.
JURY. Q. Did you state that you would pay him either in money or goods? A. No, I do not think I should have paid him in money—I bad these goods by me, and he said they would be of more service to him than money.
JAMES CLARK . I am a policeman. I took several papers from the prisoner, which I produce.
( The prisoner read a long defence in which he stated that he had been engaged by the agents of the ship in which he first left England to manage the hotel at Port Philip—that his health obliging him to return to England, he with their consent transferred his licence to another party, who agreed to take the house at the rent of 700l. per annum, and he came over with the money he had obtained, intending to take back his poor relations to settle at Port Philip, where he had sufficient influence to procure them good situations. He denied ever representing to any one that he had to receive 700l. or 800l., or that Mr. Marklew gave him the money and goods stated. He declared that he had no fraudulent intention, but that being unable to obtain sufficient money to pay the remainder of the passage-money for which he had become security he was induced to leave the vessel with his wife, and proceed to Caen, in Normandy, where his step-father resided, in hopes of procuring it from him, but without success, and that his return to England was prevented by a severe attack of illness; had his intentions been not to have gone with the vessel, he should not have paid any deposit to the owners, nor have gone to any expense in fitting up the vessel for the accommodation of the passengers; that the contents of his trunks were not rubbish, but of much greater value and importance than was stated; he produced several papers, the inspection of which, he said, would corroborate the truth of his statement.)
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Twelve Months.
NEW COURT.— Wednesday, May 18 th, 1842.
Fifth Jury.—Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18420509-1657]
1657. RICHARD HITCHCOCK was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY HINTON . I am out of business at present, and live at Stock, in Essex. In the early part of 1839 I carried on business in Queen-street, Cheapside, in partnership with Mr. Taylor, and resided in the country—I became acquainted with the prisoner in May, 1839—I rendered him some service at that time—in August, 1839, the prisoner and his wife called on me at the warehouse in Queen-street—his wife said in his presence that her husband had a valuable property case for a person named Wall, at Whetstone, and from the great kindness I had rendered him in his distress, it was his determination I should receive a portion of the property when recovered—his wife said that, and he rejoined by stating that I had been a great friend to him, that, this property would amount to a very large sum, and it was his intention that I should have a share of it, but at the same time they stated their circumstances would not allow them to carry on the suit unless furnished with money for the purpose, and also for their support during the time the Chancery suit was in hand—his wife said this, and he himself stated so afterwards—shortly after, in October, 1839, he brought me a copy of a will—this is it—( looking at it)—he said he brought it for my satisfaction, to show me that there was such a Chancery suit in existence, and to satisfy me as far as was in his power—he said he could show me that there was such a property to which this Wall was entitled, and he would introduce me to Wall, and also to other members of the family—he did introduce me to Joshua Wall, I think in September, 1839, at the Anchor in Newport-street—that was previous to his bringing me the copy of the will—on the 24th of January, 1840, I gave him a cheque for 15 l., which I produce—previous to my giving him that cheque he said that he was desirous of relieving Wall's family until the business was settled—I asked him what he proposed to allow Wall and his family until the business was settled—he said he thought 15 s. a week would be sufficient—I told him to make the most he could of the money that I advanced him, but I should have, no objection to his advancing from 15 s. to 20 s. a week to Wall until the, business was settled—on the 22nd of February he came to me again at Queen-street, and stated that he was going to the Court of Chancery, and it was his intention to make a present to some of the clerks—that he found the clerks in the Court more ready to attend to the business he had in hand if he made them presents—no one was present but our two selves when he stated that—he got from me a cheque for 5 l. on that occasion—he said he was going with Mr. Taylor to the Court of Chancery—that he (Mr. Taylor) was the acting man in the business at the Court of Chancery—this cheque is dated the 22nd of February—on the 2nd of March, he came to me again, and said he was going to the Court of Chancery with Mr. Taylor, that they should have to discharge some heavy fees at the Court, and on that occasion he obtained this cheque for 20 l.—he was in my warehouse two or three times a week on the average, all the year round, and was constantly pestering me for money—I drew so much from the firm that it almost embarrassed us, and caused a dissolution of partnership between me and my partner—about the beginning of December, 1840, as near as I can remember, the prisoner went to town—before he went, he expressed his
opinion that he should find the business settled—and when he came back, he said it was just as he had thought, that he had seen the attorneys, that the Chancery business was finally concluded, that the cheques were drawn for the division of the property, and that he had nothing more to do but to bring up Wall and the members of Wall's family to receive; that he should require a suit of clothes to appear in at the Court of Chancery on that occasion and also a great-coat—in consequence of that I wrote to Pearson, my tailor, who came down to Leytonstone, and measured him for a suit of clothes and a great-coat, of the most expensive cloth—I was present when they were brought in by my tailor, and saw the prisoner in them afterwards—they were furnished on my credit—the prisoner has had three suits of him on my account altogether—I have seen him wear that particular suit frequently, and the great-coat too—it had a velvet collar, and the waistcoat was a figured velvet—I believe the suit cost 11 l. 15 s.—this letter, ( looking at one) is the prisoner's handwriting—I received it from the hands of his wife, and gave her a 5 l. note when she brought it—it is dated Jan. 22, 1841—( read)—"Mr. Hinton, 2, Forest-place, Leytonstone,—My dear Sir, I write merely to say that the business is concluded with the exception of fetching up Wall, which I intend doing to-morrow, and must beg a little of the needful from you to enable me to do so. I should have come down to you, but I am afraid I should increase my cold, which is very bad, and my boots are too thin to attempt to come through this deep snow; my wife is coming back to me to-night, and I wish to start early in the morning, and she will bring to me what you choose to send,—I am, dear Sir, your's truly, Richard Hitchcock."—at the latter part of 1839, the prisoner said that Messrs. Shave and Taylor had sent out two agents to Antigua, who had proved the identity of Wall as the heir of the property: that they had returned, and had successfully accomplished their mission—some time after he said the Lord Chancellor had issued the decree in favour of Joshua Wall.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Have you ever paid for any of these suits of clothes that you have ordered? A. No—I am indebted to the tailor for them—I paid for two prior suits—I am involved in debt—that is not the reason I have indicted the prisoner—I am involved through the prisoner—I have been drawn out of a fine business, and totally ruined—I married the daughter of his second wife, in March, 1840, about nine months after this pretended suit was entered into—she is eighteen years of age now—I do not think she is under eighteen—I cannot speak precisely to her age—I understood she was turned sixteen when I married her—I had known the prisoner nearly twelve months before I married her—I had no acquaintance with Wall until the prisoner introduced me to him—at the time I married I was in a flourishing business, in Queen-street, Cheapside—at the time of my first acquaintance with the prisoner, I slept at Clapton—I had no dwelling-house of my own—at the time I married I was lodging in Tredegar-square, Mile-end-road—I took my wife home there after our marriage—we resided there for about a month, and then removed to Herman-hill—I was in furnished lodgings in Tredegar-square—we occupied the ground-floor for part of the time, and the latter part of the time we had the first-floor.
MR. PAYNE. Q. When the prisoner told you the names of the attorneys, what else did he say? A. He said it was on condition that I made no inquiries of them, that they would not be interfered with in the business
—he safe me strict injunctions that I should not go to them or communicate with any one of them—I lost sight of the prisoner about tea days after I sent him the 5 l.—I had hand-bills circulated, offering 10 l. reward for his apprehension—he was not taken dn this charge—he was taken in Whitechapel, I think, in October last.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What was the nature of this flourishing business you were carrying on? A. A branch of the iron business—I had been in it four years, about two years and nine months before I became acquainted with the prisoner—I think I had been living in Tredegar-square about six months before I married—the prisoner was living at Tredegaersquare the whole time—I removed and lived with them in Tredegaf-square—at least they lived with me there—I engaged the apartments—I believe I and the prisoner occupied the house on the same day together—the prisoner might be in the occupation of the house a few hours before I went there, but not a day with his family—the prisoner, his wife, the daughter I afterwards married, and three children, lived there—before I went to Tredegar-square I had a furnished bed-room near Southwark-bridge, at Bank-side—I did not live there with the prisoner—I removed my clothes and trunks to Tredegar-square from Bank-side—I had only one room there—my habits were economical—before that I lived at Mrs. Wallace's, a friend of mine, at No. 6, Stamford-grove East, Upper Clapton—I cannot toll you how long I lived there—these are foolish questions, they have nothing to do with the case—the prisoner did not live with me then, nor I with him—I believe he lived in Garnault-place then—I went to Garnault-place after the first time he came to my warehouse, previous to my going to Tredegar-square—I visited them there—I never went there to live—after leaving Tredegar-square I went to Herman-hill, on-Epping-forest, and had the greater part of a furnished house—I did not agree to board with the prisoner and his family at 100 l. a year, nothing of the kind—they engaged the house at Herman-hill, but I found the money to pay the lodgings and support them.
Q. Now, on your oath, was it not during the time you were living there that some of these sums were paid? A. What I speak of the prisoner having had, was expressly for Wall's business—I found money for the housekeeping—I lived at Herman-hill from April to June, I think—I went to live with him originally in May, 1839, and we continued to live together, from one place to another, till January, 1841—the prisoner's wife had the management of the house—I cannot say exactly how much I paid them for housekeeping during that time—I kept no account of it—on my oath the prisoner had 1000 l. from me for the purpose of carrying on Wall's business—the prisoner did not pay the whole of my wedding expenses—I paid for the whole of them—Mrs. Hitchcock received the money in my counting-house last March—I cannot say the precise amount—I kept no account of it, but she, the prisoner, and all the family, had new suits of clothes—I have received money from him to the amount of a few shillings, not to the amount of several pounds—I think Wall came to my warehouse twice, and met the prisoner there, with some other members of his family—they met for the purpose of talking of this law-suit—I should certainly not have trusted to the prisoner unless I had seen Wall—what I heard from Wall and other members of his fauie mily, and from his friends, certainly impressed the belief on my mind that he was entitled to the property—unless that belief had been impressed on my mind by Wall and his friends, I should certainly not have trusted to the prisoner's representations.
MR. PAYNE. Q. But when you advanced him these particular sums of money, did not you trust to the particular statements he made at the time? A. I did, unquestionably—I gave him the money, and the clothes to go to the Court of Chancery, on the particular statements he made at the time—I drew 1,300 l. from my business between May, 1839, and August, 1840—the prisoner and his family lived with me, not I with them—I provided for them, and supported, clothed, and housed them during the whole time this supposed suit was going on.
JOHN TAYLOR . I am a solicitor, in partnership with Mr. Shave, in Fenchurch-street. I remember some time in 1839, the prisoner coming to me and bringing a person with him who he represented to be Wall—he stated that Mr. Wall was entitled to large property, that he had been advertised for that—there were proceedings in Chancery, and he brought some papers, but what they were I do not remember—by my desire he showed the advertisement to me—it referred to Messrs. Teasdale and Symet, solicitors, in Fenchurch-street—in consequence of that reference I went to Messrs., Teasdale and Symes's, leaving the prisoner and the man he called Wall in my office—I came back and told them that the suit had been disposed of by the Court of Chancery, and there was nothing for Wall, he had applied too late, or something of that sort—the prisoner did not employ us as his attorneys for Wall in the Chancery suit—I never saw the prisoner afterwards till he was in custody—I did not go with him to the Court of Chancery to pay for the costs, or any thing else—there was no pretence for saying there was any suit going on about Wall's property in our office,
Cross-examined. Q. He did show you some papers which he brought with this person? A. He did—I had an opportunity of looking at them if I pleased—I do not remember what their contents were—I can only recollect the circumstances from going to Teasdale and Symes's—I have no entry of the time, and cannot tell exactly when it was—I remember there was a newspaper with an advertisement—I considered that Wall was too late to obtain this property, from my communication with Teasdale and Symes's—I should suppose he originally possessed some claim from what little I did see, but I cannot say.
MR. PAYNE. Q. But you did not carry on the suit? A. No—I made no bargain with him that our names were not to be mentioned.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. I suppose as a respectable solicitor, you would hardly take up a suit of that kind? A. No—there are a good many that would be very glad to do it.
WILLIAM PEARSON . I am a tailor. At the latter end of the year, 1840, I made a suit of black, and a great coat for the prisoner, on the credit, and by the desire of Mr. Hinton—I took them to the prisoner—I have seen him wear a portion of them—I have supplied the prisoner with clothes on Mr. Hinton's credit to the amount of 30 l.—this suit came to 13 l.—I did not supply them on the prisoner's credit at all.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you sent in any bill to the prisoner? A. No.
WILLIAM LOWE . I live at Stock, in Essex. I know the prisoner and Wall—I have heard the prisoner say he was inquiring after property of Wall's, and that he was going to allow Wall a sovereign a week—I saw him at Ilford—he had notes and money with him—he said Wall had a large property coming to him.
JOSHUA WALL . I live at Engleston. I know the prisoner—he never allowed me 1 l. a week—if he has got the money from any one to pay it to
me, he has not paid it—I never had the value of a sovereign from him—I have not got a decree in my favour, and a large quantity of property through his means—I never could prove be did anything for me—what has been done, Mr. Clayton of Ingateson has done for me—I never went with the prisoner to Messrs. Shave and Taylor's.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you authorised the prisoner to act for you? A. The first time I went to him was at his entreaty, at Ley tonstone—he asked me to let him look at the will, and how I got on—I told him I did not know—he asked tie to let him have the will—I let him have it, and the advertisement, where he could go and find out if there was any chance for me, and what—he went up to London with the copy of the will, and the advertisement—I saw the prosecutor several times during the course of a year or two—he sent for me many times at the Anchor—I only saw him once up at the warehouse—me and my relations went; there—I was not satisfied with the prisoner's goings on, and I was determined to see who the solicitors were—I went up to him and told him, "Mr. Hitchcock, let us know who the solicitors are"—ne said "I have orders from the solicitors not to let you know who they are"—he said, "Go home and I will bring you some money"—I entered into an agreement—he has done nothing whatever—he has brought me into great difficulties—I have seen the prosecutor alone as well as in the prisoner's presence.
MR. BALLANTINE called
MARY JACQUES . The prisoner married my mother. I am sister to the prosecutor's wife—I have lived at home with my family—I remember living in Tredegar-square—Mrs. Roberts kept the house then—Mr. Hinton and the prisoner hired the house, I believe—I should think we remained there nine months—there are five of us in family—during that time the prisoner bad the receipts for the bills made out in his name—he used to pay for what was wanted—my sister is eighteen—I have seen money pass between the prisoner and the prosecutor several times—I have seen the prisoner give the prosecutor money several times—(I am sixteen years old)—that was at Ley tons tone—I never saw it in Tredegar-square—I should think I have seen it six or seven times—Ido not know to what amount—I think it was silver—the prisoner paid the bills at Leytonstone—I do not know whether he paid them out of his own money—he was doing nothing at that time—he has been married to my mother seven years—he has always supported us since his marriage—my mother has some property—the prosecutor lived with us at Leytonstone—I do not know how he lived with us—I never heard any agreement made about payment for board and lodging—I never heard him speak about it—I do not remember his ever paying anything for board and lodging—from Leytonstone we went to Mrs. Roberts's, in Tredegar-square again—the prosecutor did not live with us there—I believe he came there twice on a visit—I do not know that the prisoner has been greatly reduced.
JURY. Q. At the time he was staying at Leytonstone, and also while visiting at Tredegar-square, was the prosecutor in or out of business? A. In business—he was in the habit of going out to his business in the morning and returning in the evening.
THOMAS JACQUES . I am brother of Mary Jacques. I remember when we lived at Leytonstone—the prosecutor was living with us then—in the month of February 1840 or 1841, I was sent by the prisoner to take some money to the prosecutor—I think I took 5 l.—I gave it to my sister, and
when the prosecutor came home, I saw her give it to him—I do not recollect how long ago that was—it only happened once. GUILTY. On the 3rd, 4th, and 5th Counts, Aged 63.— Judgment Respited.
ESSEX CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420509-1658]
1658. GEORGE PORTER and WILLIAM GARWOOD were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 65lbs. weight of hay, value 3 s., the goods of Joseph Samuel Lescher.
GEORGE MAUN ( police-constable K 96). I know the prisoner Garwood—he is ostler at the Rising Sun, at Ilford—Porter was a master carter. On the 1st of April, between seven and eight o'clock at night, I saw the prisoners in company—I saw Porter carry a trass of hay from the premises of the Rising Sun, and place it in the hind part of his cart—he was driving the cart himself—he went to the horses' head and did something to the bridle, watered his horses, then followed me down the road about a quarter of a mile, and there overtook me—I asked him where he bought the hay in bis cart?—he said, "At a corn-chandler's"—I asked "What corn-chandlers?—he said, "I shall not tell you"—I said, "How can you say you bought it at a corn-chandler's when I saw you bring it from the stable of the Rising Sun"—he then said, "I know I did."
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSOK. Q. How is it you are out of your police dress? A. I am suspended, and have been fined for neglect of duty—it does not arise from the proceeding at the Rising Sun, that I am aware of—it did come from the Rising Sun, but not in the present case—I believe it was done through ill-feeling towards me at that house—last Monday fortnight I was on duty in the same road, and a carter that goes the road interrupted me in any duty, and made use of very obscene language to me—I took him into custody for being intoxicated—it was two miles to the station—he begged hard of me to let him go, as he should have to leave his master's team, and he would never do it again, and I let him go—he afterwards made a complaint against me at the station—there were two or three carts standing in front of the Rising Sun—I cannot say there might not have been four or five.
GEORGE LORD. I keep the Rising Sun at East Ham, and have a hay and straw loft in my stable—Garwood was the ostler—the key of the door hung outside the stable—I saw the truss of hay which was found in the cart on the bench, but did not examine it to see if it was like mine.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSOK. Q. How long has Garwood been in your service? A. About three years altogether—his conduct was perfectly satisfactory—I had much confidence in his honesty—I have left him in possession of my bar and goods—it is the practice daily for carters to leave the fodder on the road down, and fetch it on returning, that it should not be splashed and spoiled as the wagons go for soil—he has been in the service of Mr. Hind, a brewer in my neighbourhood, for three years before he came to me.
JOSHUA GUY . I am carman to Joseph Samuel Lescher, of East Ham. On the 1st of April I drove my master's team to town for a load of soil—I took two trusses of clover-hay, some mixed oats, beans, and clover-chaff
—I baited at the Rising Sun—my bones consumed part of the clover-hay on their way to town, and I left the rest at the Ewipg Sun—it was rather better than a truss—my master does not authprize me to leave it there, but I always do it—I never got it back again—it was gone when I returned—I left it in Garwood's care, in the doubie-dppr stable—Garwood told me to put it there—when I got there on my way back, Lord told vie I had better not stop there, for my hay was taken away, and the police had got my corn—I have seen the hay taken from Porter's cart—here is part of it—I know it, and can say it if part of the hay J left in the stable—it is the same as master had at home, which it was cut from—there was a binding on the truss, but I did not tie it myself—I had seen it tied and am able to say it is a portion of what I had left at the fitting Sun—I saw the whole truss at Ilford station, and this is a sample which I took from it.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long have you, been in the habit of going up and down the road? A. Four of five. years—I was in the habit of leaving my hay at the stable—I alway found it safe before, Cross-examined by MR. ESPINASSE. Q. How do you know it? A. There is no particular mark—it if clover-hay, trefoil, and Dutch clover mixed, and I know the growth—I know it is the hay I had in my cart.
GEORGE MALINM re-examined. This is part of the hay I took from I Porter's cart.
JOSEPH SAMUEL LESCHER . I have a farm—I saw the hay at the station, and hare no doubt of its being fart of what I had on my premises—it is red and white clover, and trefoil.
(The prisoner Porter received a good character,)
GARWOOD— NOT GUILTY .
PORTER— GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Five Months.
[trialAccount t18420509-1659]
1659. GEORGE PORTER was a gain indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 2 sacks, value 2 s., the goods of William Maxwell,
GEOBOE MALIN . The prisoner lives at Barkingside, in Essex—I went to his house and saw his wife—I went to the place where he keeps the provision for his horses, and found three sacks, two marked, "W. M., East Ham;" the other, "H. Squires, Leytonstone."
WILLIAM MAXWELL . I live at East Ham, very near the Rising Sun—I have a farm-house by the road-side, on which the prisoner's cart travels—these two potato-sacks are mine—I had no dealings with the prisoner, for him to get possession of them.
Cross-examined by MR. ESPINASSE. Had you missed any sacks? A. No; I have above 200, and never count them—I am in the habit of sending things out in sacks to London, very seldom any where else, except to my farm—I never send corn out in the sacks—the prisoner was sent to London to a salesman many times, and they send them to the consumer.
MR. ESPINASSE called
---- STRINGER. I deal in vegetables, and am in the habit of buying and selling potatoes—I get them in. sacks, and generally have other persons' sacks coming from the market, who have our sacks, and have others sent back in return—they get changed in business—the prisoner has dealt in vegetables since Michaelmas—he was my servant six years—sometimes, I send a ton of potatoes to market, and have several back not our own—I know the prisoner cannot read.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1660]
1660. LAWRENCE SWINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, 2 bags, value 2 s.; 9 yards of canvas, value 3 s.; 1 bucket, value 1 s.; 1 lantern, value 1 s.; 1 basket, value 6 d.; 2 pieces of wood, value 2 d.; and 59 yards of rope, value 1 s.; the goods of the Eastern Counties Railway Company, his masters.
WILLIAM WILLIAMS . I am in the employment of the Eastern Counties Railway, and live at Stratford, in Essex—I have charge of a factory at Marsh-gate-lane—the prisoner assisted me there some months ago—I missed some articles, got a warrant, went to the house, and found nine pieces of canvas, a bucket, a lantern, some rope, wood, and other things, the property of the Company, which had been in the factory—nobody but him and I had access to it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. It is five or six months since he first began to assist you? A. More than that—he was there last on the 11th of April—I never had any quarrel with him—when I accused him of losing any thing, he said, "Do you accuse me of theft, if you do I will report you?"—I had nobody to ask but him if I missed anything—we took the Northern Star newspaper between us, and had a few words about paying for it—he was to have paid half and I half—it was only 5 d.—he did not pay any thing—we quarrelled about a week before I took the warrant out—I have smoked my pipe with him twice—I am a teetotaller, and never drink with anybody—I have been at his house once or twice, and he has been at mine—he has a wife—I know he is a foreigner—I am positive the things belong to the Railway—I know the bucket to be marked with the initials of the railway on it—it was branded when I picked it up on the railway with the initials "E.C.R.," but that has been cut out, painted, and the initials "L.S.S." painted on it—I picked it up about five months ago—I have orders to take anything I find on the railway belonging to the Company, and take care of it—I know the bucket by the roughness on the inside, as if it had had stone or lime in it—it was nearly falling to pieces—when I missed it I said to him, "Where is the bucket?"—he said, "What bucket?"—I said, "The bucket"—he said, "Do you charge me with stealing it?"—I said, "There is nobody but you and me."
COURT. Q. Where was the brand? A. Between these two hoops—I cannot see where it has been cut out—I cannot say how deep it was—this lantern has the same initials on it as was on the bucket, "E. C. R."—I found this in the prisoner's house under the stairs—he never used this to light himself home on a dark night, to my knowledge—I will swear I never lent it him—I last saw it in the latter end of last year or the beginning of this—when I missed it I accused him of it—nobody was present—I have taken things of the Company with their knowledge—I never took anything without permission.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you recollect a pair of trowsers and capes for the shoulders being mad count of the Company's canvas? A. It is a customary thing—I did not make any—the prisoner made a pair of canvas trowsers to slip over my things in my dirty work—Mr. Braithwaite has seen them—the Company never gave me leave to take them—I never got the prisoner to make me a cot or hammock for my children to sleep in, out of the canvas—they sleep on a bedstead—I never told him I might take as much canvas as I liked—I have never been in the habit of accusing other people of stealing things to screen myself—I made a charge at Lambeth-street against one Peffard—the case was discharged—if my master had been there it would not have been—I believe the Company had to pay 30 l. damages for my
accusation against the man—whether it was false or not is yet to be proved, but my master did not appear, and I believe that was the cause—I took the roan in the act of stealing a coil of rope—I was a witness in the case—I swore against Rickards, not against Peffard—Rickards was dismissed, and Peffard brought an action because master, Mr. Braithwaite did not appear the day he was to appear at Lambeth-street—it was before the Magistrate at 11ford—I believe he was held over to bail, and is under bail at the present time—it is six or seven months ago—he was bailed in cut the Company wanted him—I was never in the Excise—I have been in the Customs, and was superseded for being a few minutes absent without leave, after being there sixteen years—here is my certificate, and what I was discharged for—( the certificate certified that he was discharged for being absent from a vessel without leave)—I have been in the navy—I was never tried by a court-martial—I have been punished for drinking, but never for anything like mutiny—here is a basket—there is no mark on it, but I am positive of it—I know it by the handle—I have handled it for months—I found that under the stairs, stowed away with the other things—I had seen it two or three months before finding it—I accused him of taking it, and asked him for it—I sever went to his house to search before—I do not know what the basket is worth—I do not suppose I could get a new one for 3 s.—I never gave it him—I never told him any of these things were my perquisites—there is some wood that was cut up for lighting the coke ovens—I found that in his house—I never told him he might have it as old wood—this canvas had been carriage-covers, and was still used—we have cut some carriage covers up for screens, and this is part of it, but we have so much I did not mist it—I found it under the stairs—here is a tin can—I know there is no mark on it—I cannot swear to it—the lantern and pail were not in use—he resided about half a mile from the railway.
SIDNEY BBAITHWAITS . I have the king's printers' copy of the Act, incorporating the Company.
Cross-examined. Q. Has Mr. Williams authority to make trowsers out of your canvas? A. Certainly not—this canvas is old and damaged, and not fit for covering goods—it is always useful at a rag-shop.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18420509-1662]
1662. ANN GREEN was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of January, 1 shirt, value 2 s., the goods of Bartholomew Maloney.
JOHANNA MALONEY . I am the wife of Bartholomew Maloney, and live in Moor-lane, East Ham. On the 1st of January I hung a shirt out to dry near my door—I went to a neighbour's, about four or five hours afterwards, to look for it, and could not find it—I saw it again last Friday three weeks—this now produced is it—I know it by a stain in the corner.
CHARLES COLLYER ( police-constable K 30.) I went to the prisoner's house on the 14th of April, in consequence of information—I asked her to produce a shirt, which she did—the prosecutrix said it was hers—the prisoner said it was her own property—I asked her how it came her property
—she said she purchased four yards and a half of calico, at a shop at Stratford, at 4 1/2 d., a yard; that she made a shift for her child out of that quantity, and put the remainder into the shirt—I have measured the shirt, and there are five yards in it—the prosecutrix said she made two shirts at one time, and I have compared this with another shirt she gave me, and they seem to me to correspond—I went to the shop at Stratford where the prisoner said it was bought—they said that was about the price of calico, but they could not say who they sold it to, as they sold such a quantity of it—the prosecutrix pointed out this mark on the shirt before I found it—it is an iron mould, and will not wash out.
Prisoners Defence. If I stole the shirt, it is not likely I should go and hang it up close by Mrs. Maloney's door; my sister washed the shirt, and knows it to be mine.
GUILTY. Aged 27.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18420509-1663]
1663. JAMES BOURNE was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, I saw, value 8 s.; and 1 square, value 3 s. 6 d.; the goods of Benjamin Wildsmith.
BENJAMIN WILDSMITH . I am a carpenter. I lost this saw on the 8th of April, from my shop, up stairs—I can swear to it, by a particular mark—this square is also mine—my name is on it—the prisoner formerly worked for me, and I always found him honest.
JAMES ARCHER . I know this saw and square to be Mr. Wildsmith's.
WILLIAM EPPS ( police-constable N 303.) I met the prisoner in the Lea Bridge-road about eight o'clock, on the 9th of April, with this saw and square—I asked what he had got—he said he had bought it—I asked him Where—he said in Sun-street, Bishopsgate, about eighteen months ago.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to Woodford to look for work; as I was coming down Snaresbrook I met a carpenter, whom I had never seen before; he had the square and saw, and asked me to buy them, which I did, for 3 s.; when I found the name on the square it confounded me; I did hot know what to do; I thought I would take it to Bow, and send it to Mr. Wildsmith; I was never in trouble before.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 78.— Strongly recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury. — Confined Ten Days.
[trialAccount t18420509-1664]
1664. WILLIAM WINDLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of April, 2 loaves of bread, value 9 d., the goods of John Macomie.
JOHN MACOMIE . I had a wheelbarrow in the street—I went to a gentleman's house—I left my bread in my barrow—when I returned I found two loaves gone.
CHARLES PENNYPATHER . I saw the barrow at the gate—I saw the prisoner go away with two half-quartern loaves—I called him to stop—he would not.
Prisoner. The gentleman came to me; my brother was in doors; he said he stole the bread; my brother said he did not steal it. Witness. I did not charge the brother—there was another one with him, who also had a loaf—I could not swear to the other.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.

[trialAccount t18420509-1666]
1666. SARAH DRAKE was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MART ANN WILLIAMS . I am laundry-maid in the service of Mr. Catley, it Leyton, Essex. The prisoner was cook—I saw her about eight o'clock in the morning of the 6th of April in the kitchen—the appeared to be very ill—I saw her next day ill in bed—I examined her clothes, and I never saw clothes in such a state, unless a woman had been recently confined; and on the 18th my suspicions were confirmed, by seeing discharges of milk on her under-linen—I was satisfied there had been a birth.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Was the linen you saw on the 18th on the prisoner's person? A. It came with her dirty clothes—they were brought to me by Clark—I am single.
MARY CHAPMAN . I am the wife of George Chapman, of Leyton. The prisoner's linen was given me to wash on the 12th of April, by Clark—it had the appearance of the linen of a woman who had been confined.
MAHALA CLARK . I received the linen from the prisoner in the kitchen—she was confined to her bed on the 6th, 7th, and 8th of April—I lent her a box on the 13th of April—I saw it afterwards tied up, and sealed with red—I cannot tell what was in It—it was heavier than when I lent it to her—it is here.
Cross-examined. Q. You were her fellow-servant? A. Yes—she had been in the service three months—I never said any thing to her about her being in the family way—I did not observe that she was—I heard no talk of it among the servants—I said nothing to her about her having a child when I saw the clothes—the linen was folded up when she gave it to me, and asked me to take it into the laundry—I did not see it at that time—there are three other female servants besides those who are here—I have heard the prisoner speak of her friends living at Nottingham.
JOHN SLADE . I assisted the prisoner to tie up a box—I sealed it, and directed it to "Mr. Tipley, Knutsford Union, Cheshire"—I brought it to London, and sent it by the Birmingham Railway—I do not know what it contained—to the best of my belief, the box now produced is it
JAMES M'CULLOCH . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody—she said, "What will become of me!"—she afterwards said that she had given birth to a child, but that it was dead born, and the reason she sent it into the country was because she did not know what to do with it.
WILLIAM HARPER . I am a constable of Cheshire. In consequence of information, I went to the house of Mr. Catley, at Leyton, by order of the Coroner of Knutsford division, and searched the prisoner's room there—I saw some paper there, which corresponds with some which I got from Tipley, with part of a shawl, and a piece of fine linen—I brought the box from Cheshire.
JOSEPH TIPLEY . I am porter at the Union Workhouse, Knutsford, Cheshire. I received this box—it contained the body of a dead child, wrapped up in this rag, part of a shawl, and paper.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420509-1667]
1667. JOHN MILLS HASELDEN was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 3 d.; 2 half-crowns, and a sixpence; the property of Richard Holmes, from his person.
RICHARD HOLMES . I am a sawyer, and live at Woolwich. On the 19th of April, between four and five o'clock in the afternoon, I was at the sign of the Duke on Horseback. The prisoner was then sitting on my right-hand side, close to me—after sitting some time I found his hand in my pocket, and caught hold of it—I missed my money, and collared him—he tried to get away—I charged him with robbing me—Inspector Browne came up outside the house, and I think the prisoner said, "I have got your money"—I can swear I had two half-crowns, a sixpence, and some halfpence, but I cannot say how many—I missed my handkerchief from my hat, which was on my right-hand side—there was nobody but us two sitting there—I had given him some bread and cheese and beer, because he asked me for charity.
Prisoner. Q. Had you and I been previously at any other public-house drinking together? A. Yes—you followed me into the Duke on Horseback—you remained in the same room as I did—I went into another room besides the room I was robbed in, the back tap-room—then we came into the parlour, the bar is opposite the parlour—we sat in one room, but we went into the tap-room—you and I were in the tap-room first—previous to that I was with you at the Ordnance Arms—we staid there perhaps three-quarters of an hour—we went in between eleven and twelve o'clock—you kept following me from one house to another—I wanted to get rid of you—I do not know that I was in any other house with you—I started in the morning with a sovereign and 4 s.
COURT. Q. Was it all spent except this money? A. It was all spent according to the prisoner's account—he said he had no money about him. and when Browne took him he said he had got 3 d. upon him.
Prisoner. I said I had got 3 d. and the remainder of the money was yours—did you change the sovereign in my presence, so that I might see you had the money? Witness. No, I changed it at the Crown and Anchor, Market-hill, about half-past six o'clock in the morning—I had a friend with me, who saw me change it—I never saw you till between eleven and twelve—I was a little intoxicated, but I could take care of myself—you were neither drunk or sober—you never said you must go, and did not want to drink more—you followed me into the room—we were about forty yards from the house when Browne came up—you did not tell the landlord in my presence that you had my money, till I got you outside the house—I charged you at the station with stealing two half-crowns and my handkerchief.
MICHAEL BROWNE . I am inspector of the R division of police. About five o'clock in the evening I saw a crowd at the corner of Beresford-street, about forty or fifty yards from the Duke on Horseback, which is on the other side of High-street, I went up and saw the prisoner and prosecutor struggling—I asked the meaning of it—the prosecutor said, "He has got my money, he has robbed me"—the prisoner was evidently trying to get away—he put his hand into his pocket and took out two half-crowns and sixpence, and said, "Here is his money, I have got it"—I took him to the station, and found this cotton handkerchief in his hat.
RICHARD HOLMES re-examined. This is my handkerchief.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to the Ordinance Arms with a person who works at the docks—he went to work, leaving me there—the prosecutor asked me to drink—I said I had no money to join his company—he afterwards asked me to go with him—we went to the Salutation, but did not go in; we went from there to the Prince of Wales, and drank together; he went away with one woman, and I with another; he being tipsy, and likely to quarrel with a stone-mason, I did not wish to remain with him; I left him for two hours, went to the Duke on Horseback, and found him there drinking and very tipsy; he called for five or six pots of beer; as I was leaving the tap-room, he followed me, saying, "If you don't like to stop there, come into the parlour;" he called for another pot; he sat by my side, and laid his head on the table; he fell down with his hat on; and was struggling as if in a fit, or in romance; I heard a rattling in his throat, and untied his handkerchief and collar; in a short time he turned over on his side, and I observed under him his tobacco-box, two half-crowns, and a sixpence, which I took up, and his handkerchief laid there; I put that into my hat; I got him up, and put his handkerchief on, and told him I had got his handkerchief and money; he said I had robbed him, words arose, and he collared me; I was angry at the charge; he said he would call a policeman, opened the parlour door, and said to the landlord, "I am robbed;" I said, "No, he is not; I have got two half-crowns and a sixpence;" I brought the bar-maid before the Magistrate to prove whether I did not offer him the money at the bar; I hope her evidence is here; I expected her here; she swore I did offer him the money; I did not at the moment recollect that I had his handkerchief; he could not know what he was doing; if I was disposed to have robbed him, I could have walked out at any time.
M. BROWNE re-examined. The servant was examined before the Magistrate, but was not bound over—I fetched her in consequence of what the prisoner stated—the Magistrate did not consider her evidence material—she said the prisoner did acknowledge having the money, but not till after the prosecutor accused him of robbing him.
RICHARD HOLMES re-examined. When I got him out of the house, he said he had my money, but had not robbed me, but not till I collared him and brought him out of the house.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1668]
1668. ROBERT BALDERY and ROBERT BALDERY, Jun., were indicted for stealing, on the 20th of April, 1 violin, value 12 s., and 1 violin bow, value 1 s., the goods of John White; to which
ROBERT BALDERY, Jun., pleaded GUILTY .
CAROLINE WHITE . I live with my father, John White, in Powis-street, Woolwich—I know this violin—the younger prisoner called at the house about three weeks ago, and asked me the price of it—I told him, and put it back in the window—he said he would bring 2 s. that evening at eight o'clock, but he did not—he had been about it the week before that—I missed it on the 22nd of April, but it might have been stolen before—there were three strings to it—I had not seen the father—I have no reason to believe he was at the shop.
EMANUEL ACKMAN . I keep a clothes-shop in Artillery-place, Woolwich. On Wednesday, the 20th of April, the prisoners both came to me—the elder prisoner said, "Will you buy a fiddle?"—I said I did not want one
—he produced one tied up in a handkerchief—I said, "What do you want for it?—he said, 3 s. 6 d.—I said it was not worth 1 s. 6 d., he had bought it in the fair—he said, "No, I bought it of Booth, the pawnbroker, and gave 5 s. 6 d. for it, to learn my boy to play it, but I do not wish to keep it, and had better change it for a pair of trowsers"—I showed him a pair at 5 s.—he said, "I will give you the fiddle for them"—I said, "Give me the difference"—he said, "I have not got the money to-day; I will call another day and pay you the difference"—he took the trowsers, but did not pay the 18 d., and next week I was desired to attend before the Magistrate—I said there was a string short—the boy said, "I have got the string, and will bring it you," but he never did.
WILLIAM TWYMAN ( police-constable R 127.) I went to Ackman's house and found the violin—the elder prisoner told me on the way to the office that his son had bought the violin of Mr. Pilkington, in High-street, for 7 s., which he had given him to pay some rent to his landlord.
CAROLINE WHITE re-examined. I saw the fiddle safe about three weeks ago, and missed it out of the shop on the 22nd—it might have been gone before that—I am quite sure this is it.
Prisoner's Defence. When I offered it for sale I thought I was justified in making the most of any article that I considered my money had bought; I gave my boy the money to pay my landlord, and he promised he would; when I came home he said he was induced to buy a violin; I took it from him, and locked it up, but he got it again; he used to interrupt the people of the house, and I offered it for a pair of trowsers for him, rather than he should annoy me with it in the house; he is a very bad boy.
ROBERT BALDERY, Sen.— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1669]
1669. ROBERT BALDERY, JUN . was again indicted for stealing, on the 19th of April, 1 pair of boots, value 6 s. the goods of John Higgs.
JOHN HIGGS . I am a shoe-maker, and live at Woolwich. On the evening of the 19th of April I heard the door open, went and found the prisoner in my shop—he asked if Mr. Porter, the tailor, lived there—and a very few minutes after he was gone I missed the boots.
WILLIAM LEECH . I am shopman to Mr. Booth, a pawnbroker, at Woolwich. On the 19th of April the prisoner offered these boots in pawn—he said he brought them from his father—I lent him 3 s. on them.
ALFRED JOHN FINN ( police-constable R 81.) I took the prisoner at his father's house—I told him it was for stealing boots from this shop—he said there was another boy with him, his brother, and that he pawned them at Booth's.
Prisoner. The other boy stole them and gave them to me to pawn; he waited at the door; he used to go to the same school as me. GUILTY . Aged 14.— Transported for Seven Years.—Convict Ship.
