1672. MARY SIMMONS was indicted for stealing on the 6th of April, 2 sheets, value 10 s.; the goods of Richard Carpenter.
ANN MART RUDGE . I am the niece of Richard Carpenter, a licensed victualler, of Church-street, Greenwich. On the 6th of April the prisoner came to the tap-room of the house—I heard her go up-stairs—I had made the bed where the sheets were about one o'clock, and at eight I went up to show a person to bed and the sheets were gone.
Prisoner. A girl gave, them to me and told me to pawn them, which I did.
JOHN MORRIS . I am apprentice to a pawnbroker in Greenwich. I produce a sheet pawned by the prisoner on the 6th of April—she said she pawned it for a person named Smith, living in Bridge-street, Greenwich—I found the name of M. R. Carpenter upon it.
Prisoner. The female who was with me told me to pawn it in that name; I was very much in liquor.
JOSEPH BUCKM ASTER ( police-constable 42 R.) I apprehended a person named Daley who was in company with the prisoner—I apprehended the prisoner on the 7th of April—she was sober.
RICHARD CARPENTER . I keep the public-house. I know this sheet to be mine—the "M" is my wife's initial—the prisoner was in my house on the evening of the 6th of April, and was heard to go up stairs—the sheets were safe on the bed at six o'clock, and were missed at eight—the prisoner had been drinking in the tap-room that day and the day before, and so had Daley.
Prisoner. Daley was up stairs with the soldiers there; I was in liquor and hardly knew what I did. Witness. I had not seen the prisoner above four times before—there is a beck entrance to my house, so that they might go up stairs without being seen.
GUILTY . Aged 33.— Confined Three Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18420509-1673]
1673. JOHN BOYLE was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of April, 1 tea-caddy, value 2 s.; 2 spoons, value 2 s.; and 1 pair of sugar-tongs, value 1 s.; the goods of Andrew Haynes; and that he had been previously convicted of felony.
ELIZABETH HAYNES . I am the wife of Andrew Haynes, and live at No. 3, Church-street, Deptford. My husband is a tract distributor to the Christian Friend Society—I lost a tea-caddy from the front room of my house, between eleven and twelve o'clock on Saturday, the 16th of April, with a silver spoon, a caddy-spoon, and plated sugar-tongs—I keep a school myself, and leave the street-door ajar—this is my caddy, there is tea and sugar in it.
JOHN GITTINS . I am an out-pensioner of Greenwich Hospital. About eleven o'clock on the 16th of April the prisoner, in company with two more, came towards me—one of them asked me if I would have some tea, and gave me some in my hand—I thanked him—it was loose in his hand—he said, "Down that gate there is a tea-caddy, go and take it"—I stood and looked at it—they said "That is it, it is all right"—I went down the court, put the tea into it, and called to Mrs. Stacey, "Have you lost anything, this is stolen?"—they left me—I left the caddy with Mrs. Stacey, while I went after them—I met the prisoner and two more in the same spot where they gave me the tea—I said, "Did that caddy belong to you?"—"Yes," said they—I said, "Come with me"—they came with me down the alley, and one of them told Mrs. Stacey to give it me, for it was their property—the prisoner had told me that it was his property—Stacey gave
it me—I put it into my basket, followed them and got a policeman—we found the prisoner at the corner of the street—on seeing me with the policeman he made a signal to the others and ran away—the policeman caught him—they came up to me by Trinity alms-houses, where the prosecutrix lives.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You found them on the same spot, did you? A. I left the caddy with Mrs. Stacey at first, and then went away, and found them again within a few yards of where I had seen them before—one of them said it was his, then another said it was his—there was nothing but the caddy.
JOHN EWINS . I am a policeman. I went with Gittins to New-street, Deptford, between eleven and twelve o'clock, and caught sight of the prisoner at the corner of Flagon-row—he held his hands up to the others to go on—they all ran down New-street—I followed, and caught him in the middle of the street—he said, "I am not the party you are looking after"—I found nothing on him.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1674]
1674. WILLIAM WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of April, 3 quarts of beer, value 1 s., the goods of Thomas Hayes.
EDWARD EVANS . I am pot-boy to Thomas Hayes, who keeps a beershop, at Sydenham, Kent. On the afternoon of the 20th of April, I was in a cow-shed at the back of master's house, and saw the prisoner go to a cellar and bring a bucket and bottle, take it to the pig-sty, and conceal it, then return to the brewhouse, take a pint pot, and return to the cellar again—there was about three pots of beer in the bucket altogether—he brought some beer out in a pint pot, and offered it to a man named Darby, who said, "I shan't have that," and he chucked it away against the coal-shed—I told master, and the prisoner was given into custody—I saw the bucket and the beer in the sty.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you see it yourself? A. Yes—neither master nor mistress are here—it was porter—only master and mistress had access to the cellar—not the servants, unless they had leave—the prisoner is a gravel-digger—he was not in master's employment—there was nobody but Darby present—he is a navigator.
COURT. Q. How did he get into the cellar? A. He must have gone round the back of the house, then closed the middle door, while mistress was serving in the shop, and could get round to the back premises.
WILLIAM GLADWIN . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner, and asked him if his name was William Wood—he said, "Yes "—I said, I apprehended him for stealing beer from Mr. Hayes—he said, "Arn't you going to take more of them from work?"—I said no, there was no more—he then said, "There was more concerned in it than me."
Prisoner's Defence. I did not go into the cellar at all; I was drinking rum at the time it happened; Mrs. Hayes lent us the money to get the rum; I did not go into the cellar except when she sent me to draw beer, because she would not awake the baby; he swore at first he saw Darby go into the cellar; I was not given in charge till next day; they gave other men in charge that night, but he then said it was not me at all; there was only five pints, not three quarts; the boy could not see me go, because the pigsty is not level with the cow-house.
EDWARD EVANS re-examined. I am certain I saw him take it into the pig-sty in a bucket and bottle; the door was placed open; master was not
at home, and I did not like to mention it till be came home; the prisoner was then gone; he was taken next morning; the prisoner is talking about a different case altogether.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Weeks.
Before Mr. Justice Coltman.
[trialAccount t18420509-1675]
1675. DENNIS SULLIVAN was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of George James Fletcher, about the hour of three in the night of the 25th of March, at St. Nicholas, Deptford, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 coat, value 20 s.; I pencil-case, value 3 s.; 1 knife, value 1 s.; 1440 pence, and 480 halfpence, his property.
GEORGE JAMES FLETCHER . On Good Friday night, the 25th of March, I went to bed at half-past eleven o'clock—the house was perfectly fastened before I went to bed—I was very particular in seeing every thing fast, because it was a very stormy night—I got up about a quarter after six in the morning, and found the window of the back-room on the basement story broken open—we use that room as a counting-house—the window does not open—it is a fixture—part of the sash was cut out, and two squares of glass taken out—I found about 8 l. worth of copper money missing, consisting of 5 s. packages, and about 4 s. worth of halfpence in a bag—the whole amounted to better than 8 l.—my house is in the parish of St. Nicholas, Deptford.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where does the window look into? A. Into a gateway—there is no shutter to the window—the place is built of wood, and plastered inside—a person getting through the window would come into the counting-house—it is under the basement of the house—I lost my great coat, spectacles, and pencil-case, from the same place.
COURT. Q. Does the counting-house communicate with the dwelling-house? A. Yes, there is a passage besides—they got into the house by opening the counting-house door.
RICHARD OGLE . I am a green-grocer, and live in King-street, Deptford, about 200 yards from Fletcher's. On Saturday morning, the 26th of March, I saw the prisoner at a quarter after six o'clock with another young man named Barry, in New King-street—I heard the other say, "Go back, you b—, for the b—policeman is coming"—they were coming in a direction from Fletcher's at the time—they then turned towards London—the young man with the prisoner went a little way up to see if a constable was coming, and then they both joined and went towards London—I saw no more of them—the young man had beckoned to the prisoner, and then he joined him;
Cross-examined. Q. Where were you? A. At my own door—I have been a policeman, but am not so now—I ceased to be one because I went down into the country, and did not like it—I left of my own accord—I was not turned out—I was in the police two years—I left because I had taken some prisoners down to Maidstone, and they said I did not give them enough provision—they did not charge me with taking money from them.
Q. Was you not charged with taking money from them, and not giving it them again? A. They said that was the reason.
DANIEL MURPHY . On Easter Monday last, I saw the prisoner—he desired me to go and get his boots out of pawn, and gave me 1 s. 6 1/2 d. in copper to do so.
ANN BOWLES ( a prisoner.) I know the prisoner. I remember seeing him
in April last—it was the 2nd, if I am not mistaken—it was two or three o'clock in the afternoon, in Ratcliff-highway—I asked him what brought him on that side of the water—he began to smile a bit—he said he had been doing something amiss in Deptford—I asked him what it was—he said he and Barry had broken into Mr. Fletcher's house, and taken 8 l. or 9 l., and a coat out, and Barry sold the coat for 14 s.—he said Barry broke two panes of glass and got in.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Are you married? A. Yes—I have not lived with my husband for two years—I do not walk Ratcliff-highway—I am not an unfortunate woman—I go out charing and washing—I am charged with breaking into Mr. Stubbs's house—I am not guilty of that—I was in Newgate once before, and had nine months at Maidstone Jail—I was tried here—I do not know what it was for—it is going on for two years ago—I do not know rightly what it was for—they charged me with stripping my lodgings—if I was not in distress I should not have done it—I said I did not know what it was, because I did not like to explain it—I do not live with my husband, because he behaved like a wretch to me—I went to Ratcliff-highway on an errand—I live in Bluegate-fields—the first person I told about this, was Sergeant Lovell—he came over to the house, inquiring for Barry and the prisoner—I told him this at Ratcliff-highway—I was taken up on the charge of breaking into Mr. Stubbs's house a fortnight afterwards—I know Barry, but had not seen him for nine or ten years—I have known the prisoner from a child—Barry is a young man with one eye—I went before the Magistrate last Monday month I think.
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant.) On Good Friday, between eleven and twelve o'clock at night, I saw the prisoner with Jem Barry who is blind with one eye—they were about 100 yards from Mr. Fletcher's, standing at the corner of Brunswick-square.
JOHN EVANS . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner on Sunday, the 4th of April, between twelve and one o'clock at night—I told him the charge—he said he knew nothing about it—I found a new cap and a new pair of braces on him, and half a new handkerchief round his neck—I asked where he got them from—he made no answer.
Cross-examined. Q. You found him asleep did not you? A. Yes.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420509-1676]
1676. ANN BOWLES was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Ann Hockan Stubbs, about the hour of four in the night of the 1st of September, at St. Paul, Deptford, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 table-cloth, value 2 s.; 2 pillow-cases, value 1 s.; 1 shift, value 1 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 6 s.; 1 nightgown, value 3 s.; and 2 petticoats, value 3 s., her property; and that she had been previously convicted of felony.
ANN HOCKAN STUBBS . I am a tambour worker, and live at 34, Giffin-street, Deptford—I rent the whole house. On the night of the 1st of September, I went to bed about ten o'clock—I secured the front parlour window with a wedge—I got up between seven and eight o'clock, and missed from the room the articles stated in the indictment, which had been sent to me to mangle—I had seen the bedgown the day before—I have no servant in the house—I found the window shut when I came down, but the wedge was not in, that was found on the floor.
HENRY BURROWES . I am a sawyer, and lodge at the house with my parents who have lived there five yean. On the day of the robbery, I came down at half-past six in the morning, and found the window open—I shut it and went to work—I did not know any thing was gone—I shut it down outside—I did not go into the parlour, but noticed it open when I was outside, and pulled it down—it was not above three feet from the ground—there is no area before it—I know nothing of the prisoner.
ABRAHAM CURTIS . I am a labourer, and live at 32, Giffin-street, within two doors of the prosecutrix. At four o'clock in the morning of the 1st of September, I saw a woman standing at the window of No. 34—I had no previous acquaintance with her—I looked her in the face, and said "You are up early"—she made no answer—I walked away to my work—the window was up at that time—it was quite day-light—I am positive the prisoner is the woman—she had a slate-coloured bonnet on—she had a black bonnet on before the Magistrate—I knew nothing of her before.
Prisoner. I had a green silk bonnet on before the Magistrate—he only said he firmly believed it to be me.
JOHN EVERDEN . I am assistant to Mr. Sharp, a pawnbroker, at Deptford. On the 2nd of September, about eight o'clock in the morning, the prisoner pawned a night-gown for 1 s. 6 d. just as I opened the shop.
Prisoner. I was talking to a woman and she sent me in with the gown saying, he would not take it in from her, as she had quarrelled with him on Saturday night; I came out and gave her the money. Witness. I know nothing of any quarrel with, a woman—I asked the prisoner if it was her own—she said, "Yes"—she was waiting at the door before I opened it, with two others to be served—they were customers.
JOSEPH GREEN ( police-constable R 107.) I apprehended the prisoner on the 30th of April, in Bluegate-fields, Ratcliffe-highway—I could not find her before—I knew her, and had seen her at Deptford, before September—she lived in Barnes-alley—I went there to look for her, on the 2nd of September—I looked for her all over Deptford.
MRS. STUBBS re-examined. The robbery was on the night of the 1st of September—this is my bed-gown.
Prisoner's Defence. I was never in her house in my life—I went to pawn the bedgown, and gave the woman who gave it to me the ticket—I used to come to Deptford constantly to see my child—I was at the station-house against a man named Sullivan—the policeman saw me there a fortnight before he took me, and he asked the reason I did not come against Sullivan the second time.
JOSEPH GREEN re-examined. I did not see her there—I was not there, and did not hear of her being there—she was a witness against Sullivan.
(See page 202.)
JOHN EVANS . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, from Mr. Clark's office—I was present at her trial, and know her to be the person—( read.)
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18420509-1677]
1677. CAROLINE FORMAN and MARY EVANS were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of April, 2 sovereigns, the money of John Nicholls, from his person.
JOHN NICHOLLS . I am a labourer, and live at Lewisham. On the 13th of April,. I was in the Admiral Hardy, at Greenwich—the prisoners
came in, and I gave them some beer—they pleaded poverty, and said they had nothing—I said I could give them nothing else—I went away about one o'clock in the day—they followed me, and decoyed me away to their place—they pulled me about going along—they took me by each arm one on each side, and led me to their room in Roan-street, Deptford—when they got me there they wanted me to go to bed and insisted on stripping me—I do not know what for—I was not sleepy—we had had three or four pots of beer among a lot of us—I would not lay down, and then they began to pull me about—got their hands into my pockets, and then went away—I missed my money, and went after them—it was Forman put her hand into my pocket—Evans was away at first, but when Forman could not get the money from me, Evans came up and assisted her, and then they both went away—I followed them, but they got out of sight—I found them at a public-house—then they said they would be glass to my glass—I said I wanted my money—they began to knock me about shamefully—a young man came up and took my part—I had two sovereigns and some silver safe in my pocket when I went with them—I spent some silver at the Hardy—I was three parts drunk—I was telling a Greenwich pensioner what I was going to do with the money—the prisoners heard that, and then persuaded me to go with them—when they got my money they set off—I am a pensioner and received my pension the day before.
Forman. It is quite false, I neither went home with him, nor was I in the room at the time; I waited below till he gave Evans 1 s. to get something to drink. Witness. I never gave her a halfpenny—I said I had a wife and family at home, and could not afford it.
Forman. I was away an hour and a half before I heard anything about it; then he came and said he would give me into custody for stealing a sovereign; I said I had never seen him; the policeman searched me, and said I had nothing about me, and then he said somebody else must have taken it. Witness. I never said somebody else must have taken it—one could not get the better of me—the other came up and insisted on having it—they took it from me by main force. Evans. He gave me 1 s. to fetch a pot of beer and gin. I said, "Do you know what you have given me"—he said, "Yes, keep what you have got." Witness. I did not—we had nothing to drink except at the public-house.
WILLIAM ROMAN . I am a sawyer, and live in Randal-place, Roan-street. On Wednesday, the 13th of April, at half-past four o'clock in the afternoon, I saw the prisoners come out of the Grey Coat Boy, Roan-street—the prosecutor followed them—I heard him say they had robbed him of his money—they both directly knocked him down over a step into the gutter—that happened three times afterwards, and the fourth time he could not get up—I assisted him and went for a policeman—he was the worse for liquor—he was not sensible for several minutes after the fourth fall—the prisoners went to go up a court, and he went to stop them from going, saying he would have a policeman to take them, and that was the fourth time they knocked him down—I then ran and fetched a policeman—I saw Evans come out of the court the same evening, between six and seven—she shook her bands with some money, and said she had got the old b—'s money, and would have a jolly good drink with it—I heard that as I passed by—it was the court they had run up before.
Forman. The man hit me in the mouth, which was the reason I hit him. Witness. He did not hit her at all—she gave him a black eye.
JOSEPH HARRIS . I am shopman to John Smith, a draper, of Stockwell-street, Greenwich. On Wednesday the 13th, about seven o'clock in the evening, the two prisoners came to master's shop and bought calico and stockings, ribbon, and a handkerchief, to the amount of 1 l. 1 s. 7 d.—Evans gave a sovereign to Forman to change at first—it was not quite sufficient, and she gave her a second—Forman bought all the goods, and the other produced the money.
Forman. She told me he had given her the two sovereigns for two shillings, and she asked me to go with her to buy the handkerchiefs.
WILLIAM THOMAS ARTHUR . I am a policeman. On the 13th of April, at eight o'clock, I was on duty, and took Evans into custody for being partly intoxicated, and found half a sovereign, and two half-crowns upon her—she gave the half-sovereign to change to a young man to pay 4 s. 6 d. for a window which she had broken—I asked how she came by the money—she said a young man had come down to see her who was in the habit of coming, but not paying her every time he came, and she received it from him—I found the goods now produced at the prisoners' lodging, a common brothel in Roan-Street—I found a pair of the new stockings on Forman's feet, and a pair of old ones left behind in the lodging—the other articles bought at the linendraper's were all down stairs, in the room of the house they both lodge in.
Forman. She said the man gave her the money—I did not think it any harm to put the stockings on. Witness. I found some of the calico cut off for shifts—Forman was not taken till the following morning.
JOHN WHITE . I apprehended Forman the next morning, in a court in Roan-street—I told her it was for robbing a man of two sovereigns last night—she said, "I did not rob the man, I know nothing about it; I never had any money in my possession all day yesterday"—I took her to the station, returned and ascertained she had bought four handkerchiefs at a linen-draper's shop—I went to the cells and found one handkerchiefs on each of the prisoner's necks, and two more at a pawnbroker's.
JOSEPH HARRIS re-examined. These are two of the handkerchiefs they bought at our shop.
Evans's Defence. I was in liquor; the man came up stairs and asked What he was to give me—I told him two shillings, instead of which he gave Me two sovereigns. I said to him, "Are you aware what you have given Me?"—he said, "Yes, keep what you have got;" he asked me to fetch Him a pot of beer; I returned with it; he swore I robbed him.
FORMAN— GUILTY . Aged 22.
EVANS *— GUILTY . Aged 22.
Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Justice Alderson.
[trialAccount t18420509-1678]
1678. ROBERT WILSON was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously assaulting Patrick Cavannah on the 1st of April, by shooting at him with a certain loaded pistol, with intent feloniously, willfully, and of his malice aforethought, to kill and murder him.—3rd COUNT, to do him some grievous bodily harm.
ANN CHERRY . I live at Eltham, three miles on the other side of Woolwich, in Kent. I live at the house of the prisoner's mother—her name is
Mrs. Wilson—she is married to Patrick Cavannah, but goes by the name of Wilson—Cavannah had only come home three weeks before this happened, and lived there—he is not here to-day. On the 1st of April I heard him and the prisoner quarrelling between nine and ten o'clock—the prisoner came to the door and asked Cavannah, his father-in-law, to let him in—he said, "You shall not come in to-night, Robert"—he went and opened the door, and then the prisoner went away—he came back again in about five minutes and threw some little stones at the door—he said, "I want to come in"—I am sure it was the prisoner's voice—master went across to the fire-place and got the poker—he said, "You shall not come in to-night, Robert, but you may burst the door open"—then I heard a pistol immediately fired off—the door was shut at the time—the ball of the pistol went into master's cheek—I saw master's cheek bleeding immediately after I heard the noise of the pistol—master was stooping at the time, his head was close to where the parlour-door opens, about the middle, above the lock.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was it not immediately upon your master's saying "You may burst the door open" that you heard the report? A. Yes—the door had not been burst open—the prisoner could not see my master.
FRANCIS BOSSEY . I am a surgeon. I was sent for to attend Cavannah and found a wound upon his cheek about an inch and a half below the angle of the mouth—I introduced a probe, which passed about an inch and three-quarters by the side of the jaw—it came in contact with a pistol bullet, which I extracted.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see the prisoner? A. No—Cavannah had been drunk but was in some degree sobered by the accident—he had evidently been more drunk than he appeared—he was by no means sober when I saw him.
MARK WELLS . I am a musician. I saw the prisoner the night this happened, standing opposite his mother's door (I had heard the report of a pistol about three minutes before)—I saw the prisoner take a pistol out of his side pocket and fire it up the street—he said, "There is no ball in that, but whether or not, I have discharged it in the air"—he was decidedly drunk at the time.
THOMAS CLARK . I live in Powis-street, Woolwich, the prisoner came into the shop on the 1st of April and wished to look at a pair of pistols—I showed him a pair at 25 s.—he said "they will do, have the kindness to book them to my mother, Mrs. Wilson"—I said I could not book them unless his mother was present and sanctioned it—he said, "Well, I will take them; come home with me, mother will pay for them"—I went with him about a quarter to nine o'clock—his mother was asleep—he said, "I shall not disturb her, I will pay you"—he had them of me, but had no powder or bullets—he was sober then.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see Cavannah at the house when you went there? A. I saw him just as I was coming away, but I did not see the prisoner when I saw him.
CHARLES GRAY . On the night of the 1st of April, about a quarter to ten o'clock, I saw the prisoner at the sign of the Director General, Wellington-street, with a sailor—he appeared very much in liquor—he had a pair of pistols with him, and said he was going to shoot sparrows with them—but afterwards he said that fellow had come to his house and taken possession of his mother's premises during his absence in Syria, and he would have his revenge, and blow the b—'s brains out—he left the room afterwards
—said he was going out for a few minutes, and was absent about a quarter of an hour—he said nothing about going home when he left—he came back, said he could not get in, and asked the publican to go home with him—he said, "I cannot"—then he asked me to go—I afterwards consented, went to the house, knocked at the door, and asked for Mr. Wilson—a person inside said he was not at home—I asked if he would open the door—he said he would not—I returned to the public-house—I do not know where the prisoner went to.
MICHAEL GARVEY . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner in Bathplace, Wellington-street, Woolwich—he fired a pistol just at the time I came up to take him and said something—I found a pistol in his band, and took one from his pocket—they were both discharged—I had seen him discharge one of them up the street—the other was not loaded—I could not see whether it bad been recently fired, for I did not examine it—I went at first on hearing the report of fire-arms, and as I went I saw him fire another—as I took him to the station he said, "I have done it, and it would not have happened only I was drunk"—I looked at the door of the house and saw a hole through it four feet four inches from the bottom, and nine inches and three-quarters from the jamb—it might be nine or ten inches from the handle, but I did not take notice of that—it was about eighteen inches from the key-hole—it is the street-door and opens into the passage, which is of the width of the door.
ANN CHERRY re-examined. I was inside the parlour when I heard the pistol fired—the parlour door opens into the passage, and is about two feet from the street-door—two persons can pass in the passage—I could hear distinctly any thing that passes—there was no light in the passage.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder,
[trialAccount t18420509-1679]
1679. JOHN DALEY, JAMES ELLIS , and WILLIAM STEVENS were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Lawrence Rusland, on the 13th of May, at St. Paul, Deptford, and stealing therein, 2 hinges, value 1 s. 6 d., his goods.
THOMAS RAFFERTY . I am a wood-chopper, and live in Lower-road, Deptford. On the 13th of May, in the day-time, I saw the three prisoners together near Black-Horse-bridge—I saw Stevens pull up a plank and knock down part of a brick wall of Mr. Rutland's premises—then he went down into the cellar, pulled up the iron hinges, wrapped them in a coarse apron, and put them on the brick wall—the other two prisoners were standing looking at him, not many yards off—he gave the hinges to Ellis—they all three walked away together—they had not got many yards before Stevens handed the hinges to Ellis—I afterwards saw them all three together.
THOMAS MOORS ( police-constable M 231.) Rafferty pointed the prisoners out to me in Rotherhithe—I apprehended them—I asked what they had done with the hinges—Stevens said the witness was telling lies, that they had done nothing with the hinges—John Eyres gave me two hinges.
JOHN EYRES . At a quarter after two o'clock on Thursday, the 13th of May, I met the prisoners in the Lower-road, Rotherhithe—Daley was down at the water edge in the ditch—from information I received I went to the spot, and found two hinges lying in the ditch, under the water—I gave them to the officer.
LAWRENCE RUSLAND . These hinges were not fixed—they laid in
my cellar, and had been taken off a gate—the prisoners broke down part of the wall, four feet long and six inches high, and then forced up the plank, the covering of the cellar—this was part of the premises—my house is in the parish of Deptford—the boards of the cellar were safe in the middle of the day, put down temporarily, but could not be moved without force.
Daley's Defence. The house was empty, and the hinges laid on the top of a brick wall.
ME. RUSLAND re-examined. It was not empty—I have lived there eighteen months.
DALEY— GUILTY . Aged 19.
STEVENS— GUILTY . Aged 19.
Confined One Year.
ELLIS— GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18420509-1680]
1680. MARY POSTON was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of January, 2 boots, value 4 s., the goods of Charles Jackson.
JAMES HONOR . I am shopman to Charles Jackson, a shoemaker, at Greenwich. On the 15th of January, I missed some boots—in consequence of information, I went after the prisoner, and found her in Stockwell-street, and stopped her—she directly said she would give me all the money she was possessed of if I would let her go—I found these boots in her possession—they are my master's—they were thrown over a piece of wire—they could not be got at without cutting the string with a knife.
JOSEPH BUCKMASTER ( police-constable R 42.) I took the prisoner into custody—she gave me these boots and said, "Let me go home, I have only picked them up"—she had a knife in her possession—the shoes were two odd ones, and appeared to have been cut.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going along, and saw some shoes lying on the ground; I picked up two, put them on my arm, and walked down the street; I did not cut them.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1681]
1681. CATHERINE SKENNESSY was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of April, 4 half-crowns and 5 shillings, the monies of Benjamin Hubble, from his person.
BENJAMIN HUBBLE . I am a builder, living in Garden-row, Newtown, Deptford. On Monday night, the 18th of April, about half-past eleven o'clock, I was in New-street, Deptford, and found some person's hand in my coat-pocket, unknown to me—as they drew their band from my pocket, I caught hold of their left hand with my right—it escaped from my hand—four half-crowns and five shillings were taken from my pocket by some one, who I do not know—I consider it was a female by the clothes.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear I had my hand in your pocket? A. No, certainly not.
ANN HOBBS . I am a female searcher. I searched the prisoner on the evening of the 18th, and found three half-crowns and two shillings on her.
JOHN HEMSLEY ( police-constable R 193.) I heard a cry of "Stop thief," and ran after the prisoner—I caught her running down the street—I saw her running from the prosecutor's arms—no one else was near—I
took her into custody—she said she had only 6 d. about her, and that was her own money—when we got to the station she pulled two shillings and a half-crown out of her bosom, and a shilling she had in her hand.
Prisoner's Defence. The money was given to me by a gentleman—on Monday night I was going home, and met the prosecutor coming out of a public-house; he asked me if I would take a walk with him; I did; he took me down a place called Europe-walk; going down this walk, he said he had no more money than 6 d.; instead of 6 d. it was a shilling; he gave it me, and wanted to take indecent liberties with me, and I ran away, with the shilling in my hand.
BENJAMIN HUBBLE re-examined. I took no liberties with her—I had been supping that evening at the Red Lion—I left there about ten o'clock—I had received the 15 s. in change for a sovereign—the policeman might have seen her escape from my person—I went to catch bold of her.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420509-1682]
1682. ELLEN WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of April, 1 coat, value 20 s., the goods of Morris Deedy.
MORRIS DEEDY . I am foreman in the Victualling-yard at Deptford, and live in New-row. I missed a coat on the 11th of April—it was safe at one o'clock in the day—this is it.
MARY DEEDY . I missed the coat on Tuesday morning—it was safe on Monday, the 11th—the prisoner lived in the front chamber.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you a servant? A. No—I missed the coat on the Monday—I had seen it last on my husband's back on Sunday.
JOHN BROWN . I am shopman to Mr. Marriot, a pawnbroker. I produce this coat—I took it in pledge of a woman that answers the description of the prisoner—to the best of my belief it was her—it was pledged in the name of Ann M'Donald.
Cross-examined. Q. You will not venture to swear to her? A. will not—she has never pawned any thing at our shop that I am aware of—I say, to the best of my belief I think she is the person, but I do not swear positively—I cannot say whether she had a child with her as the box was full.
JOHN EVANS . I am a policeman. In consequence of information, I went to the prisoner's lodgings—I told her she was suspected of stealing Mr. Deedy's coat—she said she knew nothing about it, and requested me to go with her up stairs to her apartment, to search—I said I should—I called Mrs. Deedy up stairs, and the prisoner took several things out of her box—among them was a shawl, a silk handkerchief, and a pair of new stockings—Mrs. Deedy saw these articles, and said they were in pledge at the time the coat was stolen—the prisoner said they were not, they were taken out a week before the coat was stolen—I asked if she would go with me to the pawnbroker's where she took them out from—she said she would—I went there with her, and asked the pawnbroker if she had taken out articles on the Monday or Tuesday previous—the pawnbroker examined the tickets, and found she had not—I referred him to the 11th and 12th, and he found she had taken out these articles on Tuesday, the 12th—I found she had laid out several sums of money.
Cross-examined. Q. What do you mean by "finding out?" was she with you? A. She was—she was not long at the pawnbroker's—she went with me willingly. NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18420509-1683]
1683. JAMES HURLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of April, 12lbs. weight of bacon, value 5 s., the goods of Richard Michael Wild.
RICHARD MICHAEL WILD . I am a cheese monger and live in Turpin-lane, Greenwich. This is my bacon—it was safe about nine o'clock on Saturday night, the 16th of April—I missed it on Monday morning.
JAMES LAST . I am a policeman. I found this bacon on the prisoner in South-street, about eleven o'clock on Saturday night, the 16th, and in taking him back to the place he said he bought it at, he ran away from me, and threw it down.
WILLIAM WILTON . I met the prisoner with the constable, going back with this bacon—I followed him, and saw him throw the bacon down, and run away—I picked it up.
JOHN ROSCOE . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner a fortnight after this—as we were taking him to the station, he said he could prove that he was not there that night—I had not made reference to any night.
Prisoner's Defence. I was not in Greenwich that evening; I was in the Ordnance Arms, about three-quarters of a mile from there, from ten to twelve o'clock.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420509-1684]
1684. GEORGE WILKINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of May, 2 pairs of trowsers, value 1 l. 5 s. 6 d., and 1 handkerchief, value 8 d.; the goods of Thomas Allen.,
THOMAS ALLEN . I am a private in the Royal Marines, at Woolwich, On Wednesday evening, the 4th of May, about five o'clock, I came from Hedge-row with a bundle of clothes—I went into the Three Dews—the prisoner was there—I then went to the Marquis of Granby, and had my bundle there—soon after I missed it—it contained two pairs of trowsers—these now produced are them.
Prisoner. Are they your own property? Witness. They are not—I had them to repair, and have had to pay 25 s. 6 d. for them—I had not been drinking before we went into the Three Daws—I went to Hedge-row to fetch the bundle—I had not drank there—I work for different persons in the barracks—I think we had ten pots of beer in the Three Daws—seven or eight persons drank, but I and my comrade paid for it, and for one pot at the Marquis of Granby—we had no gin.
ISAAC FISHER . I am a sergeant in the Royal Marines. I was at the Marquis of Granby—I saw Allen with his bundle, and saw him put it on the form—I saw the prisoner take it up, and place it on his knee—he kept it there for about a minute and a half, and then walked out at the door with it—I gave information to the prosecutor, when he looked round for it—I did not know where the prisoner was going to take it.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me take it into the street? A. No, I saw you take it out of the tap-room—there was no fighting going on at the time—there were five marines there, and that was all.
SUSANNAH BASS . I keep a clothes-shop at Woolwich. I bought these things of a marine—I cannot conscientiously swear it was the prisoner—I gave 8 s. 6 d. in money, and some shirts and stockings which he wanted—they amounted to 14 s. to the best of my knowledge—I cannot say exactly what time I bought them—it was after daylight on Thursday, the 5th of May—the man was sober—I have little doubt but it was the prisoner.
Prisoner. She says it was on the 5th of May—on the 5th, in the
morning, about half-past nine o'clock, I was on her Majesty's dock-guard at Woolwich, and there I remained till the next day.
THOMAS ALLEN re-examined. She swore before the Magistrate to buying them on the 4th—the prisoner was absent from the barracks on Wednesday night—I do not know whether he was on dock-guard on the Thursday.
Prisoner. He has confessed that I was—he sent a man down to me while I was on guard to ask if I knew anything about the trowsers.
Witness. I saw him on Thursday morning about nine o'clock, and asked what he had done with the bundle—he said he knew nothing about it—I asked him again on Saturday, and he then said he knew where it was, and he would go and fetch it for me—he went out about five o'clock, and came in on Sunday without it—the young man who owns the bundle said he would give him any money for it, and he said he knew nothing about it.
Prisoner. I said I would go down to the public-house and see if it was there; I never had them.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18420509-1684a]
1684. JAMES CUMMINGS was indicted for bigamy.
SUSAN COLLIVER . I live in Church-street, Greenwich. On the 10th of January last I was married to the prisoner by license at Stoke Damerel Church, Devonshire. I lived with my mother up to that time at Stoke Damerel—she is a pensioner's wife—lived with the prisoner a week, and then some other woman came and claimed him—I had no money—he conducted himself well—it is I that prosecute.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYEN. Q. I believe you or friend paid the fees for the marriage? A. Yes—the prisoner was in the Sappers and Miners—I never heard that his other wife had been guilty of felony and imprisoned, and that led him to enlist in the Sappers and Miners—I did not send word that if he would pay the money and wedding expenses there would not be anything done to him—I send to him to say I heard he was married—we lived together one week—he left me because the company came at Woolwich—he was obliged to go—I intended to follow the month after—he did not leave me—the first wife came to me herself—I did not learn from her how long she was absent from hi—I had known the prisoner seven weeks before I married him—he came down to that place with his regiment.
THOMAS WINDSOR . I am a stonemason, living at Luton Abbott in Devonshire. I was present on the 4th of march, 1833, at the church of Abbott Kerswell, Devonshire, and saw the prisoner and Margaret M'Quinness married—I was saw her alive Wednesday morning.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the prisoner and Margaret M'Quinness after they were married? A. Yes, I know of no misfortune happening to M'Quinness—they lived in devonshire three years—I have seen nothing of them since then—I cannot tell anything that happened since they left Devonshire.
DANIEL RIORAN . ( police-constable R 23.) I apprehended the prisoner—I asked him if he was married—he said he was married to Margaret M'Quinness—I asked him if he know a person named Susan Colliver—he said he did, he was married to her—I said it was very bad job—he said,
it was a very bad job, but he must be either mad or foolish at the time he got married—I produce copies of the two registers of marriage—( read.)
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined One Year.
SURREY CASES.

1685. JOHN MASTERMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of April, 1 piece of timber, value 3 s. 6 d. the goods of William Cubitt.
JOHN PEERING . I live in Noah's Ark-court, Standgate, Lambeth—I am in the employ of Mr. William Cubitt, who is repairing Westminster-bridge. On the morning of the 28th of April I was on the bridge, and saw the prisoner in a barge—he drew a piece of timber off the platform, and threw it into the water—he afterwards towed it through the arch with a boat which he was in, took it to the stairs of the bridge, and left it—I pointed it out to George Maynard.
GEORGE MAYNARD . I live in Henry-street, Kennington-lane, and am a lighterman to Mr. Cubitt. On the morning of the 28th of April I heard something—I went to Westminster-bridge, and there saw the prisoner, who was pointed out to me by Perring—he had a maul hammer in his hand—I saw this piece of timber—he had knocked a large nine-inch spike out of it—I asked him what he had done with the timber—he said he had picked it up afloat—I gave him into custody—I knew the piece of timber, which I found to be Mr. William Cubitt's—it is over the way now.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been to Hungerford-market; I was coming across the water and the piece of timber was driving by; I picked it up, towed it ashore, and left it there; I went to see if I could find who it belonged to; I could not, so I came back, Maynard came and collared me, and said he had seen me take it off their premises.
NOT GUILTY .

1686. ANN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of April, 8lbs. weight of pork, value 5 s., the goods of William Sutton; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
JOSIAH HARRIS . I am in the service of Mr. William Sutton, a butcher, in Meadow-row, Newington. On Sunday morning, the 17th of April, I placed a piece of pork in the tray with other meat, on the outside board—I missed it that morning—I had missed meat three Sunday mornings before.
JOHN BRANNAN . I am an engraver, and live in Racquet-court, Fleet-street. On Sunday morning, the 17th of April, I was opposite Mr. Sutton's shop between nine and ten o'clock—I saw the prisoner come and take a piece of pork from the tray, put it under her shawl, and walk away with it about a dozen yards—when she saw me coming behind her, she dropped it behind a basket of greens, by a greengrocer's door—she was not out of my sight from the time of her taking it till she dropped it—I saw her taken, and then she offered to pay for it.
Prisoner. I did it through want.
JAMES BROOK . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the Clerk of the Peace of Surrey—( read)—I was present at her trial—the prisoner is the person.
Prisoner. It is false; I was convicted once for a pair of stockings, but never before.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.

1688. JOHN BULMER and MARY BULMER were indicated for stealing, on the 12th of April, 5 decanters, value 12 s.; and 4 plates, value 10 s.; the goods of Robert Passenger, the master of John Bulmer.
THOMAS SPENCES . I am clerk to Mr. Robert Passenger, a potter and glass man, in William-street. I John Bulmer was his servant—we have lost some decanters—we searched the prisoner' house in Dyer's-buildings, Gravel-lane, after they were in custody, and found these two decanter in a band-box in the front bed-room, and three smaller ones between the bed-clothes in a back-room—I know these plates to be my master's—we have missed such—I cannot tell when we lost them—they were found in the bottom room front of the prisoner's house.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you undertake to you have missed these five plates? A. I missed them after seeing them there, not previous—we have stock on the premises of the same pattern—I have not counted them since to see if any are gone—I know them from the peculiar patter—no one else has the pattern—I can only speak to the decanters from their general appearance—it is a common pattern—I could not swear to them—I do not know how many children the male prisoner has got—the female prisoner is his sister, and lived with him—he had been about ten year in the prosecutor' service.
(John Bulmer received an excellent character.)
JOHN BULMER.— GUILTY. Aged 42.— Strongly recommended to mercy.
MARY BULMER.— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1689]
1689. JOHN BULMER and MARY BULMER were again indicated for stealing, on the 13th of April, 1 decater, value 2 s., the goods of Robert Passenger, the master of John Bulmer; to which
JOHN BULMER. pleaded GUILTY . Aged 42.— Confined Six Months.
THOMAS SPENCER . In consequence of circumstances I marked this decanter on Wednesday, the 13th of April, about the time I knew Mary would bring John's tea—I placed myself on the floor above them, and looked through an opening in the floor—I saw Mary com and walk up to him—he said, "I thought it was your footstep"—he then stooped down and reached out from a dark corner the decanter where it was placed, and put it into her lap—she had her apron thrown over her arm, and drew her shawl over it—I then came down—she remained talking with her brother for some time, and at last went away—I followed her out, brought her back, and asked what she had that did not belong to her—she said, "Nothing"
—I put my hand into her apron, took out the decanter, and said to her, "What do you call this? where did you get it from?"—she said, "My brother gave it to me"—I asked, "For what purpose?"—she said she did not know—I said, "At all events it was to take away"—she said, "Yes"—the constable then came, and I gave her and John into custody—I desired him to come in to his sister, and said, "John, your sister tells me you gave her this decanter, is it so?"—he said, "Yes, it is"—I told the officer to take him.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had this decanter been found by you before this occurrence took place? A. No, by some one else—it was shown to me secreted under the stairs—it had been evidently removed by some one intending to do something improper with it—it was not off the premises—it was between two and three o'clock when I first saw it—to the best of my belief, I said before the Magistrate that I asked her where she got it, and she said, "My brother gave it me"—I said, "What for?" and she said she did not know—I think those were the words I used before the Magistrate—if it was not taken down it is the fault of the clerk—my deposition was read over to me before I signed it, and I was asked if I had any thing to add or amend—I do not know whether I did say so.
MARY BULMER— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1690]
1690. GEORGE MARROTT and JOHN FINCH were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of April, 80lbs. weight of lead, value 30 s., the goods of Thomas Salmon, and fixed to a certain building.
WILLIAM MARTIN . I live with my uncle, Thomas Salmon, who is a builder. He has built some houses in Tidman's-fields—I was on the gutters on the 15th, and they were all safe—on the 18th I missed some lead which had been fixed to the building.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Where is your uncle? A. Gone to Smithfield—these houses were built about three or four months ago—I was present at the building—I know they are my uncle's houses—he did not put the lead on them—the plumbers did—he superintended it, and paid for it—he was here about five minutes ago, and said he would come back—he was before the Magistrate, but was not examined—my uncle took the land of Mr. Wood, Lord Holland's steward—my uncle is a poor man, and lives in lodgings—he has no builder's yard or shop—he keeps the bricks on the premises when he has got any, when he is building—I do not know any thing about my uncle's entering into a written agreement with Mr. Wood—I cannot tell how long these houses have been building—I have not been with my uncle above six months—they have been left to go to rack and ruin—the floors are not in, nor the doors and windows—when I first came to my uncle they were going on—I have seen Mr. Wood on the premises now and then.
COURT. Q. Have you seen your uncle directing the men when they were building? A. Yes—I have been there every day for three months to look after the men for my uncle—there were about twenty men working there at first—I saw my uncle pay them, at Mr. Parker's—none of the houses are let—my uncle is employed by persons to build for them—the lead was put on the premises about four months ago—I saw it put on by Mr. Jones, the plumber's man—I have not seen Mr. Wood on the premises since that—I saw my uncle pay Mr. Jones upwards of 20 l., at the beer-shop, between two and three months ago—he received that money
from Mr. Shepherd, a solicitor—he is my uncle's solicitor, and Mr. Wood's as well.
EDMUND BUNCE ( police-constable V 19.) On Saturday night, the 16th of April, about ten minutes past eight o'clock, I was in Southampton New-road, Tidman's-fields—I heard the slates cracking—I stood opposite for some few minutes, and saw two persons pass through the sky-light on the top of the house No. 2—I fetched another constable and a labourer to my assistance—I sent a man to No. 4, to know what business they had there—I heard some lead thrown off from the roof against the back-door, where I stood, and the prisoners came down through the rafters from floor to floor, Marrott in No. 1, and Finch in No. 2—I took Finch into custody on the rafters—I have examined the lead thrown down, with the roofs, and find it had come from the sky-light—it fitted exactly, nail-holes and every thing.
MARROTT— GUILTY . Aged 41.
FINCH— GUILTY . Aged 42.
Confined Six Month.

1692. THOMAS RUMFITT was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of April, 4 1/2 lbs. weight of solder, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of John Wordsworth Robson, his master.
JOHN WORDSWORTH ROBSON . I live in Jamaica-terrace—the prisoner was a plumber in my service. On Monday, the 25th of April, I sent him to do a job for me at the Bull Head Dock, on board the Bathurst —he went on with the job till Friday, the 29th—about 18lbs. of solder was required to do the work—on Friday, the 29th, I went to the work, and found a much greater quantity of solder had been taken away from my premises than had been used—(I had seen him take away 1qr. 9Ibs. in the first instance)—I examined the pot, to see what was left in it, and found a great deal less than I imagined there ought to have been—there was scarcely sufficient left to make a joint with—I saw the prisoner's bag lying close by, and found in it three ladle cakes of solder—I immediately took up my ladle, tried the ladle cakes in it, and it fixed exactly, so that I had every reason to presume that the solder was mine—it had no right there—I left a person to watch the bag, and went outside, intending to intercept the prisoner as he left the premises—in about half-an-hour he came out, without his bag, and I allowed him to pass—I then went, examined the bag, and the solder was gone—I immediately followed the prisoner, but he had got too far a-head of me—about ten o'clock next morning my book-keeper called, and told me something; in consequence of which I took a policeman with me down to the job—the prisoner had gone to the ship—I followed him there, and charged him with stealing the solder—he said it was all used in the job—I weighed the remainder, and found 21lbs, short—I then asked him what he had done with the three ladle cakes that were in his bag the night before—he said he had left them in the poter's lodge—I said, "What, in your bag?"—he said, I said, "Where did you find your bag this morning?"—he said, "In the porter's lodge"—I said,
"What did you do with the three ladle cakes of solder?"—he said, "I melted them down this morning"—I said, "You did not melt them down this morning; I have witnesses to prove they were not in your bag last night, therefore I shall give you in charge on your own explanation."
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When was it you told him that if he told you what he knew about it you would not be hard with him? A. After I gave him in charge—he has a wife and eight children—the solder taken only amounts to 2 s. 6 d.—he had 30 s. a week—18lbs. of solder was returned from the vessel—lqr. 21lbs. was used on the vessel in the job—9lbs. and 5lbs. of it was capable of making a great quantity, as it was nearly all tin—I saw him take 37lbs.—he took more afterwards, but I did not see him—18lbs. was returned, and I supposed 18lbs. to be used in the job.
JOHN MORRIS . I was told to watch the prisoner's bag when he went away—when the prosecutor came back I examined it, and the solder was gone—I saw him fit the solder to the ladle, and it fitted exactly.
DAVID SALT . I am gate-keeper to Mr. Baines. The bag was left in my lodge—it had no solder in it.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1693]
1693. CHARLES HARRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of April, at Wandsworth, 25 spoons, value 17 l. 10 s.; 3 ladles, value 5 l. 13 s.; 13 forks, value 10 l. 18 s.; 1 butter-knife, value 15 s.; and 1 tray, value 1 s.; the goods of Archibald Leslie, in his dwelling-house.
ELLEN HUNTBEACH . I am cook to Mr. Archibald Leslie, of St. Ann's Hill, Wandsworth; he dwells there; it is in the parish of Wandsworth. Between three and four o'clock on the 12th of April, I was sitting in the kitchen—I heard a noise—I went to the pantry cupboard to see if the plate was all right, and it was gone—I called the lad to run and see if he could see any one—we both ran, about twenty yards from the gate there were two men running—they had got this plate-tray between them—the prisoner was one of them—they dropped the tray—some man picked it up and gave it to me—it is Mr. Leslie's tray—it contained twenty-five spoons, some ladles, forks, and a butter-knife, all silver.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Has Archibald Leslie any other name? A. No; when I heard the noise I went out of the kitchen-door into the passage, to see if I could see any one—I could see no one, and I went from there into the pantry, and missed the plate—I went up the area steps into the road, and when I got out at the gate, about twenty yards from the area steps, I saw the two men running—I only saw the prisoner's back.
THOMAS PARNCUTT . I am servant to Mr. Leslie. I ran out on the 12th of April, and saw two men, one of whom was the prisoner, with the plate-tray between them—I caught the prisoner—he was never out of my sight—I am sure he was one of those that were running with the tray—they ran—I ran after them—two ladles were dropped, which I picked up—I called "Stop thief"—a baker helped to stop them—I had seen the plate about half an hour before in the pantry cupboard—I had never seen the prisoner before.
Cross-examined. Q. When you got out did you see the prisoner? A. Yes, about thirty yards from me—he had got out of the gate, and was about twenty yards from it—Huntbeach came up directly—it was about a mile from
where I ran after them first, to where he was taken into custody—it was on the open common—the prisoner did not turn down any street—I saw him taken into custody.
STEPHEN EVERETT . I am a baker, at Wands worth. I heard the cry of "Stop thief," and saw the prisoner running—I chased him about a mile—there was a ditch—he was on one side and I on the other—I lost sight of him, but I knew him again—I stopped him at last.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you in front of Parncutt, or before him? A. I lost sight of him and the prisoner also for a short time—I had to go round the road—it formed a sort of half triangle.
(James Bridgford, upholsterer, Eliza-place, Pimlico; John Elkett, cooper, Elizabeth-street, Pimlico; and Richard Blissett, shoemaker, Queen-street, Pimlico; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18420509-1694]
1694. JOHN COCHRANE , JOHN COCHRANE, JUN ., and GEORGE BAXTER were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 5 pairs of unmade trowsers, value 1 l.; 10 pieces of wollen-cloth, containing 6 yards, value 1 l.; and 10 yards of calico, value 4 s.; the goods of John Hay, their master: and CATHERINE COCHRANE for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen: against the Statute, &c.
GEORGE WILLIAM FARLAM . I am foreman to John Hay, an army contractor and tailor, in Staple-street, Long-lane, Bermondsey; the three male prisoners were in his employ. These six pairs of trowsers and four small pieces of cloth are my master's—I am sure of that—on Thursday the policeman came, and I gave the male prisoners into custody—John Cochrane, Jun. was only given into custody on suspicion—the elder Cochrane came in, and on undoing his trowsers he pulled out this piece of unmade trowsers from between the waistband of his trowsers and handed them to me.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you find any thing on young Cochrane? A. Nothing—the woman was not given into custody by me—I saw her in custody at the Police Court—I understand she is the wife of the elder Cochrane.
SUSAN BOVIS . I live with my aunt, Mary Craddock, in Kent-street, Borough. On Saturday, the 2nd of April, all the four prisoners came to our house together—I saw the younger Cochrane take some blue cloth out of the waistband of his trowsers, as I was coming through the room—I saw the elder Cochrane at the same time take some cloth out of his trowsers—Catherine received it from both.
Cross-examined. Q. Her husband was there at the time? A. Yes—I cannot point out the blue cloth which the younger Cochrane took from his trowsers—as I was coming through there was some cloth on the form—he sat down on it, and was doing up his trowsers—I do not know what the name of the policeman is who spoke to me just now—perhaps I might hear his name—we have not been in conversation before to day—he is no acquaintance of mine—my aunt called him in and told him this—it was after I saw young Cochrane put the cloth from his trowsers—I have not been in communication with any of the police on this matter—I am not acquainted with any of them—they have not been in the habit of coming to my house—I do not know. Thomas Turvey—I do not know who
he is—I know 196 M —I have not kept company with him at all—he had not been in my aunt's house before the Thursday the prisoners were taken—I had never spoken to him before—I am quite sure I had no communication with him—I am seventeen years old—I do not know where Turvey lives—I do not know whether he is married—I cannot say whether young Cochrane was buttoning up his trowsers when I first saw him—I cannot swear to the piece of cloth—I could not see whether it was cut or marked in any particular way—it was folded up—I do not know who brought the police to the house—I told my uncle about it—I did not hear what the policeman said to me just now—he took hold of my frock—I do not know what he said—he gave me a little touch.
THOMAS TURVEY ( police-constable M 196.) In consequence of information I went to Mr. Hay's premises with Bovis—she stopped in the street while I went in and saw Mr. Hay—the three male prisoners came in—I followed them when they went out, to the coffee-house, where Bovis lives—they had something bulky with them—I remained outside in the street—I did not stop them then as I had no assistance—soon after Catherine came out with a large bundle under her arm—I followed her into Mint-street—I know she had nothing when she went into the coffee-shop.
Cross-examined. Q. Who gave you the information that took you to Mr. Hay's house? A. Mrs. Craddock, the landlady of the coffee-shop, on Thursday, the 7th—Bovis was there—that was before I saw either of the prisoners there—I never had any acquaintance with Mrs. Craddock at all—I was never in the house before—I had seen Bovis many times in Mrs. Craddock's house—I do not know how long I have known Bovis—I have been on that beat two months, and I have seen her most days—I never spoke to her in my life—I did not know her name was Susan till the Friday—I do not know that I ever met her in the street—I may have met her, but I never took any notice of her—I mean to swear that—I spoke to her when she was in the witness box—I told her she was speaking of a former occasion, and told her to confine herself to the 7th—I am quite sure that was all I said—I did not take hold of her by the arm at all—I neither pulled or touched her—I am married.
THOMAS SWAIN ( police-constable M 106.) I went into the cutting-room after Mr. Farlam—I saw the elder Cochrane take a pair of trowsers from his trowsers, and give them to the foreman, and he gave them to me.
(Patrick Kelly, tailor; Michael Cox, tailor, Petticoat-lane; Anthony Molloy, corn dealer, Upton-street, St. George's; gave the Cochrane's good characters) JOHN COCHRANE — GUILTY . Aged 62.— Transported for Seven Years.
JOHN COCHRANE, JUN.— GUILTY. Aged 20.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor, believing him to have acted under his father's influence.—
Confined Three Months.
BAXTER and CHARLOTTE COCHRANE— NOT GUILTY .

1695. WILLIAM HAMMERSTON was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of April, 4lbs. weight of copper pipe, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of John Bucknell Langton and another, his masters.
CHARLES HENRY DOWNING . I am manager for John Bucknell Langton and another; the prisoner was employed by them. In consequence of
missing property, I directed Barker to watch—this copper pipe is my masters'—the prisoner bad no business with it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What value is it? A. Half-a crown—it would not be the sort of thing to mend a shoot—it could not be used to mend one—our shoots are made of tin, not copper.
EDWARD BARKER . I was desired to watch—I watched four days behind some casks—on the 18th I saw the prisoner come and take some pipe off the press, and take it as far as the door—he put it down by the door and walked into the middle of the yard, saw it was all clear, came in, took up the pipe, and went into a stoke-hole, at one corner of the yard—I followed, and heard him beating it up with a hammer—it was quite straight when he took it—when I came in sight he put it under the furnace belonging to the steam-boiler—I thanked him for coming, as I was tired of waiting for him—he asked what I meant—I said I had been watching the copper pipe which he had taken, and asked him where it was—he said under the dust-hole, and showed me where it was—he asked me to see if I could not rub it up with his master on his behalf—I said I could not.
Cross-examined. Q. He went to his work, did he not? A. Yes, and when his time was up he was taken into custody—he did not escape from the premises—he had been in the employ nearly four years.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined Three Months.

WILLIAM BURTON ( police-sergeant V 2.) I took the prisoner—she produced these two brooches from her bosom—this calico was in her basket, in her hand, and these other things were about her person. GUILTY Aged 27.— Confined Six Months.

1697. DAVID TAYLOR was indicted for embezzlement.
FRANCIS SALMON . I am a baker, and live at Rotherhithe. The prisoner was in my service—it was his duty to receive money for me, and pay it the same day—he has only paid me 1 l. 5 s. 6 d. out of 1 l. 9 s. 6 d., received from Eliza Westwood—it was his duty to pay me the whole.
ELIZA WESTWOOD . I am the daughter of Mrs. Julph; we deal with the prosecutor. On the 5th of April I paid him 1 l. 9 s. 6 d.,—I have his signature in my book, which be signed at the time—he did not sign the bill—he brought no bill till the Thursday following—we never could get the bills of him.
Prisoner's Defence. The money was paid me on the Monday, and was not put down till the Friday—the customers pay by instalments, very iregularly, and, in the hurry of business, this was not entered in the book
till the next day—the prosecutor might forget what I paid him—I am sure I cannot say now whether I paid him 1 l. 9 s. 6 d. or 1 l. 5 s. 6 d.—I had several accounts in my pocket.
MR. SALMON re-examined. I asked him about it last Friday—I did not know of it before, on account of his not having delivered the bills—he said it looked very black on his side, and therefore he must pay it—there have been mistakes made before—I never saw the customers' books.
Prisoner. On the Saturday, when he discharged me, he knew of the mistake—he said he supposed it was a mistake, and if I wanted a character I could have one—he stopped my wages for an article I was to pay for by instalments. Witness. I stopped his wages, but I did not stop this it—I have my cash-book here in which the entry is made—there are no dates to it—I never keep dates, they are on the bills.
JURY to " ELIZA WESTWOOD. Q. You said there was a receipt to the book; there is not. A. He never put down his name, and never would—we asked him, and he said it was of no consequence, it would all be right.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1698]
1698. WILLIAM WADELAKE was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of March, 1 gelding, price 4 l., the property of William Davis; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM DAVIS. I am a cab proprietor, living in Plough-yard, Carey-street, Lincoln's-inn-fields. On the 15th of March the prisoner hired a cab and grey gelding worth 4 l. of me for the night—it was a cab night horse—we let them out at so much per night—he paid me for it before he took it out—he brought it home the next morning—he then paid me 4 s. 6 d. towards the next night—he took the hone out next night, and never returned with it or the cab —I found the cab at the green-yard on the 18th, and the horse at Mr. Gedding's, the horse-slaughterer's—the horse was kept out all day, and turned into the pound for two days without any corn.
Prisoner. I hired the horse and cab of Mr. Davis for 5 s. a night—I paid him 4 s. 6 d., and with the other 6 d. I bought a nose-bag of him—he cannot swear I took the horse out on the 17th. Witness. He paid me the money for the horse in the morning, and when I came home in the evening the "d and horse were gone—he never called to ask for his money or license, without which he could not get employment in the line elsewhere.
THOMAS MOORE . I am in the employ of Mr. Geddings, ahorse-sl terer. Between ten and eleven o'clock on the morning of the 17th of March I met the prisoner by the Cobourg Theatre, with the horse—he asked me 30 s. for it—it was not in the cab —he brought it to our premises—the prosecutor owned it on the 18th—I told him he had better take it—he paid 27 s. for it—the horse has died since—it was very poor.
THOMAS LANGTON . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner—I was in plain clothes—I called him out of the Southampton Arms, and told him I wanted him for Mr. Davis's horse—he said, "It is no use keeping it in'; you are a police-constable, I suppose?"—I said I was—I said, "You stole a horse from Mr. Davis, did you not?"—he said, "I sold the horse to Mr. Geddings, the horse-slaughterer."
Prisoner's Defence. I have sold horses to Mr. Geddings for ten years; I did not know this was Mr. Davis's horse; I told him I was sent to sell
the horse, and I might have sold a horse belonging to Mr. Davis; I did not know it was his at the time I was selling it.
GEORGE HIDE . I am a City police-constable. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—he is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Transported for Ten Tears.

1699. SAMUEL GIBBONS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of May, 25lbs. weight of brass, value 1 l.; and 2 metal cocks, value 4 s.; the goods of Thomas Lambert and another, his masters.
THOMAS LAMBERT . I am in partnership with one other person in Chalcroft-terrace, Lambeth. I have lost a quantity of brass and two metal cocks lately—to the best of my belief the brass and metal cocks produced are mine—I have no doubt whatever of it—I know some of the articles were made by us, and have never been sold—they must have been removed off the premises by some person—the prisoner hat been in our employ for three years up to the present time.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. I believe you have a number of workmen? A. Between thirty and forty—here are several articles which I know were made by us—the whole produced are worth about 20 s.—I do not apprehend they could possibly be taken at the same time—I do not say it is impossible.
SAMUEL HARRIS . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody—he gave his address—I went to his house and found the property produced.
MR. BALLANTINE called
SAMUEL WEBB . I was in the brass line—the prisoner was at that time my partner. We have had patterns of all these description of cocks—when I left the business of gas-fitting the prisoner bad part of the stock—it was about 1 cwt. at that time—I believe a great many of these articles are part of that stock.
COURT. Q. How long is it since you separated t A. Between four and five years—the things I had were similar to these, all odds and ends—during the time we were partners he conducted himself honestly.
MR. LAMBERT re-examined. One of the things the witness speaks to is an article which is an invention of my own, and has not been in existence three years—the value of this is about 4 s.—if that alone had been found on his premises I should have charged him with stealing it (The prisoner received a good character.)

1700. MICHAEL was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of March, 1 candlestick, value 1 l., the goods of John William Church; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN WILLIAM CHURCH . I am a retailer of beer, and live in Penton-street, Newington. On Saturday afternoon, the 12th of March, about three o'clock, the prisoner was in my house—I saw him go into the kitchen, deliberately take a candlestick off the shelf, and conceal it under his jacket—I immediately collared him—the policeman came up, and I gave him in charge—this is my candlestick.
Prisoner. I picked it up in the skittle-ground, where it was lying—I not capable of going about. Witness, I have no skittle-ground.
Prisoner. I do not know what he may call it, I have been in it a great many times.

1701. MARY BRITTEN was indicted for feloniously receiving, of an evil-disposed person, on the 7th of May, 168 pence, 816 halfpence, and 240 farthings, the monies of Thomas Beardshaw, well knowing them to have been stolen.
THOMAS BEARDSHAW . I am a chandler, and live in Gravel-lane—on Friday night, the 7th of May, I went to bed about ten o'clock, and the next morning, a little before four I got up—when I got to the bottom of the stairs I saw a light—I found a wooden pane removed—I went into that shop and saw nothing amiss—I returned into my room and all my money was gone, which was 4 l. 10 s. in 5 s. packets of pence, half-pence, and farthings—I can swear to a portion of the money by the tying up and the paper—this is the parcel—the papers were old sugar bags, and they are wrapped up in a slovenly manner—I did it on Friday night.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Do you often tie up your parcels in this way? A. Yes—I take a good deal of copper—I keep do account—I put them up every other day—if any body comes in for change I have plenty in my till to give without these parcels—if people come for change I give all silver, no copper—they will not take it—I swear these are the parcels I lost—they were safe on the 7th.
JOHANNA MAHONET . I am servant to the prosecutor—I went to bed on Friday night at one o'clock—the place was all safe then—I observed the pane next morning—it was big enough for a boy to get through—t woman could not get in—I could not get in myself.
JOHN MILLS . I am a policeman. Mr. Beardshaw called me a few minutes after four o'clock on Saturday morning, and gave me information—I examined the place—I am positive no woman could have got over the wall—it is a back yard where they got in from the adjoining yard—a boy could get in at the bole, but it was not big enough for any body else—when I was coining off duty at six I saw the prisoner coming out at the door of a coffee-shop which is about 200 yards from the prosecutor's house—a boy was with her—they seemed confused—the boy went one way, and the woman another—I asked the prisoner what she had in her basket she said she had nothing—I told her I thought she had—she said, "Yes, I have got some money in it; I am going to market with it"—I said, "Let me look at it; you are the very person I want "—she said, "That money I found in the Grove"—I took her to the station—I found nine packages of 5 s. papers, and two broken ones which I produce.
Cross-examined. Q. Where is the Grove? A. About three minutes walk from the coffee-shop—it is a very lonesome place—the robbery happened on my beat—it is a beat that requires two of our men to go round of a night—it was the prisoner's son that came out of the coffee-shop with her—I have always said that since the first examination—I did not know it then—he is between 16 and 17 years old.
Q. You say in your deposition, "I know the boy by name—the same name as the prisoner's, but I did not know he was the prisoner's son.
A. What I swore was that his name was Britten, but when I took the prisoner I did not know who she was.
THOMAS BEARDSHAW re-examined. I am not very particular in tying up my papers—I can swear these are the papers I tied up—I can sweat to the wrapping up and folding and to the old sugar paper—there is not another tradesman in London that wraps up theirs in this way—here is 4 s. worth of half-pence, and 1 s. worth of farthings in this paper—it is what I put in some of mine as I cannot get rid of my farthings in that neighbourhood.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18420509-1702]
1702. DAVID THOMAS was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of May, 1 funnel, value 1 l., the goods of Willam Carter.
JOHN COLLIS . I am seaman on board the Courier, lying at Windle-wharf—I had this copper funnel safe at nine o'clock in our ship. About ten minutes past nine, as I was going to bed, the prisoner came on board—I said, "Holloa"—he said, "All right," and went on shore—I watched him on shore—he then stood and looked at me—he had nothing with him—I went below—I thought he had been after something, and while I went down to dress myself the prisoner came on board again, and raised the funnel up—I went up and ran after him, and he threw it into a barge.
Prisoner. I never had the funnel in my hand. Witness. I am sure he is the man—he came back a third time to fetch the funnel, thinking I did not see him throw it down, and he tried to break into the cabin—I caught him in East-street, and he had 7lbs. of copper nails on him, which are taken out of ships' bottoms—the funnel belongs to William Carter.
Prisoner. I went down to look for work, and as I was coming away the man ran after me and asked me about his funnel; I was never aservaut t of the docks all the time.
GUILTY . † Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18420509-1703]
1703. ALEXANDER BEAN and WILLIAM GINBBY were Indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 10 pockets of hops, value 10 l.; the goods of William Dunk and another.—2nd COUNT, calling them 1,500lbs. weight of hops.
MESSRS. RYLAND and BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN PAYNE . I am servant to Messrs. William and Albert Dunk, hop-factors and warehousemen, in Compter-street, Borough. Previous to the 26th of March they had ten pockets of bops—on the evening of that day they were missed—previous to missing them we had taken these samplet—in consequence of the loss we gave directions to have them advertised—the pockets had the Excise marks on them—four of them were marked "Richard Stillman," fire were marked "Thomas Haward Peasmark," and one "Thomas Back Westwood, East-end, 1841"—those pockets had the Excise weight on them—ten pockets of hops have since been produced to me by the police—I believe the hops contained in those tea pockets to be the very hops we lost—they have been weighed, and there is 29lbs. weight difference in the ten pockets—the Excise weight on the ten pockets we lost was "15cwt. 2qrs. 22lbs.," and the weight of those produced to me by the policeman was 15cwt. 3qrs. 22lbs., which is above
the Excise weight marked on the pockets—the Excise weight on Pye's pockets was much heavier than on those we lost—nine of the pockets produced by the police were marked "William Pye, Cuxton, Kent, 1841," and the other, "L. Starn, of Leigh, Kent, 1841"—the hops bear no resemblance to Pye's or Starn's.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Where were your hops kept? A. In my master's warehouse, in Whitecross-street, Union-street, Borough—it is not a very great thoroughfare—the last time I saw the hops was at a quarter before six o'clock, on the 26th of March, when I shut up—I shut up the premises myself—I missed them about a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes past ten the same night—I do not think I was asked the time before the Magistrate—Thomas Turner, one of Mr. Dunk's men, informed me of it—he is not here—he told me that the police told him the warehouse was open—it was as near ten as possible—I do not know what policeman it was that gave Turner the information—I do not think it was either of the witnesses—Turner said the policeman found it out when he went first round his beat after nine—( looking at a handbill)—this is our handbill.
