Q. This handbill states, that about nine o'clock the hops were taken; I suppose that is pretty accurate? A. That was the time they were seen to be taken away, twenty minutes before nine.
HENRY DRAPER . I am a brewer, in Oxford-street. I know the prisoner Bean—he came to my premises somewhere about Easter, I think—I cannot recollect the day of the month or week, or the month correctly—I thought it was about December or January—he asked me if I wished to purchase some hops—he had a sample with him, which I looked at—he did not name any price—I declined having anything to do with them at that time, it was such a broken sample—I saw him again some time after—in the mean time I had seen Mr. Evans, and had some conversation with him, and before I saw Bean again the hops were offered to me by a man named Clark—I suppose they were the same hops—Clark told me they were in Bean's possession—Mr. Evans was present when Bean called again—we then agreed for the price of them, 90 s.—I knew a little about them, but not much, and had gone to Mr. Evans to ascertain their value—I should say they were worth more than that—in my judgment they were worth 1 l. the cwt.—I agreed to take them at 90 s.—Bean agreed to deliver them at my brewery next morning, but in consequence of what Mr. Evans said a few minutes afterwards, I declined them—I went back to Bean, and said I would not take them under any consideration.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You were not before the Magistrate, I believe? A. No.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. When was it you first saw Bean? A. I cannot tell within a month—I should say it was a few months ago.
JAMES UNWIN . I am in the corn and coal trade. I produce a sample of hops which I received from the prisoner Ginbey on Monday, the 4th of April—I had seen Bean on the same day, before I had received the sample of hops, at his own house—he asked me if I knew any body in the corn trade who wished to buy a bargain—I did not know what he alluded to, and said I was open to buy a bargain myself—he did not, in the first instance, tell me what this bargain was to consist of, but he afterwards said it was five pockets of hops—he did not say whether he had more than five
pockets—this was about three o'clock in the afternoon, and it was half-past five, or six, when Ginbey brought the sample round to me.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You were not before the Magistrate, were you? A. Yes, I was examined, but my depositions were not taken.
ROBERT MENDHAM EVANS . I am a partner in the firm of Richards, Evans, and Co.—they are in the hop trade in the Borough. I remember having some communication with Mr. Draper about Bean—in consequence of that I met Bean in the presence of Mr. Draper—Mr. Draper introduced me to him—I saw a sample of hops that he produced—the value of the hops that he produced the sample of was about 5 l. 12 s. the price he agreed on for them was 90 s. I asked him where the hops were, and whether we could see them—I do not know whether he made any reply, or whether he said we could see them—he said he had advanced money on each of the pockets—a bargain was struck between him and Draper for the hops—he said he would deliver them at Mr. Draper's the next morning—Mr. Draper afterwards declined having them—I afterwards called at Bean's place, but could not see him—I made an appointment for him to meet me at seven o'clock next evening, at the Wenlock Arms, in the City-road—he kept the appointment—I asked him to deliver the hops at Bardell and Bowden's brewery next morning—I went next morning, and the hops were seized by the police—I did not see him on that occasion.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. What day was this when you first saw Bean? A. On the Thursday, the 31st of March, which was the day when I saw Mr. Draper about this.
JOHN HALLAM . I am a coal and coke dealer, and live in Short-street, New-cut. I have known the prisoner Bean, as a neighbour, for some time—he came to me sometime about last Easter, and asked if I had got any place for any kind of stowage—I said I had a loft I did not make much use of, in Burrows-mews, John-street, and I agreed to let him it at 3 s. a week—on Easter Sunday, I, for the first time, saw some hope in the left—no, I think it was on the Monday, as near as I can guess—I cannot read or write—it was on the Monday se'nnight, the Monday after Whit Sunday, I think—it was the Monday after they were put in my place on the Saturday—I remember Easter—it was about that time I let him the loft, on the Thursday or Friday, I will not be sure which—after some time, I did not like to have the hops there, and sent Sale, my man, to Bean to tell him to take them away, or else I should put them out in the street—I was not present when the hops were taken away—I missed them from the loft the same evening I sent to Bean—I cannot tell the day of the month—I cannot exactly say how long they had been on the premises—I suppose seven or eight days.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long have you known Bean? A. Nine or ten months—I know a gentleman named Callard—I do not know David Russell—I know the Crown—I was not there on the evening of the 28th of March with Callard—I have been to that public-house with Bean—I never heard any dealings between Bean and Callard there about any building materials—I recollect going into the Crown with Bean about nine or ten o'clock at night—I believe it was on the Saturday night—I do not know the day of the month, nor what Saturday it was.
THOMAS SALE . I am carman to Mr. Hallam. I know the loft he let to the prisoner Bean—I saw some pockets of hops in that loft two or three days
after they were brought in—I cannot exactly say what day it was—it was before Easter Sunday, I believe—I was in the premises on the Friday, and there were no hops there then, also on the Saturday evening after Good Friday, about seven o'clock, and there were no hops there then—I was there again on the Sunday morning, but I did not go up in the loft—I went into the loft two or three days afterwards—I cannot say what day it was, and the hops were there then—I cannot be certain whether it was on Good Friday that I had been in the loft and saw no hops—I believe it was after Easter Sunday that I saw the hops there, a day or so after—on a Monday after I had seen the hops in the loft my master sent me with a message to Bean—I should not think it was the next Monday after I had seen the hops—I did not keep an account how long it was afterwards—I said, "Please, Mr. Bean, master says if you don't take them things out of the loft, I am to bundle them out"—he said, "I will see about it by and bye"—he took me into a beer—shop, gave me a drop of beer, and told me to sit there—after I had the beer I went to his door and saw him—I did not see the prisoner Ginbey there then—I saw him about six or seven o'clock the same evening at Bean's—they were both together—I asked Bean if he was going to get them away—he said he did not know where he was to get a van—I said I would tell him where he could get one—he sent Ginbey with me—I went to three different places and could not get one—just after they left me I went to the top of the street, against the warehouse, I do not know the name of the street, I saw a man named Harris come by—he kept a van—neither of the prisoners were with me then—I took Harris to Mr. Bean's house—Bean and Ginbey were there—I introduced Harris to them as a man that could let them a van—Ginbey came out and spoke to him outside the door—I did not hear what was said—about an hour after I assisted in moving the hops from the loft into the van—I gave them out of the loft window—the prisoners were not present then—Harris came to the loft with the van, alone—I asked him if he was authorized to receive the hops, and Ginbey came into Bean's as I stood at the door, and said, "This is the man who is going to move them"—it was between eight and nine in the evening when we began to remove the hops—it was rather dark—they were loaded and sent away, and I saw no more of them.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Can you tell us what month it this year Easter Sunday was in? A. No, I cannot—I do not know whether it was January or July.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Ginbey was sent with you to find a van for Bean to send for the hops? A. Yes.
JOHN HARRIS . I am a carman, and live in Windmill-street, New-cut, Lambeth. I had some conversation with Sale on the 4th of April, about carrying away some hops—I saw him at the corner of Queen—street, Marlborough—street, John-street West, Blackfriars-road—he introduced me to both the prisoners—when I went to the door Bean did not seem to have any thing to say to me—Sale went in doors, and I staid out—after I had staid out a little while, Ginbey came outside to me, and asked me whether I thought it was too late to go over the water—I said no, not if it was all right with the landlord—he said it was all right—I then went with Sale, and got ray van—while I was standing at the door I heard Bean say to Ginbey that he thought they had better let it be till the 'morning, and afterwards he said, "Do as you like"—Ginbey then came out, and
Sale too—Sale said, "Harris, will you go?"—I said, "Yes"—Sale went with me, and harnessed the horse—I then went with him to Burrow's—mews—Sale drove the van, and I staid behind to take some ropes, as I did not know what it was I was going to take—I then took ten pockets of hops from the loft in Burrows—mews—neither of the prisoners assisted in the removal of them—as I was starting Ginbey came up and told me to go the water—he said, "I am almost afraid it is too late," and asked me whether I could put them any where for the night—I said there was the Running Horse, in Blackfriars-road, but that was not approved of—T afterwards went over Blackfriars-bridge to the corner of Faringdon-street—he then told me to drive to Old-street—road—I went there and he met me there again—he said he should run them in there, it was too late to go further—it was a livery—stable yard, the sign of the Leaping Bar—I took the horse out—he asked me whether I should take the horse home—I said "Yes," at first—he said, "Put him in, and give him a feed of corn," so I ran the; horse in, and agreed to meet him next morning—I was to meet him a quarter or twenty minutes before seven o'clock, which I did—I put the horse to—I then went with the hops to Mr. Bowden's brewhouse—I untied the ropes by Ginbey's direction—while doing that, he spoke to me—I did not hear what he said at first—he told me to pull in, and say I came from the Blackfriars—road, and then he suddenly went away and left me—I did not see any body approaching at the time he went away—no person came up immediately after to speak to me, not for half or three quarters of an hour—I saw the policeman in about ten minutes, but I was not aware he was a policeman when I first saw him—he was in plain clothes—he took me into custody, with the cart and hops, and locked me up for eight days.
TRUEMAN MACHIN . I keep the Leaping Bar livery stables, in Old-street-road. On the evening of the 4th of April, I saw the witness Harris and another—I cannot positively swear it was Ginbey—I am very doubtful whether he is the person, for I did not see him two minutes scarcely—they brought the van in, which contained what I considered to be hops, from the manner of the package—it stood under my gateway, close to the street, all night, and went away next morning—Harris went with it, and a person with him something similar to Ginbey—I think it was the same person I had seen over night with Harris, but I just saw a person with him and that was all—I would not swear positively it was Ginbey—he is very much like the man, but I have my doubts—they went away from my place rather before seven o'clock—the van was in the same state as when it came in over night.
JOHN WOODHOUSE ( police-sergeant M 13.) I received some informa tion on Monday evening, the 4th of April, in consequence of which I went to Wenlock-road, City-road, on Tuesday morning, and kept a watch on Messrs. Bowdens' brewery, about 200 yards from it, and within sight of the gates—I was in plain clothes—I have known Ginbey some time, and he knows me—I saw a van come up—Harris was with it, and another person whose face I did not see—I do not know whether either of them saw me—I continued standing there for half an hour after the van drove up—in the meantime one of the men went away—I went up, and took Harris into custody, with the van and hops—I took Bean into custody on Friday, the 11th—he was in company with Ginbey—they saw me, and both set off running—I caught Bean, and another constable who was with
me caught Ginbey—I told Bean I wanted him for receiving a quantity of hops—at that time I did not know whether he had stolen or received them—he said, "Very well, I know nothing about it."
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you acquainted with Bean? A. Not at all—I had been making inquiries about this matter—we had pretty good information where to go for the persons who stole these hops—I do not know whether it is the case, that persons who steal things are not the persons who are found with them—that depends upon circumstances—I told Bean I wanted him for receiving the hops—I had information that they were in his possession—I thought at the time that that was what he was wanted for—he was committed for receiving the hops—that was the charge before the Magistrate—I heard nothing about the charge of stealing them till I came here.
ROBERT STURGEON ( police-sergeant M 4.) I was with Woodhouse—we met the prisoners walking together—they both ran away—I ran after Ginbey, and took him—he said, "I did not mean to run away"—as I was taking him to the station, he said, "It is a very fine plant "—I suppose he meant that we had been very finely planted.
EDWARD LANDSDELL . I am in the service of Mr. Horton, a hopfactor, in the Borough. I assisted in getting these ten pockets of hops into the station-house, and then assisted in taking samples of them—I have since seen samples in Mr. Dunk's possession, which I have compared with those I assisted in taking, and in my judgment they are the same hops—I have been acquainted with hops twenty years.
THOMAS SEARING . I am warehouseman to Messrs. Daggett and Harrison, hop factors in the Borough. I have seen the ten pockets of cups in the possession of the police—nine of them are marked "Pye"—those pockets were sold by us to Messrs. Thackerer, with hops in them, on the 11th of October, or it might be a day or two before—I believe the hops now in those pockets are not the same hops which were in our possession—we receive Pye's growth—he is a factor.
GEORGE EDWARD BROWN . I am warehouseman to Messrs. Thackerer and Co., hop merchants. It is my duty to superintend the weighing and delivering of the hops they sell—on the 22nd of February last they sold some pockets of hops to Messrs. Taylor and Co., the brewers—I weighed them, superintended the delivery of them, and another man marked them—I saw that they were accurately marked—Mr. Deeley saw them weighed—there were eighty-two pockets marked "Pye," and sixteen marked "Starn"—I have since seen nine pockets produced by the police, marked "Pye," and one marked "Starn," which I can say were part of those we delivered to Messrs. Taylor—I have compared the marks and the weight, and am perfectly satisfied they are the same.
JOHN DEELEY . I am in the service of Messrs. Taylor, the brewers. In February last they purchased of Messrs. Thackerer and Co., eighty-two pockets of hops, marked "Pye," and sixteen marked "L. Starn"—I saw them weighed—they were delivered at Messrs. Taylor's, and their contents used in the way of the business—the covers are my father's perquisites—somewhere about Easter, I cannot recollect the time exactly, I sold fifteen of those covers to the prisoners—I am not so positive to Bean as I am to Ginbey, but I believe he was the other—Bean came up to me, and asked if I had got any pocketing—I said I was not positive, but I would send one of the men to see—one of the men went up—I was not aware
that Ginbey went with him, but in about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour Ginbey came down, and said those pockets would not do, they were not long enough—I said some of them were tucked in straining them, perhaps if he pulled them out he would find them long enough, if not, I could not help it, it was of no consequence whether he had them or not—Bean said, "Perhaps they will do"—I then went up with them—they examined what pocketing we had, and Ginbey, in Bean's presence, selected fifteen—I believe some of Pye's were among them, but I am not positive—I did not take particular notice of the marks—I have since seen nine pockets marked "Pye," and one marked "L. Starn"—I should say those pockets were in our possession when we bought the hops of Messrs. Thackere—Bean paid for them.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. I thought you said you would not swear Bean was the man? A. I believe he is, but I would not swear it—the man who was with Ginbey paid me, not Ginbey—I believe these pockets were disposed of this year, as there is "1841" upon them—we could not have had them last year.
JOHN VINCENT . I am in Messrs. Taylor's service. I assisted in looking out fifteen pockets—I cannot tell whether nine of them were Pye's—Ginbey was very particular in wanting them of the year 1841—he was by himself then, when he went up into the loft along with me—I saw the person who was with him below, and believe it was Bean.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You will not swear it was Bean? A. I cannot swear it rightly.
MRS. EVERETT. I keep a beer-shop in Marlborough-street, Blackfriars-road. About two months ago, as near as I can recollect, Bean left a sack with me, saying it was not his, it belonged to another man—this looks like the sack—it was in the same state on my premises till the officer took it.
JOHN WOODHOUSE re-examined. I found this sack, and produce its contents, which is what is called straining tackle.
WILLIAM DUNK . I am hop factor in the Borough. The hops stolen were in my custody—I have examined the samples taken out of these ten pockets, and have compared them with the hops now at the station—I have no doubt they are my property—I believe the sample of hops produced by Unwin to be a part of my hops—they exactly correspond with one part of them.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How many partners have you? A. Only my brother—I speak to the hops from their similarity—they correponds exactly—I think hops are very easy of identity—this sample—( opening one)—has two sorts of hops in it—it is what we call false packed, consisting partly of fine hops, and partly of brown hops—they were perhaps gathered from two gardens, not having sufficient in one to make a full pocket—it is a very unusual thing to have brown hops with fine—I do not know whether it is unusual to have the growth of two gardens in the same pocket—my relations are all hop-growers.
COURT. Q. Experienced dealers in hops can tell the difference of growth by the mere feel, can they not, without looking at the sample? A. They can—they could tell one from the other if they were bindfold.
MR. PHILLIPS called
JOHN CALLARD . I am a carpenter, and live at No. 61, Granby-street, Waterloo-road. I know the prisoner Bean—I remember seeing him at the Crown, in the New-cut, on Saturday evening, the 26th of March, between eight and nine o'clock—I saw him earlier than that—I was at work opposite his house the whole of the day, and saw him outside his house selling goods—he is a baker, and lives at the corner of Short-street, Newcut—I saw him at his house in his business from nine in the morning till nine at night, on and off—it was between eight and nine when I went to the Crown—I was repairing the front of my father's house—I went with him to the Crown—it is a very short distance from his shop, only two or three minutes' walk—a person named David Russell was at the Crown—he and I were both at work together at my father's house, and he went with Bean and me to the public-house—between seven and eight o'clock, I and Russell were purchasing two corn measures outside Mr. Bean's shop—I saw he was at home at that time, and after I had finished my work I accompanied him to the public-house—we remained in the public-house about an hour—I parted with him outside the public-house door a little after nine—I do not know how far that is from Whitecross-street, Borough.
J. WOODHOUSE re-examined. I should say it is within half a mile.
MR. RYLAND to JOHN CALLARD. Q. Are you a master carpenter? A. Yes; I do business for myself—I keep a shop—I have journeymen sometimes, but not always—I have none now, nor had I any at the time in question—I had a shop at that time, at No. 61, Granby-street—I have known Bean about twelve months—I have not been at all intimate with him—I never went to a public-house with him before this Saturday evening, neither to the Crown or any other.
Q. What makes you recollect that this was Saturday evening, the 26th of March? A. I recollect its being the Saturday after Good Friday—I went with him to the public-house between eight and nine o'clock—I did not say that I saw him at his own premises till nine o'clock—I saw him at his own house as late as between seven and eight, about eight I should say.
Q. On your oath, what was the latest time in the evening that you saw him in his own house? A. Between seven and eight—it was about eight or nine that I went with him to the public-house—I cannot say exactly to the time—it was between eight and nine—I did not lose sight of him from between seven and eight till between eight and nine—he was outside his shop during that time—I went from his shop to the Crown—I cannot recollect exactly the time—I was with him outside his shop from between seven and eight till between eight and nine—it was between seven and eight that I bought the measures of him—we then went to the Crown—I left the measures at my father's house—I was not there a minute.
Q. What were you doing from between seven and eight till between eight and nine? A. Standing outside Bean's shop talking—Russell was with me the whole of the time, talking outside Bean's shop—we took notice of the time by the Crown clock—it was between eight and nine when we went there, I cannot say exactly—I looked at the clock when I went in, to see what o'clock it was—I left a little after nine—I did not look at
the clock when I came away, but, from the time we were there, I should say it was after nine—I think we were there about an hour—I had never used the Crown before—I have been there before, but not often—I cannot say how often, I should say from twenty to a hundred times—I have lived in the neighbourhood all my lifetime, and have been backwards and forwards to the house—I am well known there—a person named Essy, I believe, keeps it now—Maber did keep it—Essy kept it at this time—I cannot say how long he has been there—my father was at the public-house with me that night—he went with me, and Russell, and the prisoner—we all four went together—a bricklayer's son, named George Russell, I believe, also went with us, nobody else—I cannot say whether I saw the landlord that night—the bar-maid served us—she did not know me before—I had seen her before—I cannot say whether she had seen me—I believe she is not here, nor my father, nor George Russell, nor the landlord—I had drank there before—I cannot say whether ft was the same barmaid that served me before.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you ever charged with any offence in your life? A. Never—I cannot say whether Bean knew the landlord, or the landlord him—I have not been to the house since, that I recollect.
(William Fish, builder and publican, Islington-market; Dennis Cox, tailor, Little Russell-street, Bloomsbury; Anthony Thacker, coal-merchant, No. 8, Charles-square, City-road; James Lewson, butcher, Tower-street; William Perry, clock-maker, No. 7, Clarence-place, Penton-street, Walworth; J. Parraquay, publican, Castle-street, Leicester-square; and Robert Faulkner, cow-keeper, No. 18, Salisbury-street, New North-road; deposed to Bean's good character.)
BEAN— GUILTY . Aged 29.
GINBEY *— GUILTY . Aged 24.
Transported for Seven Years.
Before Lord Chief Justice Denman.
[trialAccount t18420509-1705]
1705. DANIEL GOOD was indicted for the wilful murder of Jane Good, alias Jane Jones. —Other COUNTS stating it to be a person whose name is unknown.
MR. ATTORNEY-GENERAL, with MESSRS. ADOLPHUS, WADDINGTON, and
GURNEY, conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM GARDINER ( police-constable V 279.) On the 6th of April I was at Wandsworth, and in consequence of some charge by a tradesman I went to the house of Mr. Shiell, in Putney, Park-lane, inquired for somebody, and was directed to the stable in consequence of my inquiry—the stable is about 200 yards or more from the dwelling-house—when I came there I saw the prisoner—he answered the ring—I was accompanied by two lads, Speed and Dagnal—on his answering the bell one of them asked him if the coachman was come home—he said, "I am the coachman"—(he is the coachman of that family I now know)—I was standing a little on one side—I then stepped up to him, and told him I had come to take him into custody on a charge of stealing a pair of black trowsers from Mr. Collingbourn—he said, "Yes, I bought a pair of breeches of Mr. Collingbourn, and I did not pay him for them;" and pulling out his purse, he said, "You can take the money to him"—I said, "No, that has nothing to do with the charge I have come upon"—he said, "Very well, then I will go with you to Wandsworth, and settle with Mr. Collingbourn"—I asked
him if he would allow me to examine the chaise and the stable, previous to going to Wandsworth, to search for the trowsers—it was a four-wheeled chaise—he said, "Yes"—I and the two lads then walked into the stable-yard—I saw a little boy about ten years old running about, who I have since ascertained is the prisoner's child, called Daniel Good—I desired the elder lad Speed to stay by the prisoner while I examined the chaise and stable—we were then in the stable-yard—I proceeded to search the harness-room and the stable—while searching the harness-room the prisoner walked over towards the chaise—he said, "We will go to Wandsworth now and settle it"—at that moment a man, who I have since ascertained is Houghton, the gardener, living on the premises, came into the yard, and asked what was the matter—I told him I had come to take the prisoner, and was searching for the trowsers—he seemed much surprised—I told the prisoner I would not go till I had searched this stable—he was standing at the stable-door with the door just open, ajar—he then allowed me to go into the stable—he had not prevented me, but stood at the door as if he wished me not to go in—I went in with the two lads, Houghton, the prisoner, and his child—when I got in I desired Speed again to stand by the prisoner—I commenced, and searched the first and second stalls of the stable, found nothing, crossed over to search the corn-bin, and while searching the bin the prisoner said, "Let us go to Wandsworth, and have it settled"—I said, "No, I will not go till I have made a more diligent search"—the prisoner then walked down to the fourth stall himself—there are four stalls all in a row—I saw him move a truss of hay from one side of the stall to the other—I followed him down—there was nobody in that stall—I went to it, and desired him to go and stand by Speed, saying I did not require any of his assistance—I then moved the hay from where he had put it—it was in the direction he had moved it to, but there were several trusses there—I cannot swear it was the same truss—I moved two trusses and some loose hay which was underneath, and discovered something laying, which appeared to me to be a goose—it was a piece of flesh—I said, "What is this? this is a goose" loud enough for every body in the stable to hear—at that moment I heard the stable-door shut, looked round, and the prisoner was gone—I ran to the door, tried to open it, and found it was fastened outside—Speed found a two-pronged hay-fork in the stable, and commenced prizing the door—I assisted him, but could not open it—I went back to be positive as to what I had seen in the stall—the two lads and Houghton followed me—I moved the remainder of the hay off, and thought it was a pig—I found it was the trunk of a female, the body—it was laying on its abdomen with the back uppermost—Speed turned it over—I had no difficulty then in discerning that it was a woman—the arms were cut off at the shoulders, and the legs cut off at the upper part of the thigh; the head severed from the body at the lower part of the neck, and the thigh-bone was taken off right at the hip—it was quite gone—the belly was cut open, and the bowels taken out—I then went to the door, and succeeded in breaking out—the head and all the four limbs were gone—I made a further search an hour and a half after—(I had not left the stable)—I searched in a third stall with another constable, removed several trusses of straw, and under it found a mattress rolled up, and two blankets rolled up in it—I unfolded the mattress, and discovered a large stain on it, apparently of urine—it was not wet—I saw some blood close to where
the body was laying—there was very little, and it appeared to be dry—I left the mattress and things in charge of the police—they have since been burnt.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did Speed go with you from Wandsworth? A. Yes—I knew him before—Dagnal is in Mr. Collingbourn's employ—it was a quarter to nine o'clock when I left Wandsworth—I got to the stable about half-past nine—it is about two miles from Wandsworth—I examined the stable by my lamp which I had—the blood in the fourth stall, where I found the body, looked dry—I did not make any further inquiry about it—I had not left the stable before I found the mattress—I stopped there till about one o'clock—I left sergeant Palmer and Tye, a policeman, there then.
COURT. Q. What quantity of blood do you suppose there was? A. I cannot exactly say—it was about as large as that paper ( half a sheet offoolscap)—it was in the straw—it began eight or ten inches from the body, not under it.
JURY. Q. It was on the ground? A. On the hay and litter, in the straw—I removed that, and saw the stain on the bricks—they were a lighter colour than the part which was stained—I could see plainly where the blood was—there was no appearance of the blood having run over the rest of the bricks—there was no stream of blood—there was some loose hay laying in the stall, not as if a horse had been there—it was loose hay from the truss—there is no hay-loft there—you go out of the stable to get to the loft.
ROBERT SPEED . I am eighteen years old, and live at Wandsworth. On Wednesday, the 6th of April, I went with Gardiner to Mr. Shiell's, at Roehampton-lane—we went to the house first, and afterwards to the stable, to a small private gate—I rang the bell—the prisoner answered it, and the young woman, the daughter of the gardener—they came to the gate—when we were at the gate, Gardiner told the prisoner he had come for a pair of trowsers he had stolen from Mr. Collingbourn—he said he had no black trowsers—when we got inside the premises Gardiner told me to take care of the prisoner—we were then inside the premises, not far from the stable—we afterwards went into the stable—the prisoner went first—I saw him open the stable-door with a key—I saw him turn the key which he produced from his pocket before he got to the door—I saw him turn the key—we all went in, and I kept near him—Gardiner commenced searching, and after searching some time the prisoner went into the place where the body was found—Gardiner told him to stand back—before that, I saw the prisoner begin taking the hay from one side of the stall, and putting it over where the body lay—it was on that Gardiner told him to stand back, he must make a more diligent search—he stood back nearer to me—we were about two yards from the stall Gardiner was searching—while Gardener was searching he said, "What is this?" loud enough for the prisoner to hear—I stepped forward to see what it was, and the prisoner rushed back towards the stable-door to escape—I saw him as he had the door in his hand going out, and immediately heard the door locked—I heard the sound of the key turning—I afterwards saw the body.
COURT. Q. You broke the door open afterwards? A. Yes, and found the key was gone.
THOMAS HOUGHTON . On the 6th of April I was gardener to Mr.
Shiell, at Putney—I went into the stable-yard that evening, and found the prisoner and policeman there—we afterwards went into the stable—I did not hear the policeman make an exclamation—the prisoner made his escape, I believe, without any one seeing him—I afterwards went into the harness-room, which was in the prisoner's care—no one else had access to it—I cannot tell how it was entered that night, as the policeman had examined it before I came—the prisoner kept the key of it, and lighted the fire in it—about six o'clock on the Tuesday morning, the day before the policeman came, I observed a smoke coming from the harness-room—it was common to have a fire as early as that, and earlier in the winter—I had not seen a fire there for perhaps more than a fortnight, I think.
Q. In what state was the stove in the harness-room on the Wednesday evening? A. There was an immense preparation of wood and coals with straw underneath ready to light—it was very considerably more than there would usually be for a fire—I should consider there were three times as much as would answer for a common fire.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you been in the harness-room before? A. I had never been there for months before—I have seen a fire there through the window, but do not know the time I was in the harness-room or stable, for the prisoner and I have not been friends for some time—in fact I never went near him at all—one window of the harness-room looks into our kitchen-garden, and the other, which is opposite, into the stable-yard—there are two windows—I am in the kitchen-garden every day, generally from six o'clock in the morning, at the time in question, and had been so for the last three or four days—I work till six in the evening, but as I live on the premises, I do not go away often—I live at a cottage on the farm connected with the premises, and with the kitchen-garden—I should think my cottage is not more than fifty or sixty yards from the stable—my wife, daughter, and son, live with me—my wife, daughter, and myself, sleep in the cottage—my son has slept in a room over the back of the stable for some time past—the bed-rooms all join together over the stable, but are separated by a partition—nobody but my son sleeps in the bed-room over the stable—the prisoner sleeps up at Mr. Shiell's house, and boards there—it is a quarter of a mile from the stable—my wife and daughter sleep about fifty yards from the stable—it is one range of buildings—a cow-house, a tool-house, seed-room, fruit-room, then the stable—there is no communication without going out. side—you cannot go into the stable without going into the stable-yard—you can get to the stable-yard from the kitchen-garden, by coming into a passage, without coming into the highway—that yard leads to the stable in question—when we have no hot-house fires to attend to, we generally go to bed about ten, or half-past—my daughter is nearly thirty years old—she sometimes retires to rest before me, and sometimes not—I went to bed on the Sunday night before this about ten, or a little after, my wife and daughter about the same time—I got up at six—my son is under-gardener, to assist me—I sometimes have occasion to go into the stable-yard—there is an iron gate, separating the stable-yard from the garden and other premises, which any body can open.
Q. The stable-door, which was locked, I presume is the door leading out to the highway? A. No; there is a colonnade over, and a stable on each side—the door opens under the colonnade—there are large gates for the carriage to go out, they lead to an open yard, and the stables are right and left—the doors facing each other—I do not know when I have seen
the stable in question unlocked, except the prisoner has been there himself—I have not been there much latterly—he has been in Mr. Shiell's service about two years and three months—master keeps two ponies, that is all.
Q. How does your son go to bed over the stable? A. There is a door opens close by the harness-room which leads up stairs, to what we call the outer door—it turns to the left when you get to the top of the stairs, and this door faces him—there are two rooms beyond where he sleeps, and one on the right of him, where the stableman used to sleep—my son's room is part over the back harness-room, where the dirty harness is kept—his window looks into the garden—he would come along the garden into the stable-yard to go to bed, and by the harness-room—it is an open staircase—I have two men and a boy besides my son—they do not sleep on the premises—a fire is necessary in the harness-room in wet weather, to dry the harness.
COURT. Q. The two harness-rooms are on the ground-floor? A. Yes, my son sleeps over the back one—that is not where I saw this great preparation for a fire—that was in the large harness-room—the prisoner locks the harness-room up, and always keeps the key—you do not go through the stable to it—it is a separate door on the left of the stable, and very near the stable—when in the stable, you do not come into the yard to get to it, but into the colonnade.
Q. Could any body in the stable-yard see any thing which is going on in the colonnade? A. They can, if they were to look into the harnessroom itself, through the large window, any body who was in the stable-yard could—there is no curtain to the window that I know of, to either of the windows which face the garden—the glass is frosted, that people should not look in out of the garden—no body in the garden can see through, unless they get steps or something, and look through the upper panes, which are not frosted—they are rather higher than a man's head—any body can see into the harness-room from the stable-yard.
SAMUEL SMITH DAONAL . I am in the service of James Collingbourn, a pawnbroker, at Wandsworth. On the Wednesday night in question, I was in Mr. Shiell's stables—Gardiner said there was a goose—the prisoner then went out of the stable—I did not see him go out—as I was holding Gardiner's lamp, I heard Houghton say he was gone, and when I looked round, he was gone—the door was locked—I tried to get out—we could not, but did a short time afterwards—Houghton's son was not there at that time.
SAMUEL PALMER ( police-constable V 6.) On the night of the 6th of April I was on duty at Putney, about a mile and a quarter from Mr. Shiell's house, and about a mile from the stable—I went to the stable a few minutes to eleven o'clock that night, or I think it must have been eleven—I saw the remains of a human body there—Gardiner, Hougbton and his son, and Speed, were there—I assisted in searching the premises—I searched the stall adjoining where the trunk of the body was—I found a mattress and two blankets tied up. with a small cord—I found nothing else in the stable—I then went into the harness-room with Inspector Busain—the door was open when I went there—I found some pieces of bone in the fire-place, which is nearly opposite the door which is in one corner of the room—there is one window on each side of the fire-place, but the fire-place is nearer the front than the back—the windows are on opposite sides oÂ£ the room—one looks into the stable-yard, and the other
into the garden—the fire was laid—there were pieces of wood, coal, and straw, a great quantity more than was necessary for any common fire—I have the remains of the bones which I found, here—they were among the ashes where the fire was laid—it is a kind of pan—they were under the fire, among the ashes—they are now in the same state as when I found them—when I found the first piece I took it out to a medical man who stood outside—a smell similar to an animal substance burning caused me to look into the fire-place—I found in the harness-room a parcel of keys tied with a kind of guard—they are in the possession of Tee—I took them from the harness-room to go up to the house to try to open two cupboards in the prisoner's bed-room, but neither of them would fit, and I forced them open—the keys either dropped out of my pocket, or I took them out in the next stable—I did not bring them away from the premises—these now produced are them—I have no doubt of them.
ROBERT TEE . I am a constable of Roehampton. I went to Mr. Shiell's stables on Thursday, the 7th of April, and saw in one of the stalls the body of a woman—I found these keys on the premises, and brought them away, and have brought them here to-day—I found them on the corn-bin in the stable opposite the stable the body was found in, not in the same stable—I have had them in my possession ever since—the body was buried on the next day week.
Cross-examined. Q. It was buried on the 15th? A. Yes—the keys laid on the lid of the bin, rolled up, with this guard round them, not on the floor.
JOSIAH TYE ( police-constable V 199.) On Wednesday night, the 6th of April, I was on duty at Wandsworth, I went to Mr. Shiell's stables, and saw the body of a woman found—I found Gardiner there—on going into the harness-room I noticed a very bad, fulsome, sickly smell—I never smelt such before, but it appeared to me like rind of bacon—after stopping some time it was so bad I went out to the sergeant, and said, "Here is a very bad smell here, something must be wrong"—I searched the fire-place, and found two pieces of bone in the cinders among the coals in the furnace of the fire-place—I afterwards took them to the surgeon—on the 12th of April, the Tuesday following, I went there again, and searched the coach-house—I went to the four-wheeled chaise, and opened the gig-box lid, I saw nothing in the box, but smelt a very bad smell, like the smell in the room—after taking off the cushions, and taking the box out of the chaise, I found a small piece of flesh on the outside—it was so very small I could not keep it—I found it here—( pointing to the box)—I found another set of bones in the left-hand side of the furnace where the fire burns—I found them all at the same time, and in the furnace.
Cross-examined. Q. What you call a small piece of flesh, was so small you could not keep it, it was not hidden by the cushion? A. No, it was about half the size of a pea—I have kept the two sets of bones all together—I have a skull-bone—all I found that night are in this paper—I did not find bones in more places than one—Palmer was with me when I found them—he found the first piece in the cinder place—I found these in the grate where the fuel was laid—there was nothing burning then—we took out two pieces of skull bone directly to Mr. Allen, a medical gentleman, who was waiting there.
MR. ATTORNEY GENERAL. Q. Are these the two pieces? A. Yes.
JOHN BUSAIN . I am an inspector of the V division of police. I went to Mr. Shiell's stable on the night of the 6th of April—I arrived about a quarter past eleven o'clock—I went into the stable, and saw the trunk on a female, and proceeded to search the harness-room, the door of which was locked—I got the key from the elder Houghton—I unlocked the door—I beg to correct myself, I was called by the superintendent to go into the harness-room, which was then open—it was at a subsequent search I got the key—the superintendent ordered me to break open a flat wooden box, which was there, locked—I found in that a quantity of clean linen belonging to a male, and some books—I searched the drawer of a table there which was shot, but there was no lock on it—I found in it this knife closed as it is now—I examined it, and observed what appeared to me to be stains of blood—I found it had been newly sharpened, and on this part of the handle was what appeared to be a stain of blood, and the knife appeared to be rusty after it had been sharpened—the stain of blood was partly on the handle, and partly on the blade—I showed the knife to Dr. Ridge the next day—I found some keys in the drawer, which sergeant Palmer has possession of—I laid them on the table—on the right-hand side of the fire-place, in a corner, standing by the chimney, I found this axe—I did not examine it that night—I kept possession of the key of the harness-room myself—I looked at it, and could not distinguish whether there were stains on it that night—I locked the room when f left, and took the key with me—I observed next morning what appeared to be stains of blood, one mark on the pole of the axe, and one on each side the edge along here—I do not think they were more visible than they are now, because I have kept it so carefully—there were lines across the room to hang clothes on, and on a line next the fire-place, I found this piece of silk handkerchief, which appears to be marked with blood in several places—I found divers clothes, but not on the line—the articles of female wearing apparel I found in a saddle-rest in the harness-room—here is what appears to me to be part of a flannel petticoat, forcibly torn in two, and one part cut in the seam—these two pieces were together as they are now, and correspond exactly where it appears cut at the seam—they also correspond in quality; they were both together, thrown over the saddle-rest—I found what appears to be part of a calico petticoat, close to the saddle-rest, underneath a box-coat, which was thrown over the chair, and one of these was knotted as if tied together, but it became untied afterwards—both are marked with blood, and on one of these there are marks of blood close to where there have been pieces cut out—they are not drops but splashes of blood—not as if dropping from a person's nose—I found also a pint bottle, under the window; there were a few drops of liquid in it—I poured it into the palm of my hand, tasted it, and believe it to be milk—I found the bottle on the 8th, but I had always kept the harness-room locked up, and had the key—on the night of the 6th I assisted in searching the fire-place, and found a quantity of calcined bones in the ashes—the fire was laid with straw, wood, and coal—I removed them, and searched underneath—there was a pair of brand-irons at the fire-place, so as to bring the fire out further from the chimney—I believe it was a Cobbett's register-stove—I found the bones under the fuel, among the cinders and ashes underneath—I showed them to Mr. Allen the same night—
they are now in the same state, except one piece of a very claimed bone fell asunder—I put a string round it to keep it together—I found about an ounce of tea in a box, which was not locked—on the 8th, the day of the inquest, in sifting the cinders through two sieves, assisted by Dr. Ridge, among these at the bottom of the stove I found more bones—I examined the body that day after it was washed, having a very clear and good light, and on the neck, about half an inch below where the head had been taken off, I found a small mole, a very dark colour, on the right side—to make sure that it was a mole, I wetted my thumb, and rubbed it two or three times—I was quite convinced it was a mole—in the harness-room I found the two umbrellas, one hanging on a nail over the chimney-piece, the other standing in a corner, not far from the axe—I found this rent-book in searching No. 18, South-street, Manchester-square, and the landlord gave me this sign-board.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you kept that knife with equal care as the axe? A. I have—I do not think there is the slightest alteration in it since I found it.
JOHN HODGHTON . I am the son of Mr. Shiell's gardener, and was in his service the beginning of April. I slept in the room over between the stable and harness-room—part of the room is over each—I get to it by the stair-case outside—I do not pass through the stable—the prisoner kept the key of the stable—it was always kept locked—I got up on Tuesday, the 5th of April, at half-past six o'clock, 0. and saw the prisoner when I came down—I smelt a very unpleasant smell, and asked him if he had been singeing—it smelt like the singeing of horses—he said he had been in liquor overnight, and had been toasting some cheese for refreshment.
Q. Can you tell whether there was a fire in the harness-room at the time? A. I heard the crackling as if there was a fire, but did not see any fire—he told me at the time he had a fire drying the harness—I hare seen a little boy with the prisoner—he told me he was his son, and that a female had the care of him named Jane Jones, who he (the prisoner) used to call his sister—I have seen her at Putney three times with the prisoner—the last time I saw her there was on Easter Monday, about ten o'clock in the morning—she was looking out at the stable gates, which go down the lane—the prisoner did not then tell me who she was—he said nothing about her—he had previously told me who she was—I think she wore a blue bonnet on Easter Monday.
Cross-examined. Q. Was Easter Monday the last time you saw her? A. Yes—I have seen a little boy there with a female—I have seen Susan Butcher there—I am quite sure I have not seen any other females there—I have been examined before—I never saw any other female at the stable with the prisoner—I swear I have not seen Butcher or any other female there since Easter Monday—I went to bed at this time between nine and tea, or about ten o'clock—I went to bed on Sunday night about ten, and about the same time on Monday—I got up about six o'clock, or a few minutes after on Monday—the prisoner and I were on terms as fellow servants; not good friends particularly—I was working in the kitchen garden on Monday morning—it lies at the back of the stable—the room I slept in was partly over the stable where the body was found—the prisoner slept at the house.
COURT. Q. There are two harness-rooms, which do you sleep over? They arc adjoining—I sleep over the back one—not where the fire was
—the harness-room has a lath and plaster ceiling—I have seen Susan Butcher there four or five times, in the daytime, about the afternoon—I have not seen her there at dusk or later, nor very early in the morning—I had heard no noise, or any thing during the Monday night—I heard the crackling when I came by the harness-room, as I came down in the morning.
ALFRED BENJAMIN ALLEN . I am assistant to Mr. Shillito, a surgeon. I was called on Wednesday night, the 6th of April, to the stable of Mr. Shiell—the trunk of a woman was shown to me—Superintendent Bicknell, and several officers were there—I examined it—the head bad been severed from the body by a very clean cut through the integuments, and through the second and third vertebrae—I imagine that to hare been done all by one cut—both arms had been taken off above the shoulder joints by a clean cut through the flesh and muscles, and the bones were rather hacked as if with a knife or chopping.
Q. Did the appearances present such as would be produced by an axe? A. Not so much as the thigh bones did—they appeared more like hacking—the legs and thighs bad a clean cut through the integument, and the bones divided just below the great trochanthus —the flesh was cut, but I think the bones were chopped—this axe was the most likely instrument to produce the appearances—the bowels were all gone, the abdominal and pelvid viscera—there was a clean cut from the sternum to the pubes, and all the way round to the vertebrae.
Q. Are you able to form any judgment whether the incision had been made before or after death? A. After death, I should say decdodly—we have the means of forming a judgment on that subject—the body was in a most healthy state—there was not the least appearance of disease whatever that I could observe in any part—I did not see the heart or lungs—Dr. Ridge had an order next day to make a post mortem examination, and I was not there—my examination terminated on the Wednesday—I saw the body again, but did not examine it—from the appearance of the blood vessels as the body lay, they appeared perfectly empty, both the blood vessels and arteries—the body appeared perfectly empty—from the general appearance of it she was not at all likely to have died of apoplexy or any sudden disease—I should say she could not have been dead longer than three or four days, or I would say five day at farthest—there was not any appearance of decay—decomposition had not taken place at all—there was a very slight smell—I saw the axe that evening, but did not examine it till before the Coroner's inquest—I certainly think the marks on it are stains of blood—they have more the appearance of blood than anything else—I saw some of the bones on Wednesday, and also afterwards at the inquest—the large bone has the appearance of part of a thigh bone, and here are portions of the skull, and a portion of the knee joint—merely the top of one of the lower bones—these two pieces roost decidedly are portions of a human skull—the serrated edges of the sutures are quite visible—I am rather inclined to say the female was pregnant, but there was not sufficient to decide that—I merely come to the conclusion from a slight appearance on the nipple, and would not speak decidedly—I should be very sorry to say this is the thigh bone of a human being, in the highly calcined state of it—I did not examine the body in conjunction with Dr. Ridge—there must decidedly have been a most intense heat to make the bones so highly calcined—I merely looked at the
knife that night—I saw very little appearance of blood on it—I did not observe any thing inside it myself.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you speak with any degree of positiveness as to the appearance on the axe? A. I think it is decidedly blood, from the appearance it had then—it is not so bright now—it was much more plain—the appearance of blood on the knife was very slight—I should be sorry to swear it was blood—I saw the axe standing in the corner that night, but did not examine it—the inspector said he would put it on one side till daylight—I saw it at the inquest on Friday—it was then in the inspector's possession—I think he showed it to me before the Coroner—the marks are decidedly not so plain now as then—they have been rubbed off, or gone off by carriage, or something, from both sides—it is certainly not so bright as it was—the blood was redder, more distinct.
Q. From what you saw of it at the inquest do you think the appearances might be very visible on Wednesday by the aid of the policeman's lamp? A. They would have been visible, but not so visible as by daylight—there was sufficient to enable a person to see the appearances on Wednesday, I think—this axe is a very likely instrument to have separated the thigh bones—there was a clean cut all the way round the neck—it has the appearance of having been done by one stroke—I think it is between the second and third vertebrae, but without dissecting, I cannot be certain—the blood vessels were very empty—there must have been a very large quantity of blood effused—it was the trunk of a full grown female.
COURT. Q. Could you form an opinion of her age? A. I should say she was upwards of thirty—there was very little to judge by—the skin had a very fair appearance—that might have been from the draining of blood—I should think she had been dead four or five days—the body was plump—I should think she was about a middle size, and rather a fine made woman—the bosom was rather plump and full—I should think she had not borne a child, from the appearance of the breast—I should think her rather of a medium complexion—the skin appeared fail by that light, and very clear—I think that she had been dead four days, from the general appearance of the remains—the rigidity of the skin was over, and its flexibility come back—I judge the incisions were made after death, from their clean appearance—if made on a living person the skin retracts—I judge it had lost its elasticity at the time the cut was given.
BENJAMIN RIDOE . I practise as a surgeon at Putney. I had the CoPark-lane roner's warrant on the 7th to examine the body of a person in Putney—I examined it on Thursday and Friday—I examined it first in the stable, but did not make a very particular examination then, as the constable would not allow me, on account of the body being so dirty, being in the stall—on the Friday it was washed, and I made a particular examination—there were no marks of disease whatever about it—I should say that it had been dead from three to four days—I could not judge within twentyfour hours, but about four days from the time I first saw it, which was Thursday.
Q. Could you form a judgment whether the cutting off of the head and limbs had taken place before or after death? A. After death—I form a clear judgment of that—the heart was in a very healthy state indeed, but perfectly bloodless—there was not the least appearance of blood in either
of the cavities of the heart—there was a small portion of blood in the large blood-vessel from the heart, the ascending aorta (but none in the descending aorta)—there was a small quantity of blood, which would fill perhaps a very small teaspoon, but not more—the lungs were very collapsed; indeed, at the first view, I thought there were no lungs at all—there was neither blood nor air in the lungs, they were perfectly empty—the person had certainly not died of apoplexy, or I think we should have found blood in the heart—I am sure we should—the appearance of the body was not consistent with death from any mode of disease I am familiar with—the lungs and heart were perfectly healthy—should be inclined to say the person had died from violence, from the sudden joss, while in health, and the muscular powers going on, that the Wood had emptied itself completely out of the body by the incision of some large.
Q. How soon after death does the blood cease to flow in the way it usually does in life? A. Very soon indeed—the fluids soon separate—a person was knocked down, and life was extinct for about ten minutes, if you opened a vein, or cut an artery, you would not find blood-coagulation takes place immediately the vitality ceases—the bones produced are most decidedly those of a human body—I have not a doubt of it—here—is the lower portion of the joint of one of the thigh-bones, where it articulates with the knee-joint—here are portions of a human skull. I have no doubt whatever—they have got into the state they are now in by being burnt in a very strong fire—I saw the grate—there was a considerable quantity of fuel in it—I saw it before it was removed—I dare say there was a full bushel of fuel altogether—the stove was quite capable of producing heat to reduce the bones to the state they were found—it had a very great draught—it is my opinion the woman was pregnant—there was nothing to judge by but the breast—there was an indication there which made me form a notion of it—from the appearance of the breast I thought she might have been pregnant between four and five months—I saw the knife on the Friday, and examined the inside of it—I was attracted by the powerful smell of that part of the knife—a strong animal smell, very much like our dissecting knives—I put a small instrument into the handle, and found a very small piece of flannel at the bottom, and that had evidently been saturated with blood—it is here now, but the smell has gone off—it was saturated—it was moist that morning, and in my opinion it was human blood.
THOHAS TATHAM . I am a member of the College of Surgeons—I have been so about twelve years—I am one of the assistant-surgeons of St. Bartholoraew's Hospital—I have heard the statements of the two medical witnesses.
Q. Is there any difficulty In discovering whether wounds or incision have been made before or after death? A. Very shortly after death there would be comparatively but little, but still there is a difference—in an hour or two after death there would certainly be a difference, and in a day or two after there would be a marked difference—(examining the bones)—no doubt this is a portion of a human skull, and this is a portion of the leg, the femur—I have no doubt—and here is a portion of the tibia, which is a bone of the leg—I should say this is a portion of the arm.
Q. Assuming this body to have been found apparently healthy, the heart and lungs healthy, and wholly destitute of blood, could you form any
opinion whether the person had died from disease or not? A. I should think it was quite clear—no disease empties the body of blood—it is not consistent with death from disease of the heart, apoplexy, or any complaint I am aware of—nor strangulation.
Q. If you find, after death, the body entirely deprived of blood, can you form an opinion whether a large quantity of blood must have flown before life was extinct? A. I should think it very probable and possible, but can scarcely say for certain—the time which blood begins to stagnate after death, varies much, but in a very few minutes—in half-an-hour or an hour after death the body could not be emptied of blood, but in some rare cases the blood does not coagulate—in death from apoplexy, disease of the heart, nor any thing else, should we find the body so empty of blood.
Cross-examined. Q. You concur with the medical gentlemen, that the draining of blood was caused by an incision of some large vessel? A. Yes—there is sometimes a considerable flow of blood from the external bursting of a vessel—my attention has not been called to a case within a few days of a lady dying from bursting a blood-vessel in the leg—I have met with cases of hemorrhage from the sudden bursting of a vessel, and death ensuing.
MR. ATTORNEY-GENERAL. Q. Could that occur without previous disease in the leg or arm? A. No—I should say certainly there must be some previous disease—I should not expect that a person in ordinary health bursting a blood-vessel would deprive the body of blood.
MR. DOANE. Q. Might not a person be going about in apparently good health to others, and yet be labouring under a disease or affection of the vessel, aneurism, such as I have put to you? A. Yes—I should hardly think it, but it might be so—she might conceal a disease in the leg—she might be able to bear the pain—persons in humble life frequently bear a good deal before they apply for medical help, and go about their work labouring under the complaint.
COURT. Q. Is that complaint attended with considerable pain? A. Yet, very great pain—though they go about their work, they would complain to people about them—it is in a very advanced state of disease that it bursts out through the wound, and after some violent effort—a person in such a case would die in a very few minutes unless they called for assistance—death is so rapid, assistance seldom arrives in time—it is not a very painful death. Q. What are the cases in which blood does not coagulate after death? A. Death from electricity, when struck by lightning, the blood is found to be fluid, and it never coagulates—it will remain, and cannot be let out by an incision; there would be no muscular action.
DR. RIDGE re-examined. I have seen the whole of the bones—the whole might belong to the skull, arms, and legs of one person—I discover among them portions of the leg and both arms—here are portions of almost every part that is missing—there is a very distinct portion of the lower jaw—there is no portion belonging to the trunk, nor of any trunk of another person.
GILBERT M'MURDO . I am surgeon of Newgate. "I have been a surgeon since 1824—the bones produced belong to a human subject—this belongs to the lower part of the thigh bone—these two to the skull—here are fragments of bones of very likely the arm and one or two portions of the lower leg-bone—I have not seen any belonging to the trunk—therein not more bones than would belong to a full-grown person whose arms,
legs, and skull had been disposed of by fire—I should expect to see more—I have heard the description given by the medical gentlemen—I am aware of no disease, sudden or otherwise, that would produce the appearance after death they have described, of the body being emptied of blood—I should consider the loss of blood had taken place by some violence previous to death—as a medical man, I have no doubt of that fact—at a period of four or five months' pregnancy there would be a decided indication of it on the breast.
DR. RIDOE Re-examined. Q. Was there any wound on the trunk which could have produced so sudden an evacuation of blood? A. None at all.
Q. If the throat or jugular vein had been cut it would produce that effect? A. I should say it would require both the jugular vein and the carotid artery to be cut to produce the effect, because it was to speedy—the bursting of a vessel in the leg, by aneurism, I should think would not produce a collapse of the lungs, as the blood would take some time to go away—I know of no other state of things which would produce the same phenomena of the heart and lungs, than by the severance effectually of the carotid artery and jugular vein.
JANE BROWN . I am the wife of John Brown, of No. 18, South-street, Manchester-square, and keep a greengrocer's-shop; I let part of the house in lodgings. I knew a person called Jane Jones—she took a lodging of me three years ago last October, in the name of Jane Jones—she afterwards went by the name of Jane Good within about six months of taking them—I saw her last on Sunday, the 3rd of April—she was rather a plump person, and rather dark—I had no opportunity of knowing whether she was with child before the 3rd of April—the prisoner used to come to see her almost at soon as she came—be never said who he was, only a friend—when she changed her name to Good, he called her his wife—there was a little boy called Daniel Good—he was about eight years old when he first came—I have seen him, the prisoner, and Jane Good together—the prisoner called Jane. Good hit wife, and sometimes Jane—the boy called her "mother" in the prisoner's presence—the prisoner told me the mother had been dead a year and a half, and so the child called this woman mother—that was when he called Jane his wife—I saw Jane go out on Sunday, the 3rd of April, between two and three o'clock in the afternoon—she was dressed in a blue bonnet, black ground shawl, with flowers on it of different colours, and a mingled cotton dress—the little boy went to school about two o'clock—I did not see him again that day—I did not know that he did not sleep at my house that night, till the day after—I only heard it—I did not observe it by his bed or any thing—last October Jane Good had the front and back kitchens, and afterwards had but one kitchen—the sign-board produced was put up to denote her trade—she kept a mangle, and took washing—( on the board was painted, "Mangling done by J, Good ")—I did not see her after the 3rd of April—I think she slept out on Easter Sunday, but cannot be certain, but a padlock was on the door at eight o'clock on the morning of Easter Monday, and it was on when she went out the night before—I cannot say whether she slept out on any other night during the time she lodged in my house, but I believe she slept at her lodgings every night but Easter Sunday—the prisoner used to call to see her two or three times a week—on Monday morning, the 4th of April, I think about ten or eleven o'clock, he called to ask if he could have the room a fortnight or three weeks longer, because there was a mangle
to be disposed of, and the bed she (Mrs. Good) would require for her use—he told me Mrs. Good was gone to her place, and would not return to her room again at all—the bed he was going to take to a gentleman's lodge where she was going to be—he had told me on the 21st of March, that she was to leave her room about the 4th of April, but he could not tell how soon she would have to leave, as she had got a situation to take care of a gentleman's lodge, about four miles from where he was then living as servant—he told me on the Monday he had come to take the little boy away to his mother, I mean Jane Good—I saw the boy that Monday evening, and the prisoner then took him away—on the Tuesday night, about ten o'clock, I saw the prisoner again—he went down into the kitchen—he brought the key with him—he said he had come to town with his family to a ball—I enquired how his wife was—he told me he had not seen her that day—I did not see him on the Wednesday night, but heard him come to the door and knock—my husband let him in—it was about twelve o'clock at night—I was in bed—I believe the kitchen-door opened.
Q. Tell us very particularly what she had on when she left on Sunday? A. A blue bonnet, which I should know again, a shawl with a dark ground and coloured flowers on it, a dark printed dress, and black boots—the bonnet now produced is hers, and what she went out in that day—she also had such a dress as this on—I believe it to be the same—I have seen her wear it many times—I cannot say these are the boots—they are like what she wore—this is her rent-book—it has my own handwriting in it—I do not know this fur tippet.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose you have seen all the articles of wearing apparel before? A. Yes, they were produced at the Police Court-the woman took my lodging—I cannot positively say that when she took it she said, "My name is Jane Jones"—I think I can swear I have heard her call herself Jane Jones—that was on her board, and she was always called by that name—they called her Mrs. Jones—I wrote "Jane Jones" on her rent-book—the board now has "J. Good" on it, but before it was " Jose Jones," not "J"—it is nearly three years since Jones's name was tuned into Good—I noticed that—I cannot positively swear it was Jane on it before, but it was "Jones"—I was first called on to give evidence on the 7th of April—the police spoke to me.
COURT. Q. Did you see her go away on the Sunday? A. Yes, she had no bundle with her.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Have you any doubt of these being the things she wore as she went out on the Sunday? A. Not the least.
SARAH YOUNG . I am a widow, and live at No. 89, East-street, Manchester-square. I knew Mrs. Good—she was called Mrs. Good, and lodged at No. 18, South-street, at Mrs. Brown's—she came to me on Sunday morning, the 3rd of April, and asked if I would take the little boy for one night, whilst she went down to him—she particularly mentioned that it was for one night only—I knew the boy who lived with her—he was not with her then—he was at school—he came to me about half-fast twelve o'clock, went to church, came back in the evening, and slept at my house—he went away about half-past eight in the morning, and did not come back again till next day—he did not sleep at my house at all after Sunday night—I never saw Mrs. Good after the Sunday.
LAVENDER LAYTON . I am a confectioner, living at Putney—I know the prisoner. On Sunday, the 3rd of April, I saw him in Hammersmith-bridge-road at a quarter to four o'clock in the afternoon—there was a
female walking arm-in-arm with him—I did not know her—they were going towards Barnes on the bridge—that is in a direction towards Mr. Shiell's—he spoke to me, and introduced the female to me as his sister—he said, "My sister, Mr. Layton"—I do not remember how she was dressed.
JOHN BROWN . I keep the Coach and Horses at Barnes—I know the prisoner—I saw him on Sunday, the 3rd of April—he came to my house and had a female with him a little before four or about four—they were in my parlour when I went in—when I went into the parlour he said, "How do you do, Mr. Brown?"—I said "How do you do, coachman?"—he ordered a glass of gin and water, which I brought him—he introduced the female as his sister, and said she was a Welshwoman, that she was a dairy-maid, or lived at a dairy—he did not say where—he said many gentlemen's families were very partial to Welshwomen as dairy-maids, and that she had brought him a bottle of cream, some tea, and sugar—I said it was very good of her—the bottle was like the one produced—it was a pint bottle, not a regular wine bottle—I cannot undertake to say it was a pint bottle—it was one of this colour—the woman took it out of her pocket, and Good said she had brought it—I observed white liquor in it—they had two glasses of wine and water, and were about twenty-five minutes in my house—she drank as well as him—I knew him before, as coachman to Mr. Shieil, but never knew his name.
COURT. Q. Did you observe the dress of the woman? A. Nothing particular, except a pair of gloves—I considered them whitey-brown cotton gloves.
FANNY HESTER . I am the wife of William Hester, who lives at New York, America—I live at Priest-bridge, near Roebampton—I have known the prisoner two years—I remember his coming to my house on the evening of Sunday, the 3rd of April, at half-past four o'clock—a female was with him—I had never seen her before—she was dressed in a Mazarineblue bonnet, dark shawl, with a black ground, very Large flowers on it, a dark cotton dress, and black boots tied on one side—they drank tea with. me.
Q. Did he give a reason for wishing to drink tea with you? A. He said the gardener was not agreeable for him to have a fire in the harness-room—he introduced the woman to me as his sister-in-law, that she came from London, and was going to return home that evening—while we were at tea I said the gardener said something very disrespectful respecting Susan Butcher sleeping in the harness-room—I said this to the female, in the prisoner's presence, and she seemed very much agitated—she asked who it was—the prisoner said it was a friend of his—she said she was quite a stranger to her—they went away at half-past five, or near six—the prisoner said he was in haste, he was going to wait at table—I knew the little boy, and asked his sister-in-law to bring the boy down, as she was saying he was in a confined school, I thought it would be a treat to come into the country—the boy came on the Tuesday with the prisoner—I had not seen the prisoner between Sunday and Tuesday morning—when he brought the boy he asked me to take charge of him for a short time—I refused, as I was coming to London, and he took him away—I found the boy at my house the same evening about half-past eight, when I returned—he slept at my house that night, and went away on Wednesday morning—I believe the bonnet produced to be the one the female had on—it is the same
colour and shape—I observed the ribbons, and believe them to be the same—it was a dark cotton dress—I believe the one produced to be the same, but would not positively say it is—the bonnet I took particular notice of—the boots were similar to these.
Cross-examined. Q. You heard some disrespectful language used by the gardener about Butcher? A. Yes, about her sleeping in the harnessroom—it was not in the stable—I believe it was in the harness-room—the female heard Good tell me she was his sister-in-law, and so on.
ROBERT PAYLING ( police-constable V 248). I was on duty in the Richmond-road on Sunday, the 3rd of April, and saw the prisoner about twenty minutes or half-past six o'clock in the evening, near Priest-bridge, about half a mile from Putney Park-lane—I knew him before as Mr. Shiell's coachman—he was going towards Putney Park-lane—there was a female with him—I did not know her—she had a grey bonnet on like the one produced, and a dark coloured gown similar to this—they were walking arm-in-arm, and the woman was conversing in a very loud tone, loud enough to make me suppose they were not on good terms—I could hear them a great many rods after I passed them.
Cross-examined. Q. She was speaking loudly to him? A. Yes, they were apparently quarrelling—he did not speak so loud as her—they appeared having words with each other by her discourse—as far as I thought it was her finding fault with him.
FANNY HESTER re-examined. I live about a mile from Roehampton, close to Priest-bridge—they left me about half-past five or near six.
GEORGE ROYAL . I keep the King's Head, Roehampton, a mile and a half from Priest-bridge, and about three quarters of a mile from Mr. Shiell's house—a person going from Priest-bridge to Mr. Shiell's, would not pass my house—I know the prisoner—I saw him on Sunday afternoon, the 3rd of April, at my house about seven o'clock—it might be a little before seven—I cannot speak to half an hour—there was a female with him who I had never seen before—she had a light bonnet on, that is all I can say of her dress—they had two glasses of gin and water—the woman drank—the prisoner pressed her to drink several times, and she seemed to drink with reluctance, as if she did not wish to take it, as she put it away from her when he offered it, more than once—they staid at my house about an hour, and left together about eight—I did not see in what direction they went.
Cross-examined. Q. Was you in their company all the time they were there? A. I was in and out of the room—I noticed the female—she was sitting opposite as I entered the room—she did not appear dejected, that I observed, but reluctant to drink.
SARAH GREENWOOD . I was cook in the service of Mr. Shiell, in the beginning of April last—while there the prisoner was also in Mr. Shiell's service—he slept in the house, on the same floor as we all sleep—we go out of the servants' hall to go to our bed-rooms—I remember the Wednesday on which Good left—on the Sunday before that I saw him at my master's house at dinner time, one o'clock—he said he could not stop 8 minute, he was going to town to meet a friend—I saw him again about half-past seven in the evening—he did not then stop in the house many minutes—he then went out—I saw him next about half-past nine—he was quite sober then—I did not see him again that night—I saw him at breakfast next morning, and not after till evening—he was alone then, but about
nine on Monday night, or a little later, he brought a little boy—he came to breakfast again on Tuesday morning, and appeared very dirty—I said, "Daniel, you are very dirty, you are not fit to sit down with us, you are like a sweep"—he said he was going to wash and shave himself—he was very black at the time, and said he had been cleaning his harness—his face and hands were dirty—I never was in the harness-room—I believe that and the stable were always kept locked.
Cross-examined. Q. You saw him on Friday night at half-past seven at his master's house? A. Yes, he came through the cooking kitchen, going to the pantry—he asked me if the dinner was out—I said it was, and there were some letters for him to take to the post-office—he said be would see about them, and went into the pantry—he did not take the letters away with him—he was to take them to the post but he did not in my presence—he went to Mr. Shiell to speak about them—I did not see him again until half-past nine, when he came borne—he bad been out, but I had not seen him go out—I remained in the kitchen, and about half-past nine saw him in the servants' hall, which is a room adjoining the kitchen—he came into the servants' ball to supper—there are four servants.
COURT. Q. He was at supper with you that night? A. Yes, at half-past nine—I left him in the hall and went to bed—he did not take the letters with him when be went to master about them, but they were gone soon after—my bed-room is nearly close to his—I did not hear him come to bed that night—I left him in the servants hall at half-past nine, when I went up to bed—I was not long going to bed—I heard nobody come up stairs—I did not go into his room in the morning—he makes his bed himself.
GEORGE JORDAN . I am footman in Mr. Shiell's service.
