On the Sunday evening before the prisoner left, I saw him at half-past seven—he came into my pantry—he was alone—I told him that Mr. Shiell wanted to see him about some letters to go to the post—he went from me to the parlour—I saw him when he came from the parlour—he said he was going as for as the Green Man with a friend of his who was going to take his boy to Ireland—the Green Man is on Putney-heath, about a mile and a half from Mr. Shiell's—he remained in the house not more than a quarter of an hour before he went out again.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know whether he took the letters to the post or not? A. He did not—I saw him go from the pantry-door, but did not see him leave the house that night.
MARY HOGG . I am a laundress, and live in South Mews, Manchestersquare. I knew Mrs. Good, who lived in South street—she mangled for me—I saw her last on Sunday morning the 3rd of April, about eleven o'clock—she appointed to come to me at nine o'clock on Monday but did not—the prisoner came to my house about twelve o'clock on Monday—I asked him where Mrs. Good was—he said she was detained on account of the poultry—I told him I expected to see her—I said she promised to be home about nine o'clock—he made no answer—he asked me to go into the front kitchen with him (where Jane Good lived) and take one sheet off the line and the other out of the copper—I was to repair them and to take them to Mr. Spice, a surgeon in Duke. street—I always washed the sheets for Mrs. Good, but nothing but the best of their things—I went and got the sheets—I saw the prisoner next on Tuesday night, between ten and eleven o'clock—he came to my house and asked for a light to make a fire in the kitchen as his people were gone to a ball—I asked him where Mrs.
Good was, and how she was—he said he did not know, he had not seen her these two days.
COURT. Q. You went to the kitchen on Monday morning? A. Yes, the sheet was hanging on the line, and one sheet on the copper, wet—the kitchen was remarkably tidy—I sent my little boy with a light.
WILLIAM JONES . I am assistant to Mr. Smith, a pawnbroker, in South street, Manchester-square. I know Daniel Good—he was in the habit of being at our shop and pledging—I have known him between two and three years—I saw him last at the shop on Monday the 4th of April—I am sure it was that day—it was between three and four o'clock as near as I can recollect—near three o'clock that afternoon he brought a gown, petticoat, and two shawls—I lent him ten shillings on them—he had an umbrella in pawn with us at the time—( looking at two umbrellas)—this silk one with a brown handle is it—he redeemed it on Monday the 4th of April, and pawned these articles—he said the reason he was pawning them was because he was going out of town for a few days and was pledging them for safety.
LYDIA SUSANNAH BUTCHER . I live at Woolwich with my father and mother. I have known the prisoner for some time—he has been to see me at my father's house several times—I have been to see him at his master's in Putney-lane three or four times—I have staid there all night—I do not know when that was—I remember his coming to me on a Wednesday—it was a week or a fortnight before that, as near as I can judge—I slept in the harness-room—the prisoner let me in, and I went away in the morning—I did not see him from the time I slept at the stable until Wednesday the 6th of April, when he came to my father's house in a pony-chaise with a little boy, between three and four o'clock in the afternoon, as near as I can tell—I had not seen the little boy before, that I know of—I heard him speak to the prisoner, he called him "father"—the prisoner brought me that day this fur tippet and gown, and the blue bonnet produced, also some boots, and here is a handkerchief which he brought me—these are the boots, they lace on one side, and this is the fur tippet, it has no collar to it—the articles were in an old handkerchief, and the bonnet in an old hat-box without any lid—he told me he had brought me those things if I would accept of them—he did not say anything particular about them then—he said they had been his wife's, he said he had brought these things for me, and he had told me previously that his wife had been dead these five years.
Q. Did you expect him to give you any thing—had he said anything about giving you anything? A. Yes, several weeks before—he did not say what they were to be, but said he had some things be would give me if I would have them—he said he had got some things of his wife's, and he would bring them for me some day, but did not say when he would bring them—he told me this some weeks before he brought them, and when he brought them on Wednesday he said he had brought them—he asked me if I would wear the bonnet—he did not say anything more—he brought two squares of silk at the same time, but took them away—it was shot silk trimmed with lace—these things remained in my possession until the next evening, Thursday, when Sergeant Parry came to my father's house—the prisoner went away on Wednesday evening, I think between five and six—he took tea with me—the little boy and me and my younger sister went with him to New Cross, but my sister got out at half-way to Greenwich.
Q. Did tire prisoner, while he was driving you, mention anything else you might have? A. Yes; I was to have gone up to London to see the things I was to have, to see different things he had got—there was a mangle and other things—he mentioned the mangle—I was to go on the following Saturday—I was to go by water to Putney, and meet him there—be was to go with me to London by coach—he mentioned where the mangle was—I do not know the name of the street—I might know it if I heard it again.
Q. Was it very near Manchester-square? A. Yes, I think that is it, I am not quite sure; South-street is one part of it I know—he asked me if be should send the little boy to his brother—he told the little boy to call me "mother," as he said his sister in-law, the child's aunt, was gone into the country.
Cross-examined. Q. How long bad you known him? A. Some time, about two years—it is within the last twelve months that he told me his wife was dead—it may not be so long as that—it is near twelve months.
Q. Has he given you presents from time to time? A. No—he brought the two pieces of silk on the Wednesday—they were two silk shawls trimmed with lace and lined with pink—I have not seen them since—he took them away with him, tied up in a blue handkerchief—I tied them up and gave them to him—he brought nothing else but the articles produced—I do not know whether there was a mattress in the harness-room—I did not look at what I slept on—I believe there was a mattress, but I did not examine it—it was in the harness-room—I never went into the stable—I never was there to sleep but once, and then I was benighted—I never visited him there but once, not in the harness-room—I went to see him several times at the house—the first time was about last Christmas—I saw Houghton, the gardener, once, and the son several times—I did not desire Good to take the two shawls back, and keep them till the Saturday following—he took them of his own accord, but I was to have them again—he did not say when.
JAMES PARRY . I am a sergeant of the police. On Thursday, the 7th of April, I took these articles from the house where Butcher lives—they have been in my possession ever since—I have produced them to-day.
RICHARD GOLDING . I am a policeman. On the 9th of April I went to No. 18, South-street, Manchester-square—I searched the kitchen—I brought some things away, which I have here—they are pieces of silk and cotton.
MARY ANN CLARKE . I am the wife of James Clarke, and live at No. 18, South-street, in the house Mrs. Good lived in—I know this to be Mrs. Good's bonnet, I made it fox her myself, about a year and a half ago, and have seen her wear it constantly—I know the little boy who used to call her mother, and know the prisoner—she pasted as his wife—I saw her last on the 3rd of April, about ten o'clock in the morning—she said to me, "Here you are," that is all—I once went with her to try a gown on, and observed, to the best of my recollection, a small dark mole, just above the collar-bone, on the right side of her neck—this silk produced is a piece of the same silk the bonnet was made of—it was separated before the bonnet was made, and this is a portion of what I gave into Mrs. Good's hands when it was done—these two pieces of flannel have been a flannel petticoat, and, I believe, the property of Mrs. Good, by the width of the band and the quality of the flannel—I have seen her put it on—the-band is more wide than is commonly worn—it is the sort of petticoat I have seen her wear and I have seen her hang it on the line—
this handkerchief is exactly the pattern and colour I have seen her wear round her neck every day—this key (on a bunch) is the key of the entrance-door of the house she lodged in—I have seen her with this key, and this guard I have seen her wear round her neck every day—this cotton umbrella, I think, I have seen in her possession.
Cross-examined. Q. How soon were you examined about this matter? A. On the 7th of April I gave an account to sergeant Golding—I went to Bow-street, I think, on the 18th, but I was at the inquest before that, on the 11th, I believe—the inspector of police was present—I gave the account I have to-day about the mole on her neck.
ELIZABETH CHRISTIE . I live on the first-floor at No. 18, South-street. I knew Mrs. Good—she lived in the kitchen—I saw her last on Sunday, the 3rd of April, at twelve o'clock at noon—she was not then dressed to go out—I can swear to this bonnet as her property—I altered the bow for her nine months ago—I have frequently seen her wear it, and am quite sure it is hers—this dress is hers, and this handkerchief—this guard I never saw her without, she wore it round her neck, and this key corresponds with the door-key—I am quite certain of the guard—I believe she kept her keys on it—I have seen the prisoner come to visit her, for the last two years, two or three times a week, sometimes more—I did not see him on the Monday; I heard his voice, and saw him on Tuesday evening, the 5th of April—I heard somebody come in at the street-door—I went down, and called out, "Who is there?"—he said, "Me"—I said, "Who is me?"—he said "Good"—I went down stairs further, knowing Mrs. Good had been robbed about a year and a half before, to see who it was—I knew his voice when I went further down—there was a little boy showing him a light—I said, "Is that your little boy, Mr. Good?"—he said, "No, my little boy is in the country with his mother"—the boy's back was towards me, or I should have known him—I am sure this was Tuesday—he said, "We are at a ball this evening"—he repeated that two or three times—he was unlocking the door—he did not stay long, but he went to the landlady—after that I went further down stairs, and put my hand on the prisoner's shoulder, to speak to him, and saw the little boy then—I have seen Mrs. Good's neck uncovered when she has been washing, and observed a dark mole, above the collar-bone, on the right side of the neck.
Cross-examined. Q. You have seen Good with her? A. Yes—I have seen them go out on a Sunday together—I have opened the street-door to him—he has said, "Is my wife at home?"—he behaved with the greatest kindness towards her—I have heard him say, when going away, "Good bye, my dear, take care of yourself"—I thought him very fond of her.
GEORGE JAKES . I drive a hackney-cab. On the night of the 6th of April my cab stood near Grosvenor-place—I was hired by the prisoner, about half-past eleven o'clock, to drive him to South-street, Manchestersquare, opposite No. 18—he had a small bundle with him—we had a dispute about the fare—he gave me is—the fare was 1 s. 4 d.—I got 2 d. more—I said that would not do, I must have the other 2 d.—he told me to come over to the public-house—we went to the Bedford Arms, and he called for half a pint of porter.
RICHARD SALMON . I am pot-man at the Bedford Arms, South-street. On Wednesday night, the 6th of April, I saw the prisoner there—I had seen him before that about South-street)—he came to my house and had a glass of ale, and the cabman a pint of porter—he then asked mistress to
lend him & screw-driver, saying he had left his key behind him, and wanted to get into his apartment—she lent him one—I went with him, to bring it back, to No. 18, South-street—Mr. Brown let him into the house—I went down to the kitchen with him—I drew the staple of the door by his direction, and then went away.
JOHN BROWN . I am landlord of No. 18, South-street. I let the prisoner in on the night of the 6th of April—I believe he stopped there till about half-past five in the morning, when I heard a cab drive away.
HENRY LITTLEBOY . I drive a cab. On the morning of the 7th of April I was in Paddington-street—the prisoner fetched me from there and ordered me to drive to Charing-cross, but in going along he said to Whitcomb-street—he had a bed, a large box, and a small one, and a hat-box—I took them up at No. 18, South-street, and drove down to Sheppard's livery stables—he took all the things out there—I left him in the street with them near the livery stables.
JAMES OSBORNE . I drive a cab, I was on the stand near the Union Club-house, on the morning of the 7th of April, between a quarter and twenty minutes to six, I was called to drive to Whitcomb-street, which I did, and saw the prisoner with a bundle, a bed, a large trunk, a small box, and a woman's reticule basket—the box appeared quite full of things—I took the trunk on the dickey, the other things were put into the cab—he ordered me to drive to the Spotted Horse, in the Strand—I said I did not know the Spotted Horse, I knew the Spotted' Dog—he said that was all the same—I drove him there, assisted him in taking off the luggage, which was put in front of the bar—it is a booking-office—I saw him pay eight-pence for booking.
MARY ANN HAZLEWOOD . I was barmaid at the Spotted Dog, in the Strand. On Thursday the 7th of April, the prisoner booked a quantity of things there, and gave the name of Stanley—he said nothing more about them—he went away in a very few minutes, came back again about ten or half-past with a female, and went into the coffee-room with her—I cannot say how long they staid—they went away together and took all the things with them—I believe they went in a cab, but I did not see.
THOMAS SAYLES . I was waiter at the Spotted Dog on Thursday, the 7th of April. I remember a man coming there in the morning—the prisoner is that man—he had a female with him—they came into the tap-room—he had a beef steak in his hand, and asked me to cook it for him—I said I had a very bad fire and could not—he said, "I don't mind waiting, I am from Bristol"—I cooked the steak—they both sat down and ate it—they were very thick in conversation together—all the conversation I noticed was, he made a reply to the female, "That b—ch won't trouble you any more"—I am quite sure I heard the prisoner say that to the woman—I saw him go for a cab —some things were put into the cab and some outside—they were all taken away—I did not see them start—he kept frequently going and outside the door—I suppose he went in and out seven or eight times while the steak was cooking, and while it was being eaten he went out twice—he was in the house about half-an-hour, and went out as much as eight times during that time.
Cross-examined. Q. What day was this? A. Thursday, the 7th of April, at ten o'clock in the morning—I saw the female at Bow-street.
COURT. Q. Did she pass by any name at Bow-street? A. Yes, Mary Good—it was the woman now in custody by that name—( looking at her).
GEORGE BLAND . I drive a cab, No. 430. On Thursday, the 7th of April I saw the prisoner at the Spotted Dog, Strand—I drove him from there down to No. 4, Flower and Dean-street, Spitalfields—he had a bed, a box, and one or two bundles inside the cab —Molly Good was with him—they went into the house.
WILLIAM PARDOE BIDDLE . I am assistant to Mr. Soames, a pawnbroker. I saw the prisoner first on Thursday, the 7th of April—he came to my shop first alone, and afterwards accompanied by a woman by the name of Mary Good—I have known her by that name some time—he came into the shop, inquired if we would take in some bed and bedding and a trunk as pledges—we objected to it not knowing any thing of him—he went away and returned with Mrs. Good—I had known bur during the time I had been with Mr. Soames, eleven months—she said in his presence, "It is all right, Mr. Soames, you need not be afraid, it is my husband just come from sea"—in about an hour after he brought a trunk, went away, and returned in about ten minutes with the bed—I went away to dinner, and returned in halfan-hour—he was still there, and the trunk open with the things displayed on the counter—he got 2 l. 10 s. on them—he said he had just come by railway, and wanted to go into the country, which was the reason he wanted to raise money, and that the goods belonged to a passenger who died on his way home from India, and gave him all these things—he returned in about ten minutes with a bundle of women's wearing apparel, and some odd china—there was two mantillas, a white scarf, shawl, a black figured shawl, and some odd cups and saucers—the police have some of the apparel and I the rest, which I produce.
STEPHEN LUDLOW . I am special constable of Tunbridge, Kent. On the 16th of April, in consequence of information from a man named Rose, I went to where I found the prisoner, and identified him—I then went into the town to the inspector, communicated with him, and went with Humphreys to where the prisoner was—the inspector brought him away—I found a watch on him and some money, and a woman's jaconet apron—there are spots of blood on it—he was taken into custody by Humphrey—he was dressed in pair of trowsers and a short fustian jacket, as a bricklayer—I did not see him with a had.
GUILTY . Aged 44.— DEATH .
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.

1706. MARY GOOD was indicted for feloniously receiving, harbouring, and maintaining the said Daniel Good, knowing him to have committed the said murder. Also for receiving on the 7th of April, 1 piece of silk, value 1 s.; 1 shawl, value 2 s.; 2 work boxes, value 2 s.; the property of Charles James, Lord Bishop of London; and 1 pair of trowsers, value 5 s.; the goods of James Stapleton Collingbourn, knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
(Upon which indictments the Learned Attorney-General offered no evidence.)
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1707]
1707. RICHARD GAMBLE was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 7th of April, 3 gowns, value 3 s.; the goods of Charles James, Lord Bishop of London; and 2 shirts, value 3 s., the goods of Solomon Penny; knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.—Three other COUNTS, varying the manner of laying the property.
Mr. Attorney-General offered no evidence.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420509-1708]
1708. ELIZA HEBBERT was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of March, 1 gown, value 6 s.; the goods of John Spragg and another.
JOSEPH NEWMAN PERKINS . I am shopman to Mr. Perkins, a pawnbroker, at Rotherhithe. On the 7th of March, about seven o'clock in the morning, the prisoner pawned a gown with me for 3 s. in the name of Mary Pritchard, Adam-street—a person of the name of Pritchard was with her at the time.
Prisoner. The gown was brought to my house by Ann Pritchard, who lived next door to me; she told me she had bought it for 5 s. 6 d. among other duplicates.
WILLIAM JOSEPH TAYLOR . I am shopman to John Spragg, a pawnbroker, at Rotherhithe. On the 7th of March we hung a gown on the door, within the door-post—it was safe between one and two o'clock—in the course of the day Mary Pritchard came and pawned a piece of cotton, and went away—I saw nothing of the prisones.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1709]
1709. JOHN GOODFELLOW was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of April, 1 brush, value 4 s., the goods of George Bromfield, his master.
EMILY BROMFIELD . I am the wife of George Bromfield, a brush-maker, of 87, Bridge-house-place, Southwark. The prisoner was our porter for about eleven months. On the 15th of April, about a quarter after eight o'clock at night, I was in the warehouse, and in consequence of what Holiday said to me I sent the prisoner out of the warehouse—he had a coat hanging on a nail in the warehouse—I saw this brush taken from the pocket by Ann Holiday when I had sent him out—I had desired him to leave and not return till Mr. Bromfield came back, but not coming back as soon as I expected, I gave him into custody.
Prisoner. Mr. Bromfield was out of town. Mrs. Bromfield had some words with me on Friday; she did not charge me with taking the brush then, but on Friday night ordered me to come for my wages on Saturday night, which I did; but she would not give them to me; I did not leave till she did; she sent for two policeman to take me out of the premises; she said she discharged me because I took half-an-hour for breakfast in the morning. Witness. I did not say so—up to two months ago we had the greatest confidence in him, but on Good Friday we went out to dinner, and the servant told us he had taken some things out, a quantity of brushes—I mentioned it to him, he said they had been gone out for orders; I looked at the day-book, and found it was not so—when he came on Saturday my husband had not returned, and I gave him in charge.
Prisoner. I was not out of the warehouse all night. Witness. Yes, I sent him out—he staid out about half-a-minute, just while we took the brush out—he returned in an instant—I did not charge him with it then, but
told the servant to take care of it—I told him to leave the premises, and not return till his master came home—he shut up and left—this was Friday—he came to work next morning, but I told the servant not to let him in—he came at night for his wages—I said his master would say what he had to say to him, and settle with him, when he came home—he was excessively impertinent, and I sent for a policeman.
HANNAH HOLIDAY . I am servant to Mr. Bromfield—I held a candle while mistress went round the warehouse on Friday night, about a quarter to nine o'clock—I passed the prisoner's coat, hanging on a nail, and it hit my arm—I spoke to mistress—she told the prisoner to go over to Mrs. White's and see what o'clock it was, and while he was gone I had only time to take this brush out of his coat pocket—I took it down to the kitchen-drawer—mistress told me to keep it—the prisoner came back directly—mistress said, "Shut up the house directly, and get out of my house, and never come near till Saturday night, when your master will be at home"—she did not explain why—he came next morning, and wassent away again—he put on the coat the brush was found in that Friday night—I gave the brush to the policeman on Sunday morning.
Prisoner. That girl is bribed to say what she does; Mrs. Bromfield has bad so many servants; the policeman has got a strap which I was ordered to thrash her with to make her comply with Mrs. Bromfield's request; because I would not do it, she would frequently throw water over her; I said I would not thrash her; I would rather thrash her mistress. Witness. She sent him down to frighten me because I would not take my medicine—she did not order him to strap me on the back—he came with a strap in his hand—he did not say anything—he said he was going to hit me with the strap because I would not take my medicine—I took it, and he did not strap me—master's brother never offered to strap me—it is true that I found this brush in his pocket—I did not put it in, and do not believe that mistress did—the coat hung behind the door—when the door opened the coat was against the wall—I have been in the service going on for four months.
WELLINGTON WEBSTER ( police-constable N 81.) I received the prisoner in charge on Sunday morning, the 17th—Holiday delivered me the brush—when the prisoner was charged with stealing it, he said, "Is that all? I thought it was something more than that "—he told the servant she was bribed to say what she had.
GEORGE BROMFIELD ( examined by the Prisoner.) Q. Did you ever suspect me of taking any thing off your premises A. Yes, I have—I did not tell you so, or I should not have caught you—I have suspected you for two months—I missed goods repeatedly from the stock, and you were the only one in the warehouse.
Q. Had not Benjamin Bromfield as much access to the warehouse as I had? A. He was foreman, but he conducted himself in such a curious manner I was obliged to discharge him—when I came home he said he was going to leave the country, and he has gone to live with the prisoner, and he sent back several towels, pillow-cases, and one of the servants gowns, which he said had been taken by mistake.
Prisoner. A man was discharged who spoke for me at Union-ball' Witness. I have discharged Benjamin Bromfield, a boy named Follet, and a man named Beaumont, but he never came, as he had got another place—I returned home on the Friday after the robbery—I was on a journey—I discharged the three men, because I thought I had better have fresh faces
altogether from their running to Union-hall instead of attending to business—I was out of town when the examination took place, and my wife unprotected—Benjamin Bromfield, instead of appearing there for me, left her to manage it herself—I thought they might be concerned with him and discharged them—they were valuable men to me, but still I thought it proper, and the prisoner was a valuable servant—I know of no dispute between him and Mrs. Bromfield—I told her to mark the brushes, as we have been missing them—she marked them B, and we found this one corresponding with that—it is now very faint, as it has been handled a good deal—Benjamin Bromfield was my brother—about twelve months ago I was enticed by my father to become his partner, but he became bankrupt—there was property concealed, and I sent two officers with the prisoner to take possession of it—since this Benjamin Bromfield comes forward to give this man character—I cannot swear the brush is my make only from the mark which I saw this minute.
WELLINGTON WEBSTER re-examined. I have had the brush since, it was at Union-hall—there was a mark referred to there, but it is not quite visible—no mark has been put since I have had it when I first had it Mrs. Bromfield pointed out a mark—there was an impression, but I cannot say it was a B.
MRS. BROMFIELD. I think the mark was here—it was a capital B in pencil—on my solemn oath, I did not put the brush into the prisoner's pocket, nor know of its being put there—I was always anxious he should get forward, and had no occasion to find fault with him—Mr. Bromfield raised his wages, and was talking of making him warehouseman if he could write better.
HANNAH HOLIDAY re-examined. I did not put the brush into his pocket, nor did any body, I noticed it in striking against his coat—I had no idea there was a brush there—I whispered about it to mistress, and she sent him out of the warehouse.
JURY. Q. Did the coat hang outside the door or inside? A. Outside—the door opening hid the coat—it was shut when my arm touched it—it leads from the warehouse into the shop—the coat hung on the front shop door—I was in the front shop, and going into the warehouse, when I brushed against it.
MR. BROMFIELD. The front the premises I call the war house—beyound the warehouse there is a workshop—there is a door from the ware-house into the workshop—it opens into the warehouse—a coat behind that door would be hid when door opened—in opening the door it must have hung before her—the door is very small and narrow, and the coat would with large pockets, which he put on when he went away—a person passing from one room to the other must touch the coat to pull the door open—there is no lock nor latch to it—I believe there is a little bit of wood to take hold of.
Prisoner. My coat did hang no the door at all, but on a post at the side, about half-a-yard from the door when it is open. Witness. There is a rack behind the door, with poles to put brooms on, and large shelves separated by posts.
pecting the prisoner.
Prisoner. What was it? I did not go out Good Friday with my coat on. Witness. Oh, John! You went out at the front door, with your
coat on, about four o'clock—I do not know where you were going—you had a fustian jacket on—I call that a coat.
Prisoner. I hope the Jury will consider my character; she has thought proper to charge me with this because I took half-an-hour to my break fast and tea; this is the second plot laid against me; she sent into the work shop, and ordered the apprentice to weigh some hair, which he did; she said, "Take it to Cann's, in Chiswell-strcet, and if he buys it come and tell me, I will come and send a policeman, and give him in charge;" Cann is a respectable oil-merchant.
MRS. BROMFIELD. I ordered some hair to be weighed, and sent the prisoner with it, by my husband's direction, as Cann had offered my husband some bristles—I have heard people say he was not a bristle-merchant, that he bought them from men who stole them; and before my husband should buy bristles, fearing they might be bought improperly, I sent to sell these, to try him.
Q. You sent your servant to test his honesty, to see if he would purchase, in an indirect manner? A. Yes, before my husband should purchase of him—this was three or four months ago—the bristles were not such as a merchant sold, but mixed, such as we often lose—I told the prisoner my object—I never quarrelled with the prisoner, nor did my husband.
GEORGE WEBB BROMFIELD . I am the prosecutor's father. The prisoner was with me five years, till my son decoyed him away from my service his own—it was a plan between my son and his wife—they are excellent to good sort of people in that business, to take my men away, and my business, &c.—the prisoner bore an excellent character—I have another son—the prosecutor decoyed him away, and he has found it out—he was discharged as well as the boy, and it is coming round again to fair play—I believe the brush was put into the prisoner's pocket.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Coltman.
[trialAccount t18420509-1710]
1710. JOHN EYLES was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of March, 13 metal cocks, value 18 s., the goods of Stephen Green, his master.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT HUNTM . I am clerk to Stephen Green, a manufacturer of stone-ware, at Lambeth—he has between sixty and seventy men in hit employ. The prisoner was one, and had been so five years, at different times—in consequence of property being missed, I was directed to watch the men who go to breakfast at eight o'clock, and return at half-past eight—the prisoner went at half-past eight, and came back at nine, that the premises might never be left—he would have the charge of the yard while the men went to breakfast—his business during that time was to try the bottles, to see if they were sound—I placed myself behind a wall with a hole in it, which commanded a view of the inner store-room, where we keep brass taps used for filtering machines—I went out of the counting-house at five minutes to eight, as if I was going to breakfast, and returned by a backway, to the place in the wall—in about a quarter of an hour I saw the prisoner, through the window of the counting-house, enter the first counting-house, very cautiously—he then entered the second counting-house—I then got up to the hole in the wall, and saw him enter the store-room—he then procured something to stand on, to reach the brass cocks, which were
in a cupboard over the door of the store-room—he first took down a quantity of taps from a broken parcel, placed them on his left arm, and then reached down a sound parcel, containing six taps—he closed the door with his right hand, removed what he stood on, and proceeded cautiously through the counting-house, with them on his arm—I then went from where I was, to the counting-house—be did not see me—when he got through the outer counting-house I opened the counting-house door which leads into the factory, and, with the noise of the door opening, he turned round to see who it was—I said nothing to him, nor he to me, but I took the taps from him, in a basket—I said, "This is quite sufficient, Eyles"—he then went down the counting-house stairs, without saying any thing—I went to speak to the foreman, and while doing so, he took his hat, walked out of the yard, and went out of the premises—it was not the time to leave, as the men had not returned—he never came back—I never saw him again till he was in custody, about a fortnight or three weeks afterfind him before—these are the thirteen taps which I took from the basket in his hand, which belongs to master—they are of two different makers, seven made by Walker, marked, "No. 2" and six by Stevens, marked, "No. 267."
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you mark them before you gave them to the policeman? A. I did, I have my initials on them—I knew where the prisoner lived—I knew he was married—I know he had one child in the prosecutor's employment—I do not know how many others he has—I went in search of him to York-street, Blackfriars-road, where a relation of his lives—I thought he might sleep there, as he did not go home—I did not tell him he was discharged, and not to come again, nothing of the kind.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had he any business in your store-room? A. None at all, he had no business to leave the yard—he had no right to meddle with these goods without a special order.
JOSEPH BAKER . I am the prosecutor's foreman. On the morning of the 31st of March, the prisoner had charge to the yard from eight o'clock to half-past—his duty was not to leave the yard at all—he had no business in the counting-house or store-room—from that day I went in search of him till the 25th of April—I could not find him at his house—I was out every night after him—I at last found him at a lodging be had taken in Queen's-court, near Park-street—he was not dressed as usual—he had a round frock on, like a coal heaver.
Cross-examined. Q. How near did you find him to your master's premises? A. Within five minutes' walk—I found him with his wife there—he has four or five children—I never had any quarrel with him—the taps were used for stone filterers—it has not been his duty to put them on for twelve months past.
WILLIAM BUTT . I am employed in the factory, it is my business to check the number of metal cocks, to see if any are missing, they are kept in the inner store-room, the prisoner had no right to meddle with them at all; there had been some lost before, and there were new regulations made. On the 31st of March there was nineteen of No. 2 Walker's—I ascertained the quantity at half-past six o'clock the same morning, and there was 146 of Stevens', No. 267—a little before nine the same morning when I came from breakfast I examined the stock again, and missed
seven of Walker's and six of Stevens'—the cocks produced I had in my hand at half-past six—they are the property of my master.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner in charge—I had been hunting for him at various places, from the 31st of March to the 25th of April.
Cross-examined. Q. You charged him with stealing thirteen taps? A. Yes, he said, "I stole no taps"—I had looked for him in the neighbourhood of the factory, and of his residence, and found him within a few minutes' walk of the prosecutor's—he had left his old lodging.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 45.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18420509-1711]
1711. WILLIAM KINGTON was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of April, at Camberwell, 3 pairs of trowsers, value 3 l.; 5 waistcoats, value 1 l. 5 s.; 2 coats, value 4 l.; 1 brooch, value 2 l.; 2 hair-pins, value 10 s.; and 1 bracelet, value 10 s.; the goods of Edmund Philips, in his dwelling-house; and that he bad been previously convicted of felony.
DANIEL NAISH . I am a carpenter. On the 16th of April, about twelve o'clock in the day, I saw the prisoner at Rose-villa, Well-street, Camberwell—he opened the front-gate, walked up the gravel-walk, went round into the back-yard, and shut the gate after him—I could not see him after that—he went round the end of the house—I waited about ten minutes, and then rung at the bell of the front-gate—Mrs. Philips opened the front-door—I told her what I had seen—I was taking a pint of beer to the house—she took the beer, and I went into the kitchen with her—I went out, and called Mr. Burrows to help me search the house—we searched the out-house, and bottom part of the house, and found nobody-Mrs. Philips and Mr. Burrows went up stairs together to look, and gave an alarm—Mr. Burrows and I and one Smith, went up stairs, and saw the prisoner in a bed-room, standing between the bed and the window—it was a quarter of an hour after I had seen him at the back-door—Mr. Philips was on the landing—we took the prisoner, and gave him into custody to Sergeant Dinn.
ELIZA PHILIPS . Naish gave me information—I went up stairs, looked under the bed, and saw a man there—I called Mr. Naish up to take him, and when I did so, he got up from under the bed—he was taken—I missed some hair-pins, a brooch, and a small bracelet, from the pincushion of the toilet-table—I saw the sergeant take a bundle from under the bed which the prisoner had been under—it contained some clothes of Mr. Philipi's, which were kept in the drawers of the same bed-room—I had seen them there in the morning—I have seen the trinkets since in possession of Dinn—these now produced are them.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You bad a servant in your employment at this time? A. Yes, I am quite sure no else one was concerned in this matter—the servant left in consequence of illness—I do not know the value of the trinkets, they were given to me.
EDMUND PHILIPS . I came home when the prisoner was on the road to the station—I went and met Dinn, and saw this brooch, two hair-pins, and a bracelet, taken from the prisoner's pocket—after leaving him at the station, Dinn went to my house with me—on going up stairs, I found a bundle under the bed, containing the clothes now produced, tied in a handkerchief that did not belong to me—I had seen them that morning, an hour or two before.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there anybody else in your house who might have put them under the bed? A. No, we occasionally hired a person to do work for us, being without a servant—nobody was there but the girl—we have parted with her on account of illness—she was not a regular servant, but merely came for a few days—we did not know her before, but knew her family—my house is in the parish of Camberwell—I believe it is St. Giles's—I know it is in Camberwell parish—I have seen "Camberwell" on the vestry papers—I should think the clothes are worth 8 l.—there is a great-coat, a dress-coat, and five waistcoats.
THOMAS BARNABAS DINN . I am a police-sergeant. I took the prisoner in charge, and found these trinkets on him—I saw him throw a brooch and some hair-pins on the ground—I took one from his band, and the rest off the ground—I went with Mr. Philips to the house, and found a bundle under the bed.
SAMUEL WRIGHT . I am a policeman. I produced a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read)—I was present when he pleaded guilty, and know him to be the person.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18420509-1712]
1712. WILLIAM HARISS was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Jemima Jane Nicholls, on the 25th of April, at Camberwell, and stealing therein 6 rings, value 15 s.; 5 brooches, value 15 s.; 3 seals, value 10 s.; 1 pair of ear-rings, value 5 s.; 2 pencil-value cases, value 12 s.; 4 spoons, value 14 s.; 1 knife, value 2 s.; 1 eye-glass, 4 s.; 1 neck chain, value 2 s.; 1 pair of bracelets, value 3 s.; 1 cross, value 1 s.; 1 snap, value 2 s.; 1 scent-bottle, value 1 s.; 1 pair of studs, value 1 s.; 1 necklace, value 1 s.; 2 sovereigns, 1 shilling, 1 sixpence, 2 groats, and 1 silver coin, called a "threepence," the property of the said Jemima Jane Nicholls,
MR. LILLY conducted the Prosecution
JEMIMA JANE NICHOLLS . I live in Denmark-road, in the parish of Camberwell. On the 25th of April, between eleven and twelve o'clock at noon, I left my house with my son who lives with me—I left the front door double locked, and the gate, the parlour door, and the door at the end of the passage, locked, but the outer door, which goes into the garden, was open, and ajar—there is a garden and a field in the rear of the house, and a road that runs parallel with our road and the cross-road leading to it—I received information, and returned to my house about five o'clock, I found the gate still locked, the street-door on the single lock, the back-parlour door open, and also the door leading to the kitchen, which I had left locked—on going up stairs, I found the drawer of the looking-glass, which I had locked, forced open—a desk in the parlour was broken open, and the things scattered about—I missed some coins from the desk, and from the looking-glass drawer a pair of bracelets, six rings, four brooches, and other articles, the whole worth between 5 l. and 6 l.—I lost a table and tea-spoon.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Are you a widow? A. Yes, I rent the house—I am certain my son locked the door—I was standing at the gate at the time, and asked him particularly if he had double locked it—the gate is two or three yards from the door, but I stood at the door—I
saw him turn the handle of the lock—I should have known it was done if I had not inquired—I have not the least doubt of it.
GEORGE MERRINGTON . I am a carpenter, and live in Denmark-street. The back of my house faces the front of the prosecutrix's—about noon, on the 25th of April, I saw the prisoner walking backwards and forwards some time before the prosecutrix's house—I saw him try the gate several times, and then he got over the gate, and opened the front door—I did not see any key, because he stood in front of the keyhole, but he appeared to be putting something to it—I did not see any thing in his hand—his hands were in front of him, as if he was opening it with a key—I saw him open the door—he then went in and shut it, I then went round to the front of the house—I found Goddard standing in the road—I told him to remain there while I went for a policeman—when I returned I found the prisoner in the hands of a policeman.
Cross-examined. Q. What was you doing at this time? A. Looking out of the window—I have been ill some time, and was not at work—my house is about 100 yards from the prosecutrix's—there is a meadow, and a garden between them—our garden is about 130 feet from the road—I could identify a person at that distance—I will not swear it is within a quarter of a mile—he was at the door about a minute.
MR. LILLY. Q. When you saw him in custody, were you certain he was the person you had seen in front of the house? A. Yes.
JOHN DONALD GODDARD . I am a druggist, and live at No. 68, Hatton-garden. About half-past one o'clock, on the 25th of April, I was in Denmark-road, Camberwell, and was spoken to at the front of the prosecutrix's house—from what was said to me I went round to the back of the house, and went to the side of the house to get to the back, along a sort of passage—when I got to the back I saw the prisoner in the garden, and asked him if be belonged to the house—he said he did—I told him somebody was in front, and he had better go and answer the door—he went in, and I went to the front again, supposing from his appearance he belonged to the house—when I got in the front I saw the back of the prisoner, walking about 100 yards off in the high road—I suppose he had got over the gardens, but could not see—he could not have come up the passage—I ran after him, and on my beginning to run he looked round, and ran too—I overtook him with considerable difficulty—I ran some distance—I brought him back opposite the house, and held him till a policeman came—he was taken to the station—I am able to swear to him from what I saw of him.
Cross-examined. Q. I think you saw him come into the garden. A. Yes—he did not appear to have any thing about him—I was at the back of the garden, in a field, about as far from him as I am from you—I did not see him go into the house—I shouted out—he was walking along leisurely when I first saw him—I am certain of him by his general appearance—I recollect his features.
THOMAS MATTHEWS . I am a policeman. On the 25th of April the prisoner was given into my custody by Goddard—I observed two silver spoons in his right-hand breeches pocket—I attempted to search him, and took them from his pocket, when he refused to be searched any further—I took him to the station—he was searched there, and I found on him two sovereigns, five brooches, one table-spoon, two tea-spoons, and one saltspoon, six gold rings, three seals, one pair of ear-rings, a gold and a silver pencil-case, a pearl-handled knife, two bracelets, an eye-glass, a cross, a
necklace, a scent-bottle, and other articles, with three picklock-keys—I did not try the keys to the door.
Cross-examined. Q. The spoons protruded from his pocket? A. Yes, I could see them.
MRS. NICHOLLS re-examined. The articles produced are all mine. GUILTY. Aged 19.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutrix, on account of his youth. — Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420509-1713]
1713. CATHERINE DONOVAN was indicted for feloniously assaulting Catherine Mahoney, on the 26th of April, and cutting and wounding her in and upon the left side of the head, with intent to do her some grievous bodily harm.
CATHERINE MAHONEY . I am single, and lodge at No. 6, Redcross-court, Borough—the prisoner's husband rents the house, and is my landlord. On the night of the 26th of April he applied to my mother for a week's rent—she could not pay it—he went down to his room, and I heard the prisoner using very low language, and calling the old woman hard names—I said my mother did not look more like the names she called her than her mother did, and then she called me the names myself—I had a bit of potato in my hand eating, and I flung it at her—then she took the chopper from the side of her kitchen fire-place in her kitchen (I must go through the kitchen to get to my room) she said, the first one that came down the stairs that night she would chop their b——head off—my mother came and locked the door—about half-an-hour after I went down for some water, and saw her with the chopper in her hand—I said, "I think you are going to do what you said"—she said, "Yes, I intend to do it"—I said, "I will see whether my head will be chopped off or not," and then she gave me a violent blow on the forehead—I flinched back from it, and received it on the top of my head, and did not receive the violent blow she was going to give me, or it would have been straight down my face—it came lighter on me through my falling back—I fell to avoid the blow—I called my mother down, and said, "I am murdered by the chopper"—she came to my assistance, and I was taken to the hospital.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You were quite yourself? A. Yes—I did not use any bad language to her, nor give her any provocation—my mother had some words with her, but I did not interfere—I said, "Mrs. Donovan, you called my mother an old b—-, but she is no more one than you are"—I did not say, "There you are sitting there, a w—to your own brother"—on my oath she did not say, if I called her a w—to her own brother again, she would chop my head—I did not say I would prove what I had said—that I swear—she had a child on her lap when I threw the hot potato—I do not know where it hit her—I will swear it did not hit her in the face—I threw it into her lap—I threw it as hard as I could—it did not hit the child—it came into her lap—I was four stairs off when I threw it—I do not know that she had her cap torn off, and her hair pulled about—I left her and my mother together—I swear the child was not knocked down before I went to the hospital—three years ago last August I had a few words with a neighbour, and was sent to prison because I could not pay 40 s.—I did not nearly knock a young woman's eye out with a brick-bat—I was sent to Kingston gaol for it, and was there twenty-one days—I was never in prison in my life before—I was never in Brixton
gaol in my life—they never said they wanted their room—we are out of the place now—we went last week.
ALLEN WILLIAMS . I am assistant to Charles Key, surgeon of Guy's Hospital. I saw the prosecutrix on the 26th—she had a wound on the left side of her head, it was not serious at all—she was quite sensible—it was about an inch and a half long, and a quarter of an inch deep, quite to the bone—the integuments of the skin were cut through to that extent—I have seen the chopper—a similar wound might be produced by just the sharp part of it.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the wound serious? A. No, a little dressing soon cured it—if very much force was used, this weapon would inflict a very serious wound.
MARY MOREEN . I am the wife of Timothy Moreen, and live in the house. I was there the night this happened, and heard Mrs. Donovan and Mahoney's mother having words—Catherine Mahoney came down stairs, and the prisoner asked her husband if he had been up—he said, "Yes, and she says she will pay me the rent to-morrow night"—the prosecutrix said the prisoner was a w—to her own brother, and she could prove it, she took the child in her arms, and took the chopper, and said, "If you call me that again, I will hit you with this"—the prosecutrix said, "I can prove it"—the husband got up to hit her—I took him by the hand, and said, "Have sense, man, and don't touch her"—she then threw the potato in the prisoner's face—she went up stairs, and came down afterwards, and said, "Now I will know whether I am to be hit with the chopper or no," and she hit her, and Mrs. Mahoney came down and took her by the hair of her head—the prisoner's husband was beating the three women, with the child under their feet—the prisoner struck two blows with the chopper—the first took effect in the face—she had a child in her arms—she did not strike her first on the arm—if it had not been for the expressions of the prosecutrix, it would not have happened—nobody could stand them—I believe she struck twice with the chopper—I know she struck once—I believe this is the chopper—it was left in the room—the policeman came for it, and got it.
Cross-examined. Q. You picked the child off the ground? A. Yes—I saw the prosecutrix strike the prisoner with the potato—after that old Mrs. Mahoney caught hold of the prisoner by the hair, and tore her cap—that was after the chopper was used.
ROBERT IRESON ( police-constable M 113.) I was called to the house between eight and nine o'clock—I noticed a few spots of blood on the wall and a spot or two outside the house—I took the prisoner's husband into custody—the prisoner followed, and came into the station and said, "It was not my husband who did it, it was me struck the party with the chopper"—I got the chopper afterwards from the house.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY of an Assault only. Aged 26.— Confined Four Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
