1715. JOHN SIMMONS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, a double-branch gas-fitting, value 2 l. the goods of Frederick West.
FREDERICK WEST . I am a baker, and live in Tooley-street, and have another shop in Suffolk-street. On the 1st of April, I left the shop in Suffolk-street locked up—I went there again on the 8th of April, found the door open, and missed the gas-fittings, a drinking-glass, a gas-branch, a brass-cock, and other articles—I afterwards saw the branch at Mr. Fisk's shop, in New Cut, and knew it again—I do not know the prisoner.
HENRY COE . I am a policeman. I have a double gas-branch, which I got at Fisk's shop on the 9th of April—I compared it with the prosecutor's shop where it had been fixed, and it corresponded with the screw-holes and every thing.
HENRY FISK . I am a cabinet-maker, and live at Nos. 15 and 16, New Cut, Lambeth. I bought this gas-branch of Davis, who keeps a shop in Friar-street.
MOSS DAVIS , being called, did not appear.
JOHN CROWTHER . At the latter end of last year I made this branch for Mr. West. NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18420509-1716]
1716. JOHN PEARL was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the warehouse of Robert Goulding, on the 12th of April, at St. John, Southwark, and stealing therein, 4 bushels of beans, value 16 s., his property.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution,
WILLIAM NETTLETON . I am clerk to Robert Goulding, a corn-merchant and warehouseman, carrying on business in Potter's Fields, St. John's, Southwark—I have lived with him thirty years—there is nobody but him in the firm—it is carried on as Robert Goulding and Son, but there is nobody but him in it. On the evening of the 12th of April, about seven o'clock, I was leaving the counting-house, and went round to the different doors to see that they were fast—on coming to the back-door of the warehouse, "B No. 1," I found the top part of the door open—I went up to the door and saw two men, each filling a bushel bag with beans—there was a balk of beans at the other end of the warehouse—I asked what they did there, and how they came there—one of them, not the prisoner, escaped almost immediately, so that I could not ascertain who he was—the prisoner came towards me and said it was all right—I said, "No, it is not all right," and asked what business they had there, and how they came in—he said it was all right, and endeavoured to make his escape—I was outside the door—the door was between us—I pushed him back and kept calling for the police—he attempted to come out at the top of the door—it is a divided door—the other man went to the other end of the warehouse, and I suppose must have dropped from the first-floor window—there was no other means of his getting away—before the policeman came the prisoner had made his escape into the other warehouse, and we went in search of him—I expect he knew the warehouse, and had been there before—the sack containing the beans was just outside the door where I saw him, ready to be carried away, besides the two sacks they were filling—the beans in the sack and bags corresponded in quality with those in the bulk—after securing the prisoner we went over the warehouse, and the following morning, about nine o'clock, I examined the back window and found one iron bar of the window standing in its proper position, but it was loose—anybody without could remove it to get in—the back-door of the warehouse was fastened from within—you could
not get in at the back of the premises except by the window—the front part of the warehouse was fastened with a common lock, and the Queen's lock also—they were quite undisturbed—the back-window is about two yards from the ground—any body could get through the window and unfasten the back-door within—a man could not get out of the up stairs warehouse without dropping from the window, unless he had a ladder, and that was found—he could not get out at the lower window, because I stood there.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. You say there is nobody in the concern but Robert Goulding? A. Yes—the beans belong to Mr. Millis Coventry, a corn-factor—the warehouse is a place for the safe keeping of goods—the back-door is divided in the centre—I found the upper part open—it is, perhaps, three yards high from top to bottom—there is no thoroughfare at the back of the premises—it is an entrance to our yard—I made no examination of the upper window that evening particularly—I do not know what had occurred to it since.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were the beans entrusted to your master for safe keeping? A. Yes—he is accountable for them—Mr. Coventry pays ware house room to my master for them.
JOHN FERNS . I am in the service of Mr. Goulding—I was on the premises, in the warehouse B No. 1—on the 12th of April, about four o'clock in the afternoon, I closed the warehouse—the front-door was secured by a lock and latch, and the Queen's lock too—I saw the backdoor secured inside—there was no fastening to the front—I came out at the front-door, and saw both the locks made fast—I did not examine the window bars.
WILLIAM SMITH . I am a policeman. I received information from Nettleton, went up to the second-floor, and saw the prisoner on the tiles of the lower warehouse endeavouring to escape—I took possession of the sack of beans and two bags—I went down stairs, round to the back premises, and met my brother constable, who got a ladder to get on to the roof, but received information that the prisoner had dropped from the wall again into an enclosed building—we went there, and found him lying on the bulk of corn—I examined the building that night—the bars of the back window seemed correct, but I did not take hold of them to try them—I tried them at nine o'clock the following morning, and found a bar unfastened—the nails bad been drawn,-and the bar removed, and put back in its place again—I bad not observed that the night before—I received the prisoner in charge—I had seen him in Tooley-street shortly before.
THOMAS TATE . I am a policeman. I went round the warehouse B No. 1, and found my brother constable there—I saw the back-window that night, but did not notice any thing the matter with it—I did not examine it particularly—I saw the prisoner endeavouring to escape, and afterwards secured him.
WILLIAMNETTLETON re-examined. Mr. Goulding keeps his own goods at the warehouse for sale as well as other people's—the beans were not in bond, but there was wheat in the same warehouse—they were in bond when first put there—Mr. Coventry might bring people there to look at them, but they generally sell them by sample at the market.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Could you have got them out without the party who puts the Queen's lock on coming? A. I could not have taken off the Queen's lock—it is taken off at eight o'clock in the morning, and put on at four o'clock in the afternoon.
GUILTY . Aged 29.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18420509-1717]
1717. LUCY COLLARD was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of March, 1 shirt, value 10 s.; 1 sheet, value 9 s.; and 2 handkerchiefs, value 5 s.; the goods of William Hayden, her master.
WILLIAM HAYDEN . I am a haberdasher—the prisoner was my servant of all-work, and had 8 l. a year. On Sunday, the 24th of April I missed some things, which made me look to see if any thing else was gone, and I missed a sheet—I told her I would not be satisfied till I had got the sheet—in consequence of what she said to me I found the shirt, sheet, and two handkerchiefs at Mr. Turner's, a pawnbroker, in Walworth-road—I went there in consequence of seeing the duplicate in her hand—she said she had pawned the sheet—she was not willing for me to get it out, she wished to get it out herself—I said, "You have no money to get it out"—she said she would pawn some of her own things to get it.
Q. How came you to know she had the duplicate? A. We said nothing about the sheet till Monday morning—I then pressed her to tell me where it was—she said she knew nothing about it—I said nobody had access to it but herself—then she said she had pawned it, and produced the duplicate—she said there was only the sheet and her own shawl—I went to Turner's, and found the articles—I had seen the shirt safe about a fortbefore—the handkerchiefs were in the back-warehouse—I knew the papers they were in were there, but I did not look into them—the policeman found a pair of gloves, which I kept in the same chest of drawers up stairs with the shirt—the drawer was found broken open on the Sunday night—I had seen it safe that afternoon—I always kept it locked—I do not think I had opened it that day—I gave her into custody on the 25th of April.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long had she been in your service? A. Since the 21st of February—she came as a single woman, I have since found she is married—she told me after a long time that she had pawned the sheet and produced the duplicate, and said she had pawned a shawl of her own at the same time, but that did not prove true—I am a widower—my family consists of a servant and four children—the prisoner did not say she had pledged the things for the purpose of sending money down to her son at Bristol—I let her have a sovereign on the 15th of March, and afterwards 5 s. on the 20th, to send her son—I saw Turner's name on the duplicate—I think I can swear to the handkerchiefs when I see them—the prisoner said she had brought them from Bristol with her after we found them—she did not deny the shirt and sheet being mine.
CHRISTOPHER THOMAS GREAVES . I am apprentice to Turner, a pawnbroker. I have a shirt, sheet, and two silk handkerchiefs—I wrote the duplicate produced for it on the 5th of March, for 8 s.—they were pledged by the woman in the name of Ann Hemmings—I cannot speak to the prisoner.
MR. HAYDEN re-examined. This shirt, sheet, and handkerchiefs I can swear to—the shirt has my name on it—I know the handkerchiefs, in consequence of their being in a damp place they got spotted, and I altered the price of them all, and put a fresh mark on them—the mark is not here—I produce one of the same quality with the same sort of spots on it—I also know the gloves.
Cross-examined. Q. Except the spots there is nothing uncommon about them? A. They are the same kind of handkerchief—after we missed the sheet she told my daughter she had pawned it—she did not say my daughter had authorised her to do so—I had said before that that I would find out where it was.
JOHN LLOYD . I am a policeman. I was called to the house, went up stairs for the prisoner, and told her the prosecutor suspected she had robbed him—she produced the duplicate—I asked if she objected to my searching her boxes—she said, "No," and gave me two keys—I found nothing in her boxes—I took her to the station—she was searched there, and this pair of gloves found on her—she said she picked them up in the room.
Cross-examined. Q. She told you, did not she, that she pawned the things intending to get them again? A. Yes.
GUILTY. Aged 38.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Nine Months.
Before Mr. Recorder,
[trialAccount t18420509-1718]
1718. WILLIAM BROMELL and JOHN PETTY were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 jacket, value 15 s.; 1 cap, valued.; the goods of John Edward Cox: 1 pair of trowsers, value 10 s.; 1 shirt, value 2 s.; and 1 pair of mittens, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Thomas Luxmore Wilson: and I jacket, value 15 s.; 1 handkerchief, value 5 s.; and 2 shirts, value 4 s.; the goods of Philip Lybbe Powys ; and ELIZABETH WARREN and ELLEN LOGDEN , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS FULLER . I am in the service of Mr. Searl, of Stangate-street, Lambeth. The members of the Oxford University Aquatic Club have a room on our premises—it is my duty to superintend the room which the gentlemen use to dress in, when they go out in the boats—they keep their clothing and things in lockers all round the room—on Monday, the 4th of April, at half-past nine o'clock, I locked the place up all safe—I returned on Tuesday morning about ten minutes to six, and found the gate open, also a small counting-house, where other gentlemen dress, and saw some of the clothes lying about, some of which belonged to the Club, and had been brought down to that room—I went up to the Oxford room, and found the door wide open—it had been opened with the key which I always leave there, in case the gentlemen should come while I am away—it is twelve o'clock sometimes before they come home—on the morning of the 4th I missed several articles belonging to the gentlemen—I saw some things afterwards produced by the policeman.
CHARLOTTE AUSTIN . I am the wife of Charles Austin, of Whiting-street, Lambeth. I know the prisoner Warren—she came to lodge with me on the 3rd of March, and left on the 5th of April—on the morning of the 5th of April, between ten and eleven o'clock, I went into her room to insist on her leaving it, as she was so noisy, and had so many followers, and I said I would not leave the room till she was gone—she left the room, and wished me to wait a few minutes till she returned—the prisoner Logden was in the room at the time—she did not lodge with her to my knowledge—Warren left the room to return in ten minutes, as she said, if I would wait, and Logden was to wait till she returned—she did not say what she was to return for, but said she would come back in ten minutes—finding she did not come back, I suspected something! and turned down the bed to see if the bed clothes were all right, and found in the bed twelve gentlemen's coats or jackets, one pair of trowsers, and eleven gentlemen's rowing shirts, tied up in a bundle—Logden was there—there was also a blue silk handkerchief, and a gentleman's cap, and in the drawer I found two drinking horns—there was a pair, of mittens also
—I called up one of my lodgers, Maria Parker, and in consequence of what passed between us, I sent her for a policeman, who came, and I delivered the articles to him—Logden wished me to take some of the things in the room as security for the rent, and let Warren stop till Thursday, till her week was up—I said no, I should take ill the things—the bedstead folds up, and the things were turned up in the bed—I brought them into my own room, and sent for a policeman—Warren did not return—I saw no more of her till she was at Union-hall—Logden did not lodge with me—I do not know how long she had been in the room—nobody could see the things without turning the bed down—she might not know they were there.
MARIA PARKER . I am single, and live at Austin's house. On the 5th of April, between three and four o'clock in the morning, before daybreak, I heard a knocking at the street-door—I knew Warren had got a key—Logden was stopping with her, I know, because I have seen her there—she was at home that night, because I heard her there—I did not see her—I could be positive it was her—I heard somebody in the room, and I bad' seen her on the stairs with Warren frequently—I cannot say she ever slept there—I saw her come down stairs next morning after ten, and spoke to her—after I heard the knocking at the door, I heard somebody get over the wall of the yard, and then heard somebody open the street-door within, and let somebody in—I could hear the footsteps of more than one person come in—I heard them go up stairs—I lodge in the parlour—Warren had the front room, over the parlour—I heard them walking about over my head—shortly after ten in the morning I saw the two male prisoners there—they came down stairs by themselves, and went out—I had never seen them before—the females were not with them—the shortest prisoner said, "Good morning"—I said, "Good morning"—they each bad a bundle, or ft coat, or jacket over their arm—my landlady called me up stairs about twenty minutes after that—I went up, and Logden was there—Mrs. Austin went to turn down the bed to see if the bed-clothes were right, and found these things there—I saw them found—next day we found a bag containing some skeleton-keys, in a drawer, which the constable has—there was all sorts of keys.
COURT. Q. You saw the men at ten o'clock, what time is the house open in the raoriing? A. Generally about seven—many persons might have come in before ten—I did not see who came in at four in the morning—I did not hear any voices—I heard them walking about—the male prisoners could not have come from any room but Warren's, as the other. room was locked up—there was a lodger in the room adjoining Warren's, hot she was in my room at the time, and her door was locked—I do not know whether the people went away that came at four—Warren has visitors, and is, I believe, in an unhappy course of life—I was never disturbed before—that is the reason which drew my attention to it—the wall the person got over joins the door at the entrance of the house, and goes to the back part of the house—it was two persons came in at least by the foot-steps—I only heard one person go out after four the morning, and before ten, that was a man who occupies the backroom—he is not here—he went out about nine.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You do not mean to say you kept awake all night? A. No—I did not go to sleep till my clock struck four, and I awoke again about six—I am certain nobody went out
from six to nine—the male lodger goes out to work at nine o'clock in the morning—I believe he is a quill-maker—my name is Maria Parker—that is the name I go by—I get my living many ways—I told Mrs. Austin what I had heard, which made her order them to leave.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You cannot tell how many people might have access to the room? A. They have a great many acquaintances I believe—the street-door is left for anybody to come in and out who has got a key—it is a street-doorkey, and not a latch-key—I have opened the door myself at times to persons who come—I slept till six o'clock—I generally get up at seven—I get out and open the shutters sometimes before I get up.
CHARLES STEWART . I am a policeman. On the 5th of April I went to No. 9, Whiting-street, between ten and eleven o'clock in the morning, and received some articles from Mrs. Austin, which I produce—I inquired if there was anybody in the room—I went up, and found Petty and Logden in the same room as the bedstead was—it was about ten o'clock in the morning—whether it was the front or back-room I cannot say, as it is a curiously-constructed house—Mrs. Parker and Mrs. Austin stood in the passage—when I went into the room I told the prisoners they must go with me to the station—they said nothing—I searched them, and found nothing—I searched the room again next day, and found several articles belonging to the club.
MRS. AUSTIN re-examined. These are what I found in the bed—I took them into my room, and the policeman took them out of my room—he took Petty and Logden in the room Warren lodged in—I removed the articles into the room I occupied myself at the back of the house built over the yard—I gave them to him in my room, not in Warren's.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF . I am a policeman. I took Warren into custody on the 19th of April, in High-street, Borough—I told her it was for receiving twelve jackets and other articles, stolen from Mr. Searle's, and found in her room—she said, "I know nothing about it; if they are found there, somebody must have put them there."
RICHARD SIMMONS . I am a painter, and know the prisoner Bromell; he is a waterman, and has been in the habit of going out with the Oxford Club at times, when they were short-handed. On the 4th of April, about four o'clock in the afternoon, I saw him and Petty pass the front of Mr. Searle's premises—they only passed as I went out, walking together—I cannot say they were in company, but walking by the side of each other.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is Mr. Searle's house in the open street? A. No—there is a thoroughfare where they passed, it is Bishop's-walk—anybody might pass there—they walked along as anybody else might—there was nothing remarkable—it is a very usual place for watermen to pass.
JAMES BROOK . I am a policeman. I took Bromell into custody, on the 5th of April, in the Waterloo-road—I told him I wanted him for a robbery at Mr. Lankester's—I searched him, and found this cap on his head—I produce a bunch of skeleton keys, which I received from sergeant Jones at the station—he is not here.
MRS. AUSTIN re-examined. These are the keys I gave to the sergeant—there are sixteen and a small crow-bar—I am certain they are the same, I particularly noticed them.
JOHN EDWARD COX . I am a member of the Oxford University Aquatic Club, and live at Hyde Park-gate; I am a member of the University. I know this to be my cap, and this jacket is mine, and a comb in the pocket—I lost a comb exactly like it—I had left it in my locker—I went to Searle's room on the Wednesday, and saw a great many of the lockers broken open—the lids were torn up—I did not notice them particularly—I think there were marks of some instrument used to prize them open.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How do you know the cap? A. By the general wear of it—I have no initials on it, but I once cut the strap shorter on one side than the other, that I might fasten it under my chin—it is not a very common kind of cap—I think mine and my brother's are the only ones in the club—I wore it on the 4th of April—I will not swear to the cut—I have no doubt of the cap, I think I should know it among a thousand that had been worn.
THOMAS LUXMORE WILSON . I am a solicitor, and live in Bedfordrow. I am not a member of the club—I keep things at Mr. Searle's, in a room under the Oxford room—these trowsers are mine, and this jacket I have worn five years—it has my name in it, written by myself, and I know the mittens and blue striped Guernsey frock.
PHILIP LYBBE POWIS . I am a member of Baliol College, Oxford, and live at Furnival's Inn; I am a member of the club. These two Guernsey frocks are mine, and this jacket I know by the particular sort of lining in the sleeves—I lost a blue handkerchief like this, but cannot speak positively to it—I believe it is mine—one of the frocks has my name in my own handwriting.
CHARLES STEWART re-examined. I took Petty for being in the room where the things were found—it was on the 5th of April, between ten and eleven o'clock—I found other articles in the room—this drinking-horn, another cap, and a silk handkerchief—this comb was found in a drawer in the room.
MRS. AUSTIN re-examined. I first saw Petty in the room when I took the policeman up to show him where I had removed the property to—I do not know how he came there—we both went up to Warren's room, and found Petty with Logden—I had not seen him there before—I saw two men go out at the street-door about an hour before, but cannot say he was one of them—Parker had given me information, which made me notice the noise there had been in the house—that was before I saw Petty in the room.
MARIA PARKER re-examined. After the two male prisoners went out with things, Petty and another man, not Bromell, came back—I saw them as I stood at my room door—the other man ran out—I had never seen him before—he ran away from the policeman—Petty and Bromell went out about ten o'clock—it must have been past ten—I saw Petty afterwards when the policeman took him—Bromell did not come back with him—he was in custody at the time—I had not seen Petty return, as I had gone to fetch a policeman, but he must have returned.
CHARLES STEWART re-examined. I saw Petty and another man, and Logden, in the front-room—I forgot to state that the other man was there—he escaped—he was not Bromell.
(Thomas Goodman, a stamper at Somerset House; Robert Smith, of Messrs. Barclay's bank; Robert Lewis, lighterman, Vauxball; J. Butler,
lighter man; and—Vincent, tailor, Union-street; gave Bromell a good character.
) BROMELL— GUILTY . Aged 20.
PETTY— GUILTY . Aged 20.
WARREN— GUILTY . Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
LOGDEN— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420509-1719]
1719. WILLIAM BROMELL was again indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Samuel Lankester, about the hour of three in the night, on the 30th of March, at Lambeth, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 medal, value 7 s.; 1 knife, value 1 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 4 s.; 1/2 lb. weight of cigars, value 5 s.; 4lbs. weight of ham, value 3 s.; 1/4 lb. weight of tobacco, value 1 s.; 1 1/2 pint brandy, value, 6 s.; 1 shilling, 1 sixpence, 1 twopenny-piece, 100 pence, 100 halfpence, and 10 farthings; his property.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the prosecution.
SAMUEL LANKESTER . I keep the Red Lion, in Fore-street, in the parish of Lambeth—I occupy the whole house. On the 30th of March I was the last person up, on my premises—I retired at 12 o'clock, after fastening every thing except one shutter at the back of the house, which was not fast—the window was shut—the shutter was up, and a bolt went through the window-chill to fasten it, but we could not get into the room, having lost the key—the bar-door was locked—I came down about seven in the morning—my premises were undergoing repairs, which Brown was superintending—I found the parlour shutter taken down, the window open, and the bar-door unlocked, which was locked when I went to bed—I found several boxes in the bar had been opened—one was a club box—they bad locks to them—I missed the money and articles stated in the indictment—this silver medal I can positively speak to from its general appearance, and the letter "P" on it—it was taken from the club-box—I have no marks on this twopenny-piece and the handkerchief—I lost a cotton handkerchief of this pattern.
Cross-examined by MR. RYLAND. Q. Had you any mark on the medal? A. No—it had been in my possession about eighteen months—I speak to it with certainty—I never saw one like it—it was given into my possession when I took the house—it belongs to the club, which is still in existence—I am answerable for it to the club—I have no doubt of its being the same—I saw it safe the Monday week before, under lock and key.
JAMES LOCKYER . I am in the service of Mr. Dempster, a pawnbroker, in the Blackfriars-road. On Thursday, the 31st of March, about six o'clock in the evening, the prisoner sold me this medal—I asked him several questions—he said he had had it six months, had pawned it several times, and was tired of pawning it—I told him I thought it belonged to the members of the Vintners' Company, and they could give him more than I could, I could give him no more than the price of old silver—he said he had had it by him six months, and would sell it—I gave him 3 s. for it—he seemed surprised, and said, "Does it weigh more than half a crown?"—I said, "Yes"—I have no doubt of him, his face was dirty, and he was dressed as a waterman—I gave a description of him to an officer, the man who was sent round with the Hue and Cry, describing the property stolen.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you had no doubt of him? A. From the first time I saw him—I never said I had a doubt of him—I never saw him till he sold the medal—I saw him again in rather more than a week—I did not say I believed it was him—I said I had not the least doubt—I told Mr. Trail so—we do not buy such things every day—I noticed the Vintners' arms upon it.
JEREMIAH BROWN . I am a plasterer, and live in Mill-street, Lambethwalk. I was employed whitewashing and repairing Mr. Lankester's premises—on the 31st of March I arrived there precisely at six o'clock—the clock was just about striking—I pulled my watch out—that is my time for going to work—it struck six a minute or two after I got there—I dare say my watch was correct—it is what I always go by—I was at work at the house next door—not at this house—I had then to go up the court belonging to Mr. Lankester, and found the back gate open—that was exactly at ten minutes past six—I went to the water-closet, and on coming out saw Mr. Lankester's back-parlour shutters down, and also his back-door open—I did not suspect anything—I thought Mr. Lankester was up and about, as he had had soldiers billetted there two days before—the window was open—I saw nobody go in or out.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not hear the clock strike at all, did yon? A. No—I go by my watch—I looked at it—it might want a minute or two to six—I was there just before six—it was not after six—I could not say it was precisely six, or two minutes before—I regulate my watch by my landlord's clock, sometimes, not by any other clock—it was not till ten minutes after six I found the gate open—I did not give any alarm then.
MARIA PARKER . I am single, and live at No. 9, Whiting-street, Lambeth. I saw the twopenny-piece in the room belonging to Warren, on the morning of the 5th of April—this is it—it was found in her apron-pocket, in her room—a bag was there found containing sixteen keys.
JOSEPH BROOK . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody on the morning of the 5th of April, within fifty yards of No. 9, Whiting-street—I found this handkerchief in his pocket—I went to No. 9, Whiting-street, and found the twopenny-piece produced.
GUILTY of Larceny. Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years more.
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18420509-1720]
1720. SARAH DOWDELL was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously assaulting Charles Dowdell, on the 27th of March, and casting and throwing loz. weight of a corrosive fluid called nitric acid, upon and over his face, and into his eyes, with intent to burn him, and whereby he was burnt.—2 other COUNTS, varying the manner of laying the charge.
CHARLES DOWDELL . I am a butcher, the prisoner is my wife. I have been separated from her rather better than three months ago, allowing her 10 s. a week maintenance—I do not know where she has resided since that—I live at Exeter-place, Great Dover-road—we have been married rather better than two years, and have one child about eighteen months old—she came to me about a month after we had been separated, and wished to come back—I refused to take her back on account of her temper being so violent—she came again after that—she said nothing then about coming back, but was constantly annoying me in my business—on the 26th of March she was to receive her allowance—she generally had it on Saturday—she came, but did not receive her money—I usually sent it by my lad
—on Sunday, the 27th, about ten o'clock at night, she came and knocked at the side shutters—I was then getting my supper—I went to the front door and saw ray wife—I am sure it was her—she asked me for the money which she did not receive on the Saturday night—I gave it to her, and as she parted away from the door, she said, "I would have you be careful, or I will have my revenge yet"—I made no reply—she went away—I went in and got my supper, after which, I changed my coat, as it was raining, and went to the Bull to get half a pint of ale—I think it must have been half-past ten or a quarter to eleven—I met her about twenty yards from my door—on seeing me, she turned, and walked in an opposite direction, crossed the road, and went on the opposite side of the way towards Eaton-place, which is opposite to the Bull—she was walking backwards and forwards there—I went into the Bull, at the private entrance, the passage being rather dark, I thought she would not see me enter that door—I got half a pint of ale there—I remained in the Bull, I think not more than two minutes—as I came out she met me at the door and asked me if I was going to stand any gin—I made no reply, and while I was going away, homewards, I saw her draw her hand from under her cloak, she threw a liquid in my face, and exclaimed, "That is what I have long wished for, and now I have got my revenge"—the liquid gave me great pain, almost as if I had been scalded with hot water, a burning pain, it went into both eyes—I was obliged to shut my eyes instantly, since that I have lost the sight of one, and partly of the other—one is getting better—I was taken to Guy's Hospital—I did not see what the liquid was in.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you run from your promise in not paying me my money on Friday precisely? A. When I went to Union-hall it was on a Friday, and I paid her the 10 s. in advance, and 1 s. 6 d. to pay her cab-hire, and it was understood I was to pay every Friday.
Prisoner. He did not fulfil his promise; I went to Union-hall, the Magistrate told me to take witnesses with me and ask him civilly for the money; he said he had got no money; I said, "What am I to do, and my poor baby is ill;" he said, "You may work for it as I do;" I said I would work, for the baby was not too bad to be left; he said, "I will send it;" and I walked away directly: I went and asked him civilly for it on Saturday night; I said, "Would you not like to see the poor child;" he said, "I neither want to see you or it;" he said he would send it, but he never did.
PETER MONTAGUE POPE . I am a surgeon of Guy's Hospital—the prosecutor was brought there—his face was very much inflamed, and both eyes injured—it was produced by some acid—if a liquid thrown in his face produced it, I should say it was some corrosive acid—I cannot say what it was, probably nitric acid—I am certain it was some corrosive fluid—he has entirely lost the sight of one eye—the other is likely to recover, but he will never have the perfect use of it—it will be partially restored.
RICHARD WOOD . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner directly opposite the Bull—I told her she must go with me—she expressed her readiness to go—she said she had been in hell the last two years, and I could not take her to a worse hell, and as we went along she said she had plenty of cause to do what she had done.
Prisoner's Defence. We were married two years ago—when we came home he said, "I consider I have married you for a servant and not for a
wife, for I have another woman I like better, I shall always stick to her;" we went out and he actually came home with another woman; and the more I put up with it, the more he kicked and ill-used me—it was natural for me not to like this, for I did not think I was marrying a s—.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Three Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420509-1721]
1721. HENRY DAKEN was indicted for stealing, in the dwelling. house of George Gurney, on the 10th of May, at St. Mary, Newington, 30 shillings, 16 sixpences, 6 groats, 60 pence, 120 halfpence, and 750 farthings, his monies; and afterwards, about the hour of one in the night, feloniously and burglariously breaking out of the said dwelling-house.
GEORGE GURNEY . I keep the Red Lion, at Wal worth-road, in the parish of St. Mary, Newington. I have a drawer in my bar with a patent lock to it—I keep the silver in it, which is dropped into it in the course of the day through a space, out of the other tills, every two hours, and at night I empty it, and leave some silver on the sideboard for change—on Tuesday morning, about a quarter after two o'clock, I observed a shilling with a mark on it in the basket and left it there—I left my house that day and did not return till the following evening, Wednesday, when I had information about a quarter past seven—the basket would not be emptied in consequence of my absence, not until the last thing at night—the prisoner was not employed in my house at all.
THOMAS GARNER . I am barman at the Red Lion. I was at the bar on Tuesday night about twelve o'clock, and saw Mrs. Gurney clear the basket which stands in the drawer—she left out 2 l. in silver for change for the following day, and put it on the sideboard loose out of the basket about ten minutes before twelve—we close the house up at twelve o'clock—I was the last that left the bar—the money was safe then—I bolted the side shop door—about five next morning I got up and found the side shop-door unbolted and half open—it opens into the street—I missed from the sideboard the 2 l. in silver—a wine decanter was removed from the shelf and put on the counter—persons could come into the yard of our house until about eleven o'clock at night—we have a coal cellar—I did not go into it over night, but the door was closed—I cannot say whether it was locked—it is in the house, in the passage leading to the yard—except the coal cellar I had examined the house, and if anybody was concealed in the house they must have been in the coal cellar—the side shop-door must have been opened from within—there were two casks placed so as not to let the door open wide—I had seen the prisoner several times during the Tuesday evening backwards and forwards in the passage leading to the yard.
Prisoner. Q. When was the last time you saw me? A. About eleven o'clock—I saw you in the tap-room about ten minutes or a quarter after eleven.
Prisoner. I was in the yard between nine and ten o'clock but not afterwards.
DAVID EVEREST . I am a sergeant of the V division of police. On Wednesday evening, about nine o'clock I apprehended the prisoner in a beershop in Hill-street, Newington—I told him I had come to take him on suspicion that he was the person who had robbed Mr. Gurney's house—he said he knew nothing about Mr. Gurney's house—he said he had no money—I searched him and found 30 s. and 10 d., and among it a marked shilling which I have.
Prisoner. I said I had got no money because I did not know what you came about; I did not know who you was; you did not say you had come to take me or that you was a constable. Witness. He must have known me—I was in plain clothes—I told him I apprehended him on suspicion of being concerned, or having robbed Mr. Gurney's house—Mr. Gurney was with me.
GEORGE GURNEY re-examined. When I left the money in the basket in the drawer there was a shilling with a mark on the head—I am certain this is it, in consequence of a small T, and another T being on it, and a stamp underneath the T—I am certain I noticed these three marks on it—I took particular notice of the silver to see if any was bad, and am positive this is one of the shillings—the silver would not be disturbed till night, and then left in the bar as change for the morning.
Prisoner's Defence. The money I came by honestly; I do not see why this shilling should not be changed at the house if any body came in for change; I was in Mr. Gurney's house on Tuesday night near upon eleven o'clock; I came home about five minutes after eleven; my mother had left the door ajar; I went to bed, got up between seven and eight, came up to the Lion, stopped there most all day, and had all this money from Monday morning in my pocket.
GEORGE GURNEY re-examined. We always keep a bag of loose silver besides—my son counts the money up—we count out 40 s. for change, the rest is put into a drawer with a patent lock and locked up—when the silver was gone for change, I should resort to that which was left in the drawer—each day's change is put out for change on the following day, and if there is not sufficient we resort to the bag—this shilling might have been paid away if 1 l. worth of silver was wanted—it might be given in exchange for gold, but nothing else.
THOMAS GARNER re-examined. I had not given any change in the morning, as all that was put there for change was gone.
DAVID EVEREST re-examined. I apprehended the prisoner at nine o'clock on Wednesday night.
THOMAS GARNER re-examined. I had not examined the forty shillings to see the marked one—we have a bag of loose silver besides for change—the rest of the silver in the basket is left in the drawer locked up—it is counted up in pounds in the morning, and put out for change for general use—it gets into circulation in the course of the day—I saw the prisoner in the house on Wednesday, but he never changed any money.
Prisoner. I had not worked for a master for nine months, but my mother keeps a mangle; she keeps me, and gives me about 4 s. a week for clothes, besides having porter's jobs; I saved the thirty shillings up.
---- DAKEN. I am the prisoner's mother—I cannot say where my son was on Tuesday night—I went to bed very tired, and ordered the door to be left ajar—I do not know how he could have thirty shillings, but he earned many a shilling of me—I have said, "You come home to-day and I will give you 2 d. or 3 d.," and he saved this money up to buy clothes, I firmly believe.
NOT GUILTY .
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, JUNE 13 TH, 1842.
